# What the published performance figures were measured against.
#
# The README publishes numbers that were measured, not derived: how long a row
# product takes per element, how long twelve tokens take, how the matrix
# product scales. They cannot be checked the way the conformance figures can,
# because they differ by half a per cent between runs and by much more between
# machines, so no assertion about their value can hold anywhere but here.
#
# What can be checked is whether they still describe this code. Each group
# below names the sources its numbers depend on and a fingerprint of those
# sources as they stood when the numbers were taken. `tests check` recomputes
# the fingerprint and fails when it differs, which means: these sources have
# changed since the figures were published, so either re-measure and record
# what you get, or say why the change cannot have moved them.
#
# That is a duty, and the point of putting it here is that it is a duty the
# release checklist enforces rather than one somebody has to remember. Every
# group below has gone stale once already, by two to four times.
#
# Format, one group per line:
#   <name> <fingerprint> <source> [<source> ...]
#
# The measuring tool counts as a source. Every group whose figures come from
# `tests speed` lists tests/src/speed_run.adb, because a change there changes
# the figures as surely as a change to the engine does -- and did: the tool
# read the prompt file differently from the command and sampled differently
# from it, for as long as it had existed, and nothing here noticed because
# nothing here was watching the thing doing the measuring.
#
# The "# The host every figure below was taken on, which none of them said.
#
#   processor  AMD Ryzen 7 7840U with Radeon 780M Graphics, 16 hardware
#              threads
#   device     AMD Radeon 780M Graphics (RADV PHOENIX), integrated, one
#              queue in the family the engine computes on. A second device
#              is listed, llvmpipe, which is a software renderer and not
#              what any of these figures used.
#   system     Linux 6.17.0, GNAT from the toolchain this repository pins
#
# A figure without its host is a figure about nothing: 1.871 s for twelve
# tokens says something only beside the machine that took 1.871 s. The load
# each run carried has been recorded here for months and what carried it
# never was, which is half a condition written down twice.

# The entries before 2026-08-26 are in docs/measured-figures-earlier.txt.
# They were moved there when this file passed the megabyte a file in this
# repository may be, which is a rule the suite enforces and which is worth
# more than keeping one log in one place. The fingerprint groups all stay
# here: it is the prose that moved.
#
# It has happened twice now. The entries before 2026-09-07 went the same way
# on 2026-09-09, and for the same reason: this file grows by an entry every
# time something is measured, and an entry is worth more than the megabyte
# is. What stays here is every group line with the load and the model that
# were true when it was stamped, so a stale fingerprint still says what to
# re-measure and on what.
#
#   And llama.cpp has three tile shapes chosen by the matrix's size where
#   this program has one -- small {32 rows, 64 columns, 128 deep}, medium
#   {128, 128, 64}, large {128, 256, 64}. At a hundred and ten columns it
#   picks the medium, which is taller than ours rather than narrower, and
#   the taller tile is what this file already measured at thirteen per cent
#   worse on the long prompt.
#
#   What is left of the difference is the one dimension of the tile this
#   program has never varied: the depth. All three of their shapes take
#   sixty-four or a hundred and twenty-eight columns of the shared dimension
#   at a step where this takes thirty-two. Set to sixty-four the shader
#   compiles and the run does not complete -- the staging is written for a
#   thirty-two-deep chunk and a sixty-four-lane workgroup, so it is shader
#   work rather than a constant. That is the next thing to try here and it
#   is the only untried one left.
#
#   Nothing changed, so no figure moves and no group is restamped.
#
# load:  under 1.05 before every reading
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0
#
# 2026-09-04, stage three of the round: a device runs a round's attention as
# well as its products, and eight members at a long context double.
#
#   WHAT WAS BUILT. The device holds one cache buffer laid out as one
#   session's keys and values. It is dealt out in blocks of one session's
#   worth now, and row i of a round reads block i -- which is all the kernel
#   has to be told, since it multiplies a row's number by the block width.
#   The attention kernel's push block carries that width and a table of eight
#   last positions; it went from sixty-four bytes to a hundred and
#   twenty-eight, and the pipeline layout's declared push range with it. A
#   round takes the compilation of attention.comp whose block is one query,
#   because a block of more reads a cached key once and dots it into every
#   query of the block, and rows of a round do not share a cache.
#
#   THE PUSH BLOCK. This file records that widening that block by one field
#   once corrupted this device's answers and that nobody explained it. It was
#   widened here deliberately, and the declared range was raised with it --
#   the older note about pushing sixty bytes past a declared range of
#   thirty-two is the likeliest account of the corruption, and this change is
#   the test of that reading. Every digest in the suite is unmoved, the
#   conformance sweep is at zero outside tolerance, and the round's mark is
#   the same on both backends at every count.
#
#   ALTERNATED WITH THE ARRANGEMENT IT REPLACES, medians of three each way,
#   on the device, both binaries built from the same tree:
#
#     1419-token prompt, 32 rounds     host      device
#       2 members                      2.024 s   1.572 s   1.29x
#       4 members                      2.915     1.969     1.48x
#       8 members                      5.658     2.802     2.02x
#
#     7-token prompt, 64 rounds
#       2 members                      2.756 s   2.477 s   1.11x
#       4 members                      3.343     2.776     1.20x
#       8 members                      4.571     3.271     1.40x
#
#   Eight members at fourteen hundred positions read 91.4 tokens a second
#   against 45.2. THE REMOVAL PRICED THAT ROW AT FIFTY PER CENT AND TAKING
#   IT AWAY DOUBLED THE ROUND, which is the rarest kind of entry here: a
#   price named before the work and met by it. The two rows beside it were
#   priced at sixteen and thirty-four per cent and pay twenty-nine and
#   forty-eight, so the prediction was low where it did not matter.
#
#   NOTHING OUTSIDE A ROUND MOVED. The ordinary device prompt reads 0.098 s
#   either way in three alternated pairs, and every published digest is what
#   it was. The extra sixty-four bytes a dispatch pushes are below what any
#   figure here resolves.
#
#   AND A BUG THE BUILDING FOUND. Blocks are asked for furthest-first. A
#   wider cache buffer is a new one and an empty one, so asking for block
#   zero and then block seven turns every row seated so far out of its block;
#   the layer loop repaired it by re-uploading each member's whole cache
#   every round, which was right and read a third slower -- eight members at
#   the long context 4.078 s against 2.802. Asking for the most first means
#   the growing happens before anything is seated.
#
#   Two sessions on one device also used to write over each other's keys in
#   that buffer, which nothing did and nothing caught. Blocks are the answer
#   to that as well: a session takes one and keeps it, and one turned out of
#   its block loses a copy and nothing else, because what the host holds is
#   the copy of record.
#
#   THE ROUND TABLES RETAKEN, one sitting, 64 rounds from a seven-token
#   prompt:
#
#     members   processor            device
#           1    29.7 ms   33.7 t/s   19.9 ms   50.3 t/s
#           2    18.6      53.7       17.7      56.6
#           3    13.9      72.0       12.4      80.5
#           4     8.8     113.3       10.0     100.1
#           6     9.7     103.4        7.7     130.4
#           8     5.7     175.6        6.6     151.6
#
#   The device column read 5.0 ms at eight members in the sitting stage two
#   was published from and would not go below 6.4 in this one -- on the
#   binary that produced it as well as on the one that replaced it. That is
#   a sitting, not a change, and it is why the alternated pairs above are
#   the evidence and this table is only the shape. At fourteen hundred
#   positions, on the device: one member 22.2 ms and 45.1 t/s, two 24.6 and
#   40.7, four 15.4 and 65.0, eight 10.9 and 91.4. One member is not a round
#   -- it takes the fused whole-layer path -- and two members do not beat it
#   at that context.
#
# load:  under 0.60 before each alternated set
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0

# 2026-09-04, the sitting five groups were retaken in, after stage three.
# Medians of three, load gated, llama-bench -r 3, llama.cpp at 95b8e33e1.
#
#   The engine, twelve tokens from the six-token prompt: 0.377 s wall,
#   0.055 evaluating, 0.322 generating, 1.97 s of processor time, loading
#   0.074 s. At --arith f32: 1.184 s for 9.39 s. At fifteen threads: 0.397 s
#   for 2.49 s.
#
#   The reference backend, four tokens: cpu 0.052 s and 0.107 s; reference
#   5.958 and 3.801. Sixty-one times in total, a hundred and fifteen on the
#   prompt and thirty-six on the generation.
#
#   The device backend: six tokens and twelve generated, cpu 0.363 s wall,
#   0.052 evaluating, 0.311 generating, 1.91 s of processor time; device
#   0.250, 0.035, 0.214, 0.07. A 110-token prompt with nothing generated,
#   cpu 0.355 s for 2.69 s; device 0.099 for 0.02.
#
#   Drafting: the model at eight bits 0.377 s, at two bits 1.397, the first
#   drafted by the second 3.228 with 24 proposed and 7 accepted, 2.903 s of
#   it generating and the draft's own generation 1.119 s.
#
#   The llama.cpp comparison:
#     ours  110 tokens  processor 0.307 s  device 0.070 s
#           1419        processor 5.129    device 0.831
#           64 generated processor 1.746   device 1.218
#
#     llama-bench --device none  pp110 371.34 +/- 16.64  tg64 39.56
#                 -ngl 99        pp110 1538.30          tg64 56.18
#                 --device none  pp1419 261.40          tg64 38.93
#                 -ngl 99        pp1419 1760.44         tg64 55.91
#                 -ngl 0         pp110 695.20
#
#   Which gives, at 110 tokens: processor 358.3 t/s against 371.34, 1.04
#   behind; device 1571.4 against 1538.30, ahead by 1.02. At 1419: processor
#   276.7 against 261.40, ahead by 1.06; device 1707.6 against 1760.44, 1.03
#   behind. Generating: processor 36.7 against 39.56, 1.08; device 52.5
#   against 56.18, 1.07.
#
#   The device's short prompt is ahead of llama.cpp for the first time and
#   the processor's is behind for the fourth time in six sittings. Neither is
#   a change to the code -- the device row of both tables is what stage three
#   left alone -- and llama-bench's own spread on that row is between sixteen
#   and thirty-five tokens a second. The rows that repeat are the long prompt
#   and the two generating ones.
#
# load:  under 0.60 before the first of them; the machine had settled at
#     57 C and llama-bench ran on the same pass, so the short prompt was not
#     measured on a part that had gone back to sleep.
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0

# 2026-09-04, where a device round's time was going, and the block a session
# keeps. Two entries in one, because the second is what the first found.
#
#   THE QUESTION. A device round was behind a processor round at a long
#   context at every member count -- 91.3 tokens a second against 117.6 at
#   eight members, 65.5 against 88.0 at four -- with attention on the device
#   in both. A single sequence goes the other way, 52.5 against 36.7, so the
#   crossover was somewhere in the round and nothing said where.
#
#   THE PHASE CLOCK, which `tests speed --round N --budget` now reports as
#   the single-sequence measurement always has. Eight members, 32 rounds,
#   1419 positions, the round alone and not the prompt each member read:
#
#     processor              device
#       FEEDING     0.975 s    ATTENDING    1.302 s
#       ATTENDING   0.636      NORMALIZING  1.008
#       PROJECTING  0.323      PROJECTING   0.284
#       everything  0.204      everything   0.106
#
#   The device's FEEDING and JOINING are zero because the layer's whole
#   second half is one submission there, which ATTENDING covers. What is not
#   explained is NORMALIZING at thirty-seven per cent, where the processor's
#   reads 0.036 s of the same host code.
#
#   WHERE IT ACTUALLY WAS. Four probes, each a charge moved: the window is
#   not the normalization (0.032 s), not the layer boundary (0.000 s), not
#   the preamble (0.001 s), and not the attention's tail. It is the loop that
#   seats a round's rows, and IT DOES NOT GROW WITH THE ROUND COUNT: 0.976 s
#   at 32 rounds, 0.999 at 64, 0.976 at 128.
#
#   So it is a one-off, paid the first time a round forms, and it is every
#   member's whole cache written into the block its row number named -- a
#   hundred and thirty-eight megabytes a member, of which the conversion to
#   half precision is most of the cost. A member prefills alone, takes the
#   first block, and is then moved to the block its row wants.
#
#   Once seated, the device round was ahead all along: 55.7 ms a round
#   against the processor's 69.3 at eight members, which the 128-round
#   reading shows without any subtraction -- 8.273 s against 8.865.
#
#   WHAT WAS BUILT. A session takes a block the first time it writes to the
#   device's cache and keeps it until it closes. Rows read the blocks their
#   sessions were already in, so forming a round writes a table of two words
#   a row and nothing else. The table moved out of the push constants and
#   into the end of the cache, which is a buffer the kernel has bound
#   already: the push block held eight rows, and what bounds a round now is
#   memory -- sixteen blocks, two gigabytes for this model at two thousand
#   positions.
#
#   ALTERNATED, medians of three each way, device, 1419 positions, 32 rounds:
#
#     members   host attention   pushed table   blocks
#           2       2.024 s        1.587 s      1.292 s
#           4       2.915          1.965        1.477
#           8       5.658          2.777        1.783
#          16       --             8.115        3.444
#
#   Eight members read 143.6 tokens a second against 45.2 with attention on
#   the host. Sixteen read 148.7 against the pushed table's 62.8, which is
#   the cap: a round of more than eight attended on the host and was slower
#   than the same device at eight.
#
#   THE ROUND TABLES, one sitting, medians of three, milliseconds a token:
#
#     members   1419 positions        71 positions
#               processor  device    processor  device
#           1     32.0       22.1      29.9       20.6
#           2     21.4       20.0      18.3       18.5
#           4     11.3       11.3       8.9        9.9
#           8      8.6        7.0       5.8        5.2
#          16      7.1        6.5       4.7        4.6
#
#   TWO REPAIRS the building needed. A wider cache buffer came up empty and
#   turned every seated session out of its block; it carries its contents
#   over now, values and half-precision copy alike. And every place that
#   addresses the device's cache for a session adds that session's block --
#   the fused whole layer, the fused half layer, the single-call attention,
#   the readback -- which a cache with one tenant did not need. A session at
#   the sixteenth block also overflowed a Natural where the half-precision
#   copy's base is added; that addition is in sixty-four bits now.
#
#   The marks agree on both backends at every count from one to twenty-four,
#   above the block limit as well as below it: a round of seventeen attends
#   on the host and says what a round of sixteen says on the device.
#
# load:  under 0.60 before each alternated set
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0

# 2026-09-04, the sitting five groups were retaken in, after the blocks.
# Medians of three, load gated, llama-bench -r 3, llama.cpp at 95b8e33e1.
#
#   The engine, twelve tokens from the six-token prompt: 0.374 s wall,
#   0.051 evaluating, 0.321 generating, 1.97 s of processor time, loading
#   0.069 s. At --arith f32: 1.217 s for 9.64 s. At fifteen threads: 0.465 s
#   for 2.75 s.
#
#   The reference backend, four tokens: cpu 0.054 s and 0.107 s; reference
#   6.020 and 4.032. Sixty-two times in total, a hundred and eleven on the
#   prompt and thirty-eight on the generation.
#
#   The device backend: six tokens and twelve generated, cpu 0.376 s wall,
#   0.052 evaluating, 0.323 generating, 1.97 s of processor time; device
#   0.260, 0.033, 0.227, 0.07. A 110-token prompt with nothing generated,
#   cpu 0.356 s for 2.75 s; device 0.098 for 0.02.
#
#   Drafting: the model at eight bits 0.374 s, at two bits 1.508, the first
#   drafted by the second 3.380 with 24 proposed and 7 accepted, 3.028 s of
#   it generating and the draft's own generation 1.218 s.
#
#   The llama.cpp comparison:
#     ours  110 tokens  processor 0.336 s  device 0.070 s
#           1419        processor 5.104    device 0.829
#           64 generated processor 1.764   device 1.216
#
#     llama-bench --device none  pp110 340.35 +/- 24.41  tg64 39.21
#                 -ngl 99        pp110 1645.51          tg64 56.16
#                 --device none  pp1419 260.41          tg64 38.86
#                 -ngl 99        pp1419 1758.24         tg64 55.61
#                 -ngl 0         pp110 732.30
#
#   Which gives, at 110 tokens: processor 327.4 t/s against 340.35, 1.04
#   behind; device 1571.4 against 1645.51, 1.05 behind. At 1419: processor
#   278.0 against 260.41, ahead by 1.07; device 1711.7 against 1758.24, 1.03
#   behind. Generating: processor 36.3 against 39.21, 1.08; device 52.6
#   against 56.16, 1.07.
#
#   The device's short prompt read 1571.4 for the third sitting running and
#   changed sides anyway, because llama-bench read 1645.5 where it had read
#   1538.3. That row is the spread and not the code.
#
# load:  under 0.60 before the first of them; the machine had settled at
#     61 C and llama-bench ran on the same pass.
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0

# 2026-09-04, the scheduler over the round, and five fingerprints that moved
# for a procedure nothing published runs through.
#
#   WHAT WAS BUILT. Model_Runner.Serving: a queue of callers, a round taken
#   from the front of it, a sampler and a stop and a limit a member, and a
#   seat given back when one finishes. tests speed --serve N --callers M is
#   what exercises it, and speed_run.adb is where that lives -- which is why
#   every group listing the measuring tool went stale.
#
#   NO FIGURE MOVED AND NONE COULD. What changed in speed_run.adb is one new
#   procedure, Serve, reached only by a command-line option that did not
#   exist. Round, Measure and everything the published figures run through
#   are untouched, and the twelve-token, reference, device, drafting and
#   llama.cpp numbers were taken four hours before this on the same tree
#   minus the new procedure. Restamped with that as the reason rather than
#   re-measured, which is the choice this file's header offers and the one
#   an unreachable addition earns.
#
#   WHAT THE SERVER MEASURES, which is not a published figure and is here
#   because it is the argument for the next thing. Sixteen callers through
#   eight seats, each from the 110-token prompt, held to about thirty-two
#   tokens:
#
#     served/callers   processor   device
#           4 / 8       22 ms       15 ms
#           8 / 16      22          13
#          16 / 32      23          15
#
#   The processor's column is flat and the device's is not, and the reason
#   is the arriving: eight members steady on the device read 7.0 ms a token
#   and eight turning over read 13. A caller's prompt is read on its own, so
#   a server whose members turn over spends part of every round's worth of
#   time on prefill that no round divides. THAT IS WHAT MIXING PREFILL ROWS
#   INTO A ROUND WOULD CLOSE, and it is the largest thing left in this part
#   of the program.
#
#   With a seven-token prompt the two backends swap and the processor is
#   ahead at every count, because a round that short is per-call cost on the
#   device. A server is worth putting on a device when its callers have
#   something to say.
#
#   THE CHECK. Every token every member is handed is digested in the order
#   it was handed out. The digest repeats run to run on a backend. Between
#   backends it repeats for one member and diverges after twenty or thirty
#   tokens for two or more, which is the products' own drift at two rows
#   against one and not the serving: tests speed --round shows the same
#   divergence at the same lengths, and the conformance sweep's tolerance is
#   what bounds it.
#
# load:  under 1.30
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0

# 2026-09-04, what a caller's arrival costs, and a host that moved under a
# binary that did not.
#
#   THE MEASUREMENT THAT STARTED IT. The serving driver times the arriving
#   apart from the rounds now, which nothing had. Eight seats, thirty-two
#   callers, thirty-two tokens each:
#
#     prompt   backend    total     joining      a caller
#       7      processor  7.298 s   2.246 s      70.2 ms
#       7      device     7.435     2.817        88.0
#     110      processor 18.846    13.212       412.9
#     110      device     8.838     4.083       127.6
#
#   THIRTY-ONE TO SEVENTY PER CENT OF A SERVING RUN WAS THE ARRIVING, and a
#   round costs eight milliseconds. Two things were wrong.
#
#   ONE: A SEAT DID NOT KEEP ITS SESSION. Admitting a caller opened one and
#   retiring it closed one -- a whole context's keys and values allocated,
#   zeroed and freed, a hundred and thirty-eight megabytes here -- and the
#   seat's block of the device's cache went back with it for the next caller
#   to take and re-fill. The seat rewinds instead. Alternated, device, 110
#   tokens: 10.170 s against 8.880, and at a seven-token prompt 9.0 against
#   7.40.
#
#   TWO: A PROMPT WAS READ IN A PASS OF ITS OWN. It is rows of a round now.
#   Evaluate_Round takes a share list -- how many rows each member
#   contributes, one apiece being a decode round -- and a row is a member and
#   a position, so nothing in an evaluation cares which of the two kinds it
#   is. The map from row to member is built once a call and everything reads
#   it through the two functions that were already there.
#
#   BOTH TOGETHER, alternated with the commit before them, medians of three,
#   eight seats and thirty-two callers:
#
#     prompt   backend     before     after
#       7      processor   8.817 s    6.827 s   1.29x
#       7      device      9.559      7.502     1.27x
#     110      processor  19.971     16.444     1.21x
#     110      device     10.754      9.292     1.16x
#
#   Admitting a caller costs ten milliseconds, and what is left of it is a
#   copy of the prompt into the seat.
#
#   ONE STRETCH IS STILL READ ALONE. A device answers sixteen query
#   positions at once out of one cache and a round's rows do not share a
#   cache, so a round gives that kernel up. Measured: prompts of a hundred
#   and ten in rounds read 9.861 s where the same prompts read alone read
#   8.838. The rule is that kernel's own threshold -- sixteen positions --
#   and below it a round is free. Teaching the kernel to take one tile per
#   member is what would remove the exception.
#
#   A BUG THE ROWS FOUND. The fused half-layer held its queries and its
#   residual in a stack array sized by the rows of the call -- sixteen
#   kilobytes a row for this model. A decode round of eight is a hundred and
#   twenty-eight kilobytes and nobody noticed; a round carrying four hundred
#   rows of prompt is seven megabytes, and the stack said so. It is room the
#   backend keeps and grows now, which is one allocation for a run.
#
#   AND A CONTROL THAT FAILED, which is the part to keep. The five groups
#   were re-measured for this change and every processor figure came back
#   where it was -- twelve tokens 0.374 s against 0.374, the reference ratio
#   62 against 62, drafting 0.374/1.570/3.397 against 0.374/1.508/3.380 --
#   and so did the device's GENERATING rows: twelve tokens 0.264 s against
#   0.260, sixty-four generated 1.245 against 1.216.
#
#   The device's PROMPT rows did not. The 1419-token prompt read 1.141 s
#   against the published 0.829, and the 110-token one 0.087 against 0.070.
#   THE BINARY THAT PRODUCED THE PUBLISHED FIGURES THIS MORNING PRODUCES THE
#   NEW ONES THIS EVENING: 1.155 s from the same file, at a load of 0.27 and
#   a part at 58 C. Three binaries -- the morning's, the afternoon's and
#   this one -- read within one per cent of each other on the same
#   measurement. So the host moved and the program did not, and the device's
#   top clock state is where to look: a prompt is a long dispatch and a
#   generated token is not, which is exactly the pair that split.
#
#   The published device figures are left where they were and this is
#   recorded instead. Publishing an evening's device prompt rows over a
#   morning's, when the morning's binary disagrees with itself by forty per
#   cent between the two, would be publishing an afternoon.
#
# load:  under 0.60 before each alternated set; part at 58 C
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0

# 2026-09-04, the clock a device figure was taken at, which none of them
# carried.
#
#   WHY. The entry above records a control that failed: the same binary read
#   a 1419-token prompt at 0.829 s in the morning and 1.155 in the evening,
#   quiet machine, settled part, three binaries agreeing with each other and
#   disagreeing with the morning. Sampling pp_dpm_sclk by hand during a run
#   showed the part swinging 800 to 2700 MHz and spending a third of the
#   prompt at the bottom. Nothing in a published figure said which had
#   happened.
#
#   WHAT IT DOES NOW. tests speed samples the clock and the power every
#   twentieth of a second through the region the wall time is taken around
#   and prints the mean, the range and the watts beside the load. The round
#   and the server carry it too. A host that keeps no such numbers says
#   nothing and is left where it was.
#
#   WHAT THE PUBLISHED DEVICE ROWS READ, one sitting, load under 0.71:
#
#     measurement                        mean   range        watts
#     110-token prompt, generating none   951   800 - 1539   22.8
#     12 generated                       1695   800 - 2542   16.4
#     110-token file, 12 generated       1710   800 - 2601   24.8
#     1419-token prompt                  1761   800 - 2675   28.6
#     64 generated                       2066   800 - 2541   30.4
#
#   A TENTH OF A SECOND OF WORK NEVER GETS THE PART ABOVE 1539 MHz, and a
#   second and a quarter of it holds two thirds of the top state. The row
#   this file has explained twice -- the short prompt that generates nothing
#   -- is the one at 951, and it is the only measurement here that does not
#   reach half. What was inferred from two hand-samplings is now a number on
#   the line.
#
#   REPORTED AND NOT GATED ON, which a measurement decided rather than a
#   preference. The load has a bound and refusing above it is right. A clock
#   has none: a threshold high enough to catch the slow sitting would refuse
#   the 951 row, and that row is a published figure this file keeps on
#   purpose.
#
#   THE INSTRUMENT'S OWN COST, measured rather than assumed, because a
#   sampler that perturbs what it samples is worse than no sampler. Reading
#   pp_dpm_sclk is not a file read -- the driver asks the part -- and at a
#   hundredth of a second, with the kernel's directories searched at every
#   sample, a 1419-token prompt read 1.492 s against 1.477: one per cent,
#   consistently, in three alternated pairs. Resolving the paths once at
#   elaboration took it to 0.7 per cent and a twentieth of a second took it
#   under what the measurement resolves -- 1.173 s against 1.167, with the
#   third of three pairs level. A run on a processor samples nothing at all:
#   the watcher is told whether to look, because a task nobody starts is a
#   block nobody leaves.
#
#   The five groups moved because speed_run.adb is in every one of their
#   source lists. Restamped with the measurement above as the reason: no
#   processor figure can have moved, because nothing samples on that path,
#   and the device perturbation is under half a per cent and inside what
#   three alternated pairs resolve.
#
# load:  under 0.71
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0

# 2026-09-04, where a server's time goes, and the fused half a round was
# still refused.
#
#   THE INSTRUMENT. tests speed --serve --budget reports the phases, summed
#   across the seats: a round charges them to the session the call was made
#   on and which seat that is changes with who is in the round, so one seat's
#   clock is one seat's rounds and the sum is the server's.
#
#   WHAT IT SAID. Eight seats, thirty-two callers, the 110-token prompt:
#
#     phase                  processor   device
#       FEEDING              10.268 s     0.000 s
#       PROJECTING            3.095       4.829
#       ATTENDING             0.517       3.153
#       everything else       1.402       0.750
#       accounted            15.282       8.732 of 9.213
#
#   The device's feeding is zero because its whole second half is one
#   submission and is counted under attending. What the first row says is
#   that FIFTY-FIVE PER CENT OF A DEVICE SERVING RUN WAS THE THREE MATRICES
#   BEFORE ATTENTION -- and a round was doing them one call at a time, on a
#   path a batch of one sequence had not used since the layer's first half
#   went over as one submission.
#
#   THE EXCLUSION WAS OVER-BROAD. Normalize_And_Project names no cache and
#   no run of positions: it reads the layer's input, normalizes each row,
#   multiplies the batch by three matrices, and turns the queries and keys
#   by angles the caller tabulates a row at a time. Every one of those was
#   already a row at a time. What does name a cache is Whole_Layer below it,
#   and that one still refuses a round -- it would have every row attend the
#   first member's history and write over the first member's cache.
#
#   ALTERNATED, medians of three, device, marks and digests unmoved:
#
#     1419 positions, 32 rounds, 8 members    1.775 s -> 1.656 s   1.07x
#     1419 positions, 32 rounds, 16 members   3.428   -> 3.192     1.07x
#     8 seats, 32 callers, 110-token prompt   9.328   -> 8.777     1.06x
#
#   AND THE EXCEPTION RE-PRICED. A stretch of sixteen positions or more is
#   read on its own on a device, because a round cannot use the matrix
#   attention kernel. Asked again with the fused half in place: reading
#   everything in rounds reads 9.2 s against 8.6, so the exception still
#   earns its keep and teaching that kernel one tile per member is still
#   worth about seven per cent. Not built.
#
#   AND WHAT DID NOT MOVE, which is why the five groups are restamped
#   rather than retaken. For a call that is not a round the change is
#   nothing at all: the condition it removed from the outer test is the one
#   it added to the inner choice, so a batch of one sequence takes the same
#   two branches it took before. Asked anyway, alternated: twelve tokens on
#   the device 0.264 s against 0.265, digest 5abff916f9d83ca6 either way,
#   and the clock 1705 MHz against 1688 -- which is the first pair of
#   figures in this file to say that the two sides were measured at the same
#   clock rather than to hope so.
#
# load:  under 1.10
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0

# 2026-09-04, the matrix attention kernel for a round: built, measured, not
# kept -- and what it found instead.
#
#   THE IDEA. A device answers sixteen query positions at once out of one
#   cache, and a round's rows do not share a cache, so a stretch of sixteen
#   positions or more is read on its own. But a MEMBER's own rows do share
#   one: they are one sequence, one cache and consecutive positions, which
#   is a batch in everything but name. Dispatch a round a member at a time
#   and each dispatch can have the kernel a batch has.
#
#   WHAT WAS BUILT. A row base in both attention shaders, so a dispatch can
#   answer a slice of the rows the caller laid out rather than all of them.
#   A per-member list on the attention step: one push and one dispatch a
#   member, each with its own cache base, its own first and last position
#   and its own row count. The kernel choice then falls out of the row count
#   as it does for a batch -- sixteen rows or more and the matrix kernel
#   runs. A round whose rows are one apiece keeps the table and its single
#   dispatch, because a push and a dispatch a member would cost more than
#   the kernel could save on one row.
#
#   WHAT IT MEASURED, device, eight seats and thirty-two callers:
#
#     110-token prompts, read in rounds, before   9.2 s
#     110-token prompts, read in rounds, after    8.9
#     110-token prompts, read on their own        8.70
#
#     7-token prompts, in rounds, without it      6.860
#     7-token prompts, in rounds, with it         6.961
#
#   It is better than it was and still behind reading alone, and on short
#   prompts it is worse than not having it: eight rows a member never reach
#   the tile the matrix kernel wants, so all the change adds there is eight
#   pushes and eight dispatches where there was one.
#
#   WHY IT LOSES, WHICH IS THE PART TO KEEP. The matrix kernel was not the
#   whole reason a lone prompt is fast on a device. A prompt read on its own
#   takes Whole_Layer -- the entire layer in one submission, the cache write
#   included -- and a round cannot, because that sequence names one cache
#   and one run of positions for every row. So the spread hands a round the
#   attention kernel and leaves it paying normalize-and-project, a rotation
#   on the host, a cache write on the host and a fused second half: four
#   submissions a layer against one.
#
#   THE NEXT THING IS THEREFORE NOT THIS. It is Whole_Layer for a round --
#   per-member cache writes and per-member attention inside one sequence --
#   and the per-member dispatch above is about a third of it. Reverted
#   rather than kept, because a path nothing reaches is a path nothing
#   checks, and the measurement is worth more than the code.
#
# load:  under 1.10; device 1519 to 1677 MHz mean of 2700 across the
#     alternated sets, which is what says the two sides were compared at the
#     same clock.
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0

# 2026-09-04, the fused whole layer for a round: built, worth twelve per
# cent, and wrong. Not kept.
#
#   THE IDEA, which the entry above named as the next thing. A prompt read
#   on its own takes Whole_Layer -- the entire layer in one submission, the
#   cache write included -- and a round could not, because that sequence
#   names one cache and one run of positions for every row. But a round
#   already has a per-row table in the cache, two words a row: where the row
#   has got to and where its block begins. Give the step that writes the
#   cache the same table the step that attends already reads, and the
#   sequence stops naming one of anything.
#
#   WHAT WAS BUILT. place.comp reads the table and writes each row into its
#   own member's block at its own position rather than one stride after the
#   row before it. Add_Place and Whole_Layer carry the table through; the
#   product push block went from twenty-eight bytes to thirty-two to hold
#   it. The readback was generalised: a round's rows come back into the
#   member whose cache they belong to, a row at a time. And the round was
#   let into the path.
#
#   WHAT IT MEASURED, alternated, medians of three, on the device:
#
#     1419 positions, 32 rounds, 8 members    1.655 s -> 1.466 s   1.13x
#     1419 positions, 32 rounds, 16 members   3.160   -> 2.809     1.12x
#     8 seats, 32 callers, 110-token prompts  8.786   -> 7.533     1.17x
#     8 seats, 32 callers, 7-token prompts    6.938   -> 6.329     1.10x
#
#   AND IT REMOVED THE EXCEPTION. With the whole layer in reach, prompts
#   read as rows of a round beat prompts read on their own -- 7.43 s against
#   8.21 -- so the sixteen-position threshold and the pass it protects could
#   both go.
#
#   AND THE ANSWERS ARE WRONG. Members of a round disagree, which is the
#   check the round driver exists for: two members with the same prompt and
#   the same rule must say the same text, and they do not.
#
#   THE ERROR HIDES AT A LONG CONTEXT, which is what made it worth writing
#   down. A wrongly placed new position is one part in fourteen hundred of a
#   softmax: a 1419-token round agreed with the processor for thirty-two
#   tokens and disagreed at sixty-four, while a seven-token round disagreed
#   at ten. The first measurements taken of this change were the long ones,
#   and they said it was right.
#
#   WHAT THE BISECT ESTABLISHED, for whoever takes it up:
#
#     The table does reach both shaders. Passing zero for it changes the
#     answer, so it is read and used.
#
#     One whole layer is already wrong. Letting a round take Whole_Layer for
#     the first layer alone moves the mark off the processor's, so it is not
#     an interaction between layers.
#
#     It is not the carry -- turning the carry off for a round changes
#     nothing -- and not the aliasing of the cache buffer in the place
#     shader: reading the table through the read-only binding that shader
#     already has, rather than through the writable one, changes nothing.
#
#   So the suspect is the fused layer's own cache write or its attention's
#   cache base: the two things the table is supposed to redirect and the two
#   the unfused path gets right. The measurement to take next is a round of
#   two members with the same prompt, with the layer's blend read back for
#   each of them and compared -- the collision says one member reads or
#   writes the other's cache, and that would say which.
#
#   Reverted. The prize is twelve per cent of a device round and a serving
#   run that needs no exception, and it is still there.
#
# load:  under 0.60; device 1933 to 2085 MHz mean of 2700 across the
#     alternated sets
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0

# 2026-09-04, a round's members now sit at different positions, and two
# things that came out of making them.
#
#   THE CHECK WAS WEAKER THAN IT LOOKED. `tests speed --round N` gave every
#   member the same prompt, so every member sat at the same position -- and
#   a round whose rows read each other's positions would have passed. Every
#   round figure in this file was taken that way. The driver gives each
#   member after the first a prompt one token shorter now, so the members
#   sit at different positions, which is what a server's members do.
#
#   THE SHIPPED ROUND PASSES IT. Processor and device agree at two, four
#   and eight members at one token and at four: 69a1504ba5575dfe,
#   b756687e5f5ee4ef, 997a64034590b0e8, 0f7b467c6c67777a. At thirty-two
#   tokens they part, which is the drift this file already records for two
#   rows or more against one and not a fault of the round.
#
#   WHAT IT SAID ABOUT THE WHOLE LAYER. The attempt above -- the fused whole
#   layer for a round -- fails the check, and the bisect it allowed got
#   further than the last one:
#
#     The placement is not the fault. Making the host write the cache over
#     what the fused layer's place step wrote leaves the answer wrong.
#
#     The table is read and used. Setting a row's reach to zero changes the
#     answer; setting its block to zero changes it again.
#
#     And one reading could not be reconciled: with the whole layer turned
#     off for rounds -- which leaves a round on exactly the shipped path --
#     the tree still answered differently from the processor. Either a
#     build in that sequence was stale or something in the shared plumbing
#     reaches a round, and the write-up says so rather than guessing.
#
#   AND A GHOST NOT REPRODUCED. This file records that widening the
#   attention push block by one field "once corrupted this device's answers
#   and nobody has explained" it. The product push block was grown by one
#   word here -- twenty-eight bytes to thirty-two, a spare word nothing
#   reads, every aggregate filled, nothing else touched -- and the round
#   agreed with the processor at ten and thirty-two tokens and the
#   twelve-token digest was 5abff916f9d83ca6 as always. SIZE ALONE IS NOT
#   IT. Whatever that note is about, it is not a block that got bigger.
#
#   The five groups moved because speed_run.adb is in their source lists.
#   The change is to the round driver's prefill, which no published figure
#   runs through: Measure is what takes them and it is untouched. The round
#   tables in the README were taken with the members level and are left
#   as they were, with this entry saying they were.
#
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0

# 2026-09-04, the whole layer for a round, hunted with the stronger check.
# What it is not, and one thing that should not be possible.
#
#   THE COLLISION WAS THE WRONG SIGNAL. The round driver prints each
#   member's first choice now, and in the failing tree BOTH members choose
#   the same wrong token: 275 at a logit of 17.13, where the processor and
#   the shipped device both say 29889 at 12.55 and 29892 at 12.60. Nothing
#   is leaking between members. The round's per-row table is not implicated
#   at all -- the whole round is wrong, equally, for every row.
#
#   THE PLUMBING IS NOT IT EITHER, bisected a piece at a time against the
#   committed llama.adb, each built and run:
#
#     the product push block grown by a word, every aggregate filled   ok
#     place.comp's two new fields, its table read, its binding renamed  ok
#     Add_Place carrying a table through to that push                   ok
#     Whole_Layer carrying a table through to its steps                 ok
#
#   AND THE THING THAT SHOULD NOT BE POSSIBLE. What breaks it is the
#   rewriting of Whole_Layer's arguments in terms of a constant --
#   Whole_Seat, which is Block_Base for a batch and zero for a round -- and
#   THAT CALL IS NOT REACHED FOR A ROUND. It was guarded with
#
#     if Rounding then raise Program_Error; end if;
#
#   as the first statement inside the branch. The exception does not fire.
#   The answers are still wrong.
#
#   What was ruled out around it:
#
#     Not the optimiser. It fails at -O0 with -gnatn removed, and the
#     failure is byte-identical to the -O3 one.
#
#     Not Ada's own checks missing something: this crate compiles without
#     -gnatp, so range and index checks are on and none fires.
#
#     Not stack layout by itself. A live local of the same type in the same
#     declarative part, written and read, changes nothing. Declaring the
#     two constants and marking them unreferenced changes nothing. It is
#     declaring one AND using it in the unreachable argument list.
#
#     Not the shaders drifting under us. After reverting, the regenerated
#     model_runner-shaders.ads is byte-identical to the committed one, so
#     the compiled words are a function of their sources.
#
#   THAT IS THE SAME SIGNATURE AS THE NOTE THIS FILE HAS CARRIED FOR WEEKS
#   -- "widening the attention push block by one field once corrupted this
#   device's answers and nobody has explained it". The entry above proved
#   the size is not it. This one says what the shape of the thing actually
#   is: a change with no execution path to the failing computation moves
#   the answer, deterministically, at every optimisation level. Something
#   in this program reads memory it does not own, and both notes are that
#   thing seen twice.
#
#   The whole layer for a round stays out. What is worth more than the
#   twelve per cent is the reproducer: `tests speed --round 2 --max-tokens 1
#   --backend device` against the same on the processor, with the two
#   constants and the argument rewrite applied to llama.adb, is four seconds
#   and shows it every time.
#
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0

# 2026-09-04, the whole layer for a round: found, fixed, kept. And the
# entry above corrected.
#
#   THE BUG WAS MINE AND IT WAS ONE LINE. The rewrite that broke it hoisted
#   Block_Base (Item) -- where this session's block of the device's cache
#   begins -- into a constant declared at the top of the block:
#
#     Whole_Seat : constant Element_Count :=
#       (if Rounding then 0 else Block_Base (Item));
#
#   Take_Block, which is what gives a session its block, runs a dozen lines
#   BELOW that declaration, inside the same block. So the constant held the
#   seat the session had before it had one -- zero -- and a batch wrote its
#   whole layer's cache into somebody else's block.
#
#   WHICH EXPLAINS EVERY SYMPTOM. Both members equally wrong: their
#   prefills, which are batches, wrote to the same wrong block. The round's
#   per-row table not implicated: it was right all along. The branch not
#   being entered for a round: the damage was done before the round, by the
#   prefill that does enter it. Surviving -O0 and showing no memory error:
#   the addresses were in range, just wrong.
#
#   AND IT CORRECTS THE ENTRY ABOVE. That one says "a change with no
#   execution path to the failing computation moves the answer" and draws a
#   parallel with the note about widening the attention push block. THE
#   PARALLEL IS WITHDRAWN: there was an execution path, through the
#   prefill, and I did not see it because I was watching the round. The
#   push-block note is unexplained again and this is not another sighting
#   of it. What that entry does still establish is that the push block's
#   SIZE is not the cause, which was measured directly.
#
#   WHAT FOUND IT, after ASan found nothing and -O0 changed nothing: asking
#   the program whether the two forms were equal, inside the branch that
#   uses them --
#
#     if <new form> /= <old form> then raise Program_Error; end if;
#
#   It fired on the first run. The rule that follows is worth more than the
#   fix: WHEN A REWRITE IS SUPPOSED TO BE ARITHMETICALLY IDENTICAL, ASK THE
#   PROGRAM, DO NOT READ IT AGAIN.
#
#   THE FIX. The block is read where it is used rather than hoisted, and
#   the comment beside it says why.
#
#   WHAT IT IS WORTH, alternated against the commit before it, medians of
#   three, on the device, every pair at a comparable clock:
#
#     1419 positions, 32 rounds, 8 members     1.667 s -> 1.473 s   1.13x
#     1419 positions, 32 rounds, 16 members    3.191   -> 2.809     1.14x
#     8 seats, 32 callers, 7-token prompts     6.993   -> 6.283     1.11x
#     8 seats, 32 callers, 110-token prompts   8.782   -> 7.489     1.17x
#
#   AND THE EXCEPTION IS GONE. A stretch of sixteen positions or more was
#   read on its own on a device, because a round could not use the matrix
#   attention kernel. With the whole layer in reach a round beats reading
#   alone -- 8.184 s against 7.496 for the same server -- so the threshold,
#   the pass it protected and the procedure that made it are removed. The
#   serving path agrees with the processor at one, two and four tokens with
#   it gone.
#
#   NOTHING OUTSIDE A ROUND MOVED: twelve tokens on the device 0.252 s
#   against 0.255, digest 5abff916f9d83ca6 either way, at 1698 MHz against
#   1676. The batch's own arithmetic is unchanged -- the seat and the
#   position are added at the call as they always were -- so the five groups
#   are restamped on that pair rather than retaken.
#
#   The round marks agree with the processor at two, four, eight and
#   sixteen members with the members sitting at different positions, which
#   is the check the entry before last put in.
#
# load:  under 0.60 before each alternated set
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0

# 2026-09-04, the first comparison of several sequences at once. This
# program's rounds against llama.cpp's batched decoding.
#
#   WHY IT WAS MISSING. Every figure in the llama.cpp table is one sequence
#   against one sequence, and most of this month's work was about serving
#   several. llama-batched-bench is the counterpart to tests speed --round:
#   -npl B decodes B sequences together, each with its own prompt
#   (is_pp_shared 0), and reports generated tokens over decode time, which
#   is the quantity the round driver reports.
#
#   THE RUNS:
#
#     llama-batched-bench -m M -c 24576 -npp 1419 -ntg 32 \
#       -npl 1,2,4,8,16 -ngl 99
#     llama-batched-bench -m M -c 24576 -npp 1419 -ntg 32 \
#       -npl 1,2,4,8,16 --device none -t 8
#     llama-batched-bench -m M -c 4096 -npp 7 -ntg 64 -npl 1,2,4,8,16 ...
#
#     tests speed --model M --prompt-file speed-prompt-long.txt \
#       --max-tokens 32 --round N --backend device|cpu
#     tests speed --model M --prompt-file speed-prompt-short.txt \
#       --max-tokens 64 --round N --backend device|cpu
#
#   GENERATING, TOKENS A SECOND, 1419-token prompts:
#
#     seqs   ours cpu  llama cpu   ours dev  llama dev
#        1      31.1      33.8       45.2      55.1
#        2      47.8      57.5       64.3      98.1
#        4      89.5      92.8      111.2     155.9
#        8     116.6     119.9      175.3     217.5
#       16     142.5     128.7      182.0     445.6
#
#   AND WITH SEVEN-TOKEN PROMPTS, sixty-four generated:
#
#     seqs   ours cpu  llama cpu   ours dev  llama dev
#        1      34.0      39.5       49.8      57.6
#        2      55.6      71.1       71.1     108.0
#        4     112.8     135.9      136.4     196.8
#        8     172.7     221.9      250.6     259.1
#       16     216.6     242.1      266.7     613.9
#
#   THE PROCESSOR HOLDS UP AND THE DEVICE DOES NOT. On the processor this
#   program is 1.03 to 1.28 behind and AHEAD AT SIXTEEN SEQUENCES WITH A
#   LONG CONTEXT -- 142.5 against 128.7, where llama.cpp's own curve turns
#   down. On the device it is behind everywhere: 1.2 at one, 1.4 to 1.5 at
#   two and four, 1.03 to 1.24 at eight, and 2.3 to 2.5 at sixteen.
#
#   THE SHAPE IS THE FINDING. A curve that is worse at two, level at eight
#   and twice as bad at sixteen is not a slower program but one whose
#   thresholds sit in different places. Ours stops scaling between eight and
#   sixteen -- 250.6 to 266.7 short, 175.3 to 182.0 long -- while
#   llama.cpp's roughly doubles over the same step, its decode time barely
#   moving (1.177 s to 1.149 at 1419 positions).
#
#   TWO THINGS TO LOOK AT, neither measured:
#
#     Sixteen rows is where a batch switches to the matrix attention kernel
#     here, and a round is excluded from that kernel. We lose it at exactly
#     the count where the comparison worsens most.
#
#     llama.cpp's own step from eight to sixteen is 2.37x, which is more
#     than the rows. Something switches on their side too, and what it is
#     would say whether the target here is a kernel or an arrangement.
#
#   WHAT IS FAIR. Each side gives every sequence its own prompt, both decode
#   with a full cache behind them, both report the decode alone. Ours
#   staggers the members' prompts by a token each so they sit at different
#   positions and llama.cpp's does not, which is too little work to see.
#   Prompt-reading is not compared here; both read sixteen prompts at the
#   rate they read one, and those numbers are in the single-sequence table.
#
# load:  under 0.60 before each set
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0; llama.cpp at 95b8e33e1

# 2026-09-04, the pass over the weights a tenth row cost. GROUP in
# row_product.comp, and a third compilation of it.
#
#   THE PROFILE THAT NAMED IT. A device round of N sequences, 64 generated
#   after 7-token prompts, is a step and not a slope:
#
#     8 rows   2.005 s
#     9 rows   3.557 s     78 % more time for 12 % more rows
#    10 rows   3.614 s
#    16 rows   3.830 s
#
#   Nine to sixteen all cost the same thing. row_product.comp's GROUP was
#   eight -- an invocation carries GROUP accumulators and reads each weight
#   once for all of them -- and the dispatch loop steps First by Batch_Group
#   until it covers the batch, so a batch of nine is two dispatches and the
#   second reads every weight in the model again.
#
#   The phase clock says attention is 0.0 per cent of a round's accounted
#   time, because a whole layer goes down as one submission; the matrix
#   attention kernel a round is excluded from, which the entry above named
#   as the candidate, is not what this was.
#
#   THE CHANGE. A third compilation of the same source with WIDER, GROUP at
#   sixteen, bound by Row_Line when the batch is above Batch_Group; Row_Group
#   makes the dispatch loop step by what the bound kernel carries. Below nine
#   the eight-wide kernel stays; at Tile_Least (32) the matrix product takes
#   over, and its tile is 128 vectors wide, so sixteen rows through it would
#   be seven eighths invented zeros.
#
#     glslangValidator -V -DWIDER src/shaders/row_product.comp row_wide.spv
#
#   WHAT IT IS WORTH, alternated, three rounds each, load 0.56 before the
#   first, TinyLlama-1.1B Q8_0 on the device:
#
#     9 seqs, 7-token prompts, 64 generated
#       before  3.573, 3.611, 3.632 s
#       after   2.810, 2.827, 2.859 s      1.28
#
#     16 seqs, same
#       before  3.799, 3.861, 3.874 s
#       after   3.059, 3.077, 3.088 s      1.26

#     9 seqs, 1419-token prompts, 32 generated
#       before  2.299, 2.319, 2.337 s
#       after   1.943, 1.944, 1.964 s      1.19
#
#     16 seqs, same
#       before  2.870, 2.872, 2.853 s
#       after   2.505, 2.483, 2.505 s      1.15
#
#   THE STEP AT NINE IS SMALLER AND STILL THERE: 2.002 s at eight against
#   2.810 at nine. One sixteen-wide pass is not one eight-wide pass -- it
#   does sixteen multiply-adds a weight rather than eight and holds sixteen
#   kilobytes of shared memory rather than eight, which halves how many
#   workgroups a compute unit keeps in flight. Reading the weights once
#   rather than twice buys more than that costs and not the whole
#   difference.
#
#   NOTHING OUTSIDE A ROUND MOVED, checked and not argued. A generated token
#   is one vector and takes the narrow kernel; a prompt is read 128 tokens
#   at a time and takes the matrix one. Alternated in this sitting:
#
#     1419-token device prompt   before 1.162, 1.176, 1.164 s
#                                after  1.173, 1.176, 1.186 s
#      110-token device prompt   before 0.123, 0.123 s
#                                after  0.123, 0.122 s
#
#   Digests unchanged: cbf29ce484222325 for the 110-token prompt,
#   448c2ed68ec342ee for sixty-four generated on the device.
#

# 2026-09-04, where a round of seventeen belongs, and three ways of
# reading a batch that were measured and not kept.
#
#   THE BAND THE ENTRY ABOVE LEFT BEHIND. Between Wide_Group (16) and
#   Tile_Least (32) a batch was served by neither kernel well, and a round
#   of seventeen cost more than a round of thirty-two:
#
#     seqs   before    after
#       16   3.153 s   3.112 s
#       17   5.431     4.155
#       24   5.704     4.336
#       31   5.982     4.482
#       32   4.348     4.348
#
#   Seventeen went down as two dispatches of the sixteen-wide row product,
#   which is two passes over every weight; thirty-two went to the matrix
#   product, whose tile is 128 vectors wide whether it is handed seventeen
#   or thirty-two, so it costs the same across that whole span. The
#   boundary belongs immediately above the row group and is now written
#   Tile_Least : constant := Wide_Group + 1, so the two cannot drift.
#
#   With a 1419-token context: 2.849, 2.986, 3.094 s before against 2.140,
#   2.183, 2.245 after -- 1.33 to 1.38. Sixteen untouched. Conformance
#   0 outside tolerance; the matrix kernel is half precision, so batches of
#   17 to 31 answer within tolerance rather than identically.
#
#   THE CEILING, MEASURED BEFORE ANYTHING WAS BUILT. The wide kernel reads
#   sixteen activations per decoded weight, one vector-length (8 KB) apart.
#   Two wrong-answer builds, alternated against the right one, 64 rounds of
#   sixteen sequences:
#
#     as it stands, sixteen loads sixteen lines apart   3.08 s
#     the same sixteen loads inside one line            2.16 to 2.45 s
#     one load, sixteen multiply-adds                   1.78 s
#
#   So the spread is worth about 1.34 and the loads about as much again.
#
#   THREE THINGS BUILT TO CHASE IT, ALL SLOWER.
#
#     A TRANSPOSED BATCH -- x[col * GROUP + k], the sixteen contiguous --
#     reads 4.02 s, a third worse. Contiguity across k costs contiguity
#     across lanes: eight lanes read eight consecutive columns today, one
#     32-byte fetch, and under a transpose they read eight blocks sixteen
#     floats apart. The layout cannot have both.
#
#     FOUR COLUMNS TO A LOAD, vec4 instead of four scalars, reads the same
#     3.10 s. The compiler did not merge them and cannot: the base is
#     vector * columns and nothing says columns is a multiple of four.
#
#     A WINDOW IN SHARED MEMORY, 256 columns at a time in the array the
#     reduction uses later, reads 12.9 s as first written and 5.3 s with
#     the bank conflicts out of it -- eight lanes a block apart land on one
#     bank of thirty-two, and giving each lane one word of every block
#     rather than every word of one block fixes that and makes the weight
#     read contiguous besides. Still 1.7 times slower than doing nothing.
#     The same answer this file recorded once before for a shared-memory
#     window; the cache is already serving these out of the nearest level.
#
#   AND A FOURTH, ABOUT THE ARITHMETIC RATHER THAN THE READS. Bounding the
#   accumulate loop by what the dispatch holds rather than by GROUP reads
#   5.72 s against 2.85 at nine sequences, 2.5 times worse. A dynamically
#   bounded loop is not unrolled and an array indexed by a variable in a
#   loop that is not unrolled goes to scratch memory. The shader's note
#   says the trip count has to be constant to unroll; it is right.
#
#   THE TWO FINGERPRINT GROUPS, RE-MEASURED, AND A CORRECTION.
#
#     THE ENTRY ABOVE PUBLISHED 1182.8 AND 1211.8 t/s FOR THE DEVICE PROMPT
#     ROWS AND SAID THEY WERE A CLOCK STATE RATHER THAN THE CODE. THREE
#     HOURS LATER THE SAME BINARIES SAY SO OUTRIGHT: alternated, four
#     readings each on the 1419-token device prompt, 0.812 to 0.841 s here
#     against 1.162 to 1.186 there, with the part at 1889 to 1944 MHz
#     rather than 1755. The rows below are the second reading. Both are
#     kept because the pair is the finding: the same binary, host and
#     command, 1.41 times apart on a clock state.
#
#     llama.cpp comparison, one sitting, load 0.49 before the first,
#     llama.cpp at 95b8e33e1:
#
#       110 tokens        prompt    generating 64
#         ours cpu        347.0       36.6
#         llama cpu       375.3       39.5
#         ours device    1506.8       50.3
#         llama device   1560.0       55.5
#
#       1419 tokens       prompt    generating 64
#         ours cpu        277.0       36.6
#         llama cpu       255.4       39.2
#         ours device    1648.1       50.3
#         llama device   1734.7       55.7
#
#     seconds behind the rates: cpu 110 in 0.317 s, 64 in 1.748; device
#     0.073 s and 1.273. llama-bench at -ngl 0 with a device present reads
#     724.2 t/s where --device none reads 375.3.
#
#     The device backend's six cells, load 0.49 to 0.57:
#
#       6-token prompt, 12 generated  cpu 0.378 s   device 0.260 s
#         evaluating the prompt           0.054         0.034
#         generating                      0.323         0.228
#         processor time                  1.98          0.08
#       110-token prompt, none generated  0.362         0.100
#         processor time                  2.79          0.01
#
#     THE 110-TOKEN DEVICE CELL HAS NOW READ 0.098, 0.122 AND 0.100 s IN
#     ONE DAY with the code unmoved between the second and third. The
#     middle sitting was the odd one; alternated binaries read 0.123 and
#     0.122 there and 0.100 here. A median of three inside one window is
#     not a defence against a window that is itself unusual, which is the
#     second time this had to be said today.

# load:  0.93 for the A/B, 0.49 to 0.57 for both published groups
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0

# 2026-09-04, a tile the size of the batch. matrix_product.comp compiled
# twice more with NARROW, and two items closed without building them.
#
#   THE PREMISE, MEASURED FIRST. A tile costs what its width costs whether
#   the batch fills it or not. With Tile_Least dropped to nine as a probe,
#   the 128-wide tile reads 3.983 s at nine rows and 4.139 at sixteen --
#   flat, because both round to one tile -- against 1.28 s for a batch of
#   one, which is a weight pass and nothing else. Most of the rest is
#   arithmetic on the zeros the rounding invented.
#
#   THE CHANGE. matrix_product.comp compiled again with NARROW (TILE_V 32
#   rather than 128) and again with NARROW beside MORE_FORMATS, so all four
#   format-by-width combinations exist. AV goes from eight accumulator
#   fragments to two; the weight tile is unchanged. Tile_Pipeline picks one
#   of the four and Uses_Matrix refuses the matrix path when the one this
#   batch needs was refused by the device, because Whole_Tiles decides the
#   room before the pipeline is bound.
#
#     glslangValidator -V --target-env vulkan1.3 -DNARROW \
#       src/shaders/matrix_product.comp matrix_narrow.spv
#     glslangValidator -V --target-env vulkan1.3 -DNARROW -DMORE_FORMATS \
#       src/shaders/matrix_product.comp matrix_narrow_extra.spv
#
#   WHAT IT IS WORTH, alternated three rounds each, load 2.25 before the
#   first -- above the usual gate, which is why every reading is given:
#
#     seqs   64 rounds, 7-token prompts, 64 generated
#        9   before 2.885, 2.865, 2.950   after 2.046, 2.003, 2.080   1.41
#       16   before 3.081, 3.116, 3.215   after 2.132, 2.175, 2.104   1.46
#       32   before 4.480, 4.551, 4.543   after 2.280, 2.298, 2.281   1.99
#       64   before 5.248, 5.169, 5.109   after 4.261, 4.193, 4.266   1.21
#
#     and 1419-token prompts, 32 generated, two rounds each:
#        9   1.953 -> 1.515   1.29
#       16   2.503 -> 2.000   1.25
#       32   2.268 -> 1.258   1.80
#       64   2.676 -> 2.248   1.19
#
#   BOTH BOUNDARIES WERE FOUND AND NOT CHOSEN.
#
#     Tile_Least is Batch_Group + 1. From two to eight the row product and
#     the narrow tile measure the same -- 1.812 against 1.817 at two, 2.048
#     against 2.009 at eight -- and the row product is exact where the tile
#     is half precision, so the exact one keeps what it does not lose on.
#
#     Narrow_Limit is 64 and is its own constant rather than the tile's
#     width reused. Above the narrow tile's width a batch is several narrow
#     tiles: that wins at 33, 48 and 64 (3.604, 3.932, 4.267 against 4.340,
#     4.743, 5.135) and loses at 128 (8.731 against 6.199).
#
#   THE WIDE ROW KERNEL OF THIS MORNING IS NOW UNREACHABLE ON THIS HOST.
#   Nine to thirty-one vectors was its band and the tile has it. A build
#   with it switched off alternates 2.091 and 2.398 against 2.370 and 2.080
#   -- noise on both sides. It stays for a device without the instruction.
#
#   TWO ITEMS CLOSED WITHOUT BUILDING THEM.
#
#     HALF-PRECISION ACTIVATIONS FOR THE WIDE ROW KERNEL. The kernel is no
#     longer reached here, so the change would be measurable by no figure
#     this repository publishes. Not built. Worth what it is worth on a
#     device without the matrix instruction, which is where to measure it.
#
#     THE PROCESSOR'S ROUND PATH HAS NO DEFECT OF ITS OWN. One sequence
#     33.9 t/s against llama.cpp's 39.5, sixteen 218.7 against 242.1 -- so
#     sixteen amortize 6.4 times here and 6.1 there. It amortizes better and
#     starts further back; the round is 1.11 behind because a token is 1.17
#     behind. The phase clock puts FEEDING at 64 per cent and PROJECTING at
#     20 at every count from one to sixteen. A perf profile of the round:
#
#       rows_by_strips           52.8 %
#       rows (the setup around it)  20.9
#       worker (the pool waiting)   10.4
#       mat_mul_range_packed         1.4
#       quantize_blocks              1.3
#
#     The 20.9 per cent is the next processor question and it is not a
#     question about rounds.
#
#   THE ROUND TABLE'S DEVICE COLUMN, re-measured whole, tokens a second:
#
#     seqs   short, was    is      long, was    is
#        1        50.0    49.8          44.6   44.5
