# move and by more than any spread: the four-bit file's 110-token processor
# prompt was published at 0.990 s and reads 0.875 in an alternated sitting
# whose other half read 1.046.
#
# The device figures did not move and could not have: nothing in the device
# path changed. Their fingerprints went stale because the kernels file is
# in every group's source list.

# 2026-08-26, half precision and brain floating point on the device's tile.
#
# What changed: matrix_product.comp decodes F16 and BF16 as well as the four
# block formats. Neither is really a decode -- half precision is already
# what the tile holds, and brain floating point has fewer mantissa bits than
# half precision has, so the widening is exact for every value inside half
# precision's range.
#
# Three alternated rounds in one sitting, one binary with the two formats
# refused by the kernel against the same binary with them allowed:
#
#   --repack bf16, 110-token device prompt   1.164 s  ->  0.302 s
#   Q8_0,          110-token device prompt   0.286 s  ->  0.287 s
#
# better in every round on the sixteen-bit one and inside the floor on the
# control. Digest cbf29ce484222325 throughout. A sixteen-bit model now reads
# a prompt level with the eight-bit one.
#
# Measured through --repack bf16 rather than on a sixteen-bit model file,
# and that is worth saying. There are three half-precision models on this
# machine and all of them are embedding models, which `tests speed` cannot
# run: it wants a model that generates. So the format's own path is
# exercised by the device format test at sixty-four rows and a batch of
# forty, and by the conformance sweep, while the figure above is the
# brain-floating-point path on a model the tool can run. The two share
# every line of the kernel but the four that widen a weight.
#
# What the two formats settle, which is worth more than the figure. On the
# device format test's fixtures the six tiled formats differ from the
# processor by:
#
#   half precision            7.9e-3
#   brain floating point      7.9e-3
#   eight-bit blocks          7.1e-3
#   four-bit k-quant          7.4e-3
#   five-bit k-quant          9.5e-3
#   six-bit k-quant           8.1e-3
#
# The first two arrive at the tile exactly -- there is nothing to lose in
# the widening -- and they differ by as much as the ones that are unpacked
# from six-bit fields. So the difference is the half-precision operand and
# not any one decode, which is what three separate entries above had guessed
# and none had shown.
#
# Binary32 is not tiled and will not be. The operand is half precision and a
# binary32 weight would lose thirteen bits of mantissa reaching it; a caller
# who kept a model at binary32 asked for those bits. It stays on the row
# product, and the device format test holds it to the tight bound -- 3.8e-6
# -- so that a change which quietly tiled it would fail.
#
# The device table and the llama.cpp comparison were NOT re-taken: both are
# Q8_0, and the control above is the figure they are built from.

# 2026-08-26, two comment lines reflowed to the width the file keeps, and
# the figures NOT re-taken.
#
# The device and outside fingerprints name
# model_runner-platform-device-products.adb, and a comment in it was
# rewrapped. No token of the program changed, so no figure can have moved;
# this entry exists because the fingerprint cannot tell a comment from a
# kernel and the rule is that a stale group is answered rather than
# re-stamped in silence.

# 2026-08-26, why the processor's kernel runs at a tenth of its own
# instruction's peak. A measurement and no change.
#
# The question. A 110-token prompt is 103.6 thousand million multiply-adds
# through the strip kernel -- counted by a counter in the panel loop, not
# estimated -- in 13.4 thousand million cycles at one worker. That is 7.75 a
# cycle where a byte dot product delivers thirty-two of them and this part
# issues two a cycle, so twelve per cent of the peak, and nothing in this
# file explained it.
#
# The answer is the instruction mix, and there is no stall in it.
#
# The insertion's loop body is forty instructions and eight of them are the
# byte dot product. The other thirty-two are the eight zeroed accumulators,
# the eight converts from integer to binary32, the eight multiply-adds that
# apply the scales, the two weight loads and four of loop control. That is
# one instruction in five, and it is not waste: a format with a scale every
# thirty-two elements cannot convert or scale less often than that.
#
# Counted end to end at one worker:
#
#   the whole prompt                     43.55 G instructions
#   the strip kernel's symbol            31.2  G   (71.6 %)
#   the insertion inside it              16.2  G   (37 %)
#   the Ada around the insertion         15.0  G   (34 %)
#   everything else                      12.4  G   (28 %)
#
# and 404.8 million turns of the insertion's block loop at forty
# instructions each is where the 16.2 comes from.
#
# So one instruction in ten across the prompt is a byte dot product, each
# delivers thirty-two multiply-adds, and the measured 3.26 instructions a
# cycle gives about ten multiply-adds a cycle before the rest of the program
# is counted. The 7.75 measured is that. The peak of sixty-four is a loop of
# nothing but the multiply, which no per-block-scaled format can have. The
# tenth is arithmetic, not a stall.
#
# An ablation that did not work, kept because it looked as though it had.
# The insertion was replaced by a single nop and the run counted 42.6
# thousand million instructions against 43.55, which would have made the
# byte dot product two per cent of the program. It is wrong: with the
# insertion stubbed the model no longer answers the prompt with its end
# token, so that run generated twelve tokens the reference run did not, and
# the two counts are of different work. The counter in the panel loop is
# what replaced it, and a counter is exact where a difference of two runs is
# not.
#
# What the mix says to do next, named and not built. One five-hundred-and-
# twelve-bit dot product covers two blocks in one instruction, and one
# convert and one multiply-add can then serve both if the scale table is
# built sixteen wide with the two blocks' scales side by side. That is four
# instructions per sixty-four elements where there are now four per
# thirty-two -- the ratio the accounting above says is the whole of the
# problem, halved. It is a rewrite of the insertion, of the table beside it
# and of the reduction that ends it, and it is the first time this file has
# had a reason to expect something from the wider registers rather than a
# hope.

# 2026-08-26, the five-hundred-and-twelve-bit rewrite: its premise tested
# and false, and the entry above corrected.
#
# The entry above said one wide dot product covers two blocks in one
# instruction and called that "the first time this file has had a reason to
# expect something from the wider registers rather than a hope". It was a
# hope. The premise is that a wide instruction does the work of two narrow
# ones in the time of one, and on this part it does not.
#
# A loop of eight independent byte dot products, nothing else in it, three
# runs each:
#
#   256-bit                   279 G multiply-adds a second
#   512-bit                   292 G
#   one 512-bit instruction is worth 1.04, 1.07, 1.10, 1.13 narrow ones
#
# and the same question of the multiply-add, which the kernel issues as
# often as the dot product:
#
#   one 512-bit vfmadd231ps is worth 1.03, 1.15, 1.16 narrow ones
#
# So a wide instruction takes twice as long and does twice as much: the
# datapath behind it is 256 bits and the wide form is issued over two
# passes. That is a fact about this processor -- a Zen 4 -- and not about
# the instruction set: a part with full-width datapaths would answer two,
# and the reason to write this down is that the next machine may.
#
# What that does to the rewrite. It would have cut the loop from eighty
# instructions per two blocks to about fifty-two, but the floating-point
# work is what it was: eight dot products, eight converts and eight
# multiply-adds per two blocks, at twice the cycles each. The kernel is
# already only about half occupied on those pipes and the hoist above
# proved it is not issue-bound either -- a fifth of its instructions
# removed bought two per cent of its time. A third fewer instructions for
# identical pipe work would buy about nothing, and it would cost a rewrite
# of the insertion, of the scale table beside it and of the reduction that
# ends it. It is not done.
#
# Two blocks are not even contiguous, which would have made the load worse
# rather than better: a Q8_0 block is thirty-four bytes, so the second
# block's quants begin two bytes after the first block's end and a wide
# load would need two narrow loads and an insert.
#
# What the accounting does point at, since it is not the registers. The
# floating-point floor for this kernel is three operations per thirty-two
# multiply-adds per row and vector -- 9.7 thousand million operations for
# this prompt, 4.9 thousand million cycles at two a cycle -- against 13.4
# measured. The difference is instructions that are not the arithmetic, and
# the largest block of those is the fifteen thousand million in the Ada
# around the insertion, which is more than the insertion's own sixteen.
#
# And that Ada works four floats at a time, because a strip is four
# vectors, on pipes that are two hundred and fifty-six bits wide. It uses a
# quarter of what it is issued on. A strip of eight vectors would put it in
# full-width registers and keep the accumulators at sixteen of the
# thirty-two there are. That is the next thing this file has a reason for,
# and it is about the arithmetic around the instruction rather than the
# instruction.

# 2026-08-26, the strip of eight: built, measured, thirteen per cent slower,
# and not kept.
#
# The entry above said the arithmetic around the insertion is fifteen
# thousand million instructions -- more than the insertion's own sixteen --
# and that it works four floats at a time, because a strip is four vectors,
# on pipes that are eight wide. A strip of eight would put it in full-width
# registers. That was the change.
#
# Built the cheap way, so that only the thing being tested changed: the
# strip carries eight vectors and the table beside it is built eight at a
# time, while the insertion still takes four and is run twice. The table's
# blocks are twice as far apart, so the insertion indexes it with a scale of
# two and the second row's four displacements move from sixteen to
# thirty-two bytes in. Three lines of assembly and one constant.
#
# Three alternated rounds, better in none:
#
#   110-token processor prompt, strip of four    0.776 s   3.82 s of processor
#   110-token processor prompt, strip of eight   0.881 s   4.45 s
#
# The stack frame it doubled is not the reason. The tables are sized for the
# widest row this kernel accepts -- a thousand and twenty-four blocks --
# which at eight vectors is a hundred and twenty-eight kilobytes of frame
# against sixty-four. Shrinking that room to two hundred and fifty-six
# blocks, a quarter of the frame, changed nothing: 0.748 and 0.780 s against
# a baseline of 0.776. So it is not the frame, and it is not worth shrinking
# the room either.
#
# What is left as the explanation is the shape of the read: the insertion
# now walks the table twice, each pass taking half of every sixty-four byte
# entry, where before each pass had a table of its own laid out contiguously.
# That is a guess, and it is written as one.
#
# A second thing the change did, which would have needed answering even if
# it had been faster. A batch of four to seven vectors no longer reaches a
# strip at all -- eight is the step now -- so it goes through the
# single-vector kernel instead, and the two agree to the sweep's bound
# rather than bit for bit. The twelve-token run's digest moved from
# 5abff916f9d83ca6 to 7d3e2df2d776ba62 on a six-token prompt. Nothing was
# wrong; the batching was different. It is recorded because a performance
# change that moves a published digest has to be noticed rather than
# discovered later.
#
# Where this leaves the processor kernel, after three attempts in a row that
# measured and were not kept. The hoist above removed a fifth of its
# instructions and bought two per cent of its time. The wide registers do
# not make an instruction worth two. The strip of eight halves the width of
# the arithmetic around the insertion and is slower. Taken together they say
# the same thing: this kernel's time is not set by the number of
# instructions it issues, and every lever reachable from the instruction mix
# has now been tried. Whatever the two-and-a-half times to the other runtime
# is, it is not that.

# 2026-08-26, attention's exponentials worked out once instead of a hundred
# and twenty-eight times.
#
# Attention is eighteen per cent of a device prompt -- 0.032 s of 0.177 by
# the doubling measurement above -- and had never been looked at. What it was
# doing, per tile of sixty-four cached positions: every lane worked out
# exp(score - raised) for every score of the tile to sum them, and then every
# lane worked it out again for every score for every component of the value.
# Eight thousand exponentials a tile where sixty-four are distinct.
#
# Each lane now works out its own and puts it back in the shared tile, which
# costs two barriers.
#
# Priced by dispatching attention twice and taking the difference, minimum of
# four runs:
#
#   attention, exponential per use     0.0336 s
#   attention, exponential once        0.0318 s
#
# Five per cent of attention, and one per cent of a prompt: the whole device
# prompt reads 0.293 s against 0.287 over three alternated rounds, which is
# inside the floor. The digest did not move.
#
# So the exponentials were not what attention spends its time on, and that is
# the finding rather than the five per cent. A hundred and twenty-eight-fold
# redundancy in a transcendental function was worth a twentieth of the
# kernel, which says the kernel is waiting for something else.
#
# What is left in it, unmeasured and named: a lane computes one score by
# walking sixty-four components of a key in series, and the sixty-four lanes
# of a wave then read sixty-four keys that are a whole cached position apart
# -- a thousand and twenty-four bytes for this model. Each lane's own walk is
# contiguous, so the traffic is not wasted, but a wave's worth of it lands on
# sixty-four cache lines at every step. That is the shape row_product.comp
# was rewritten to avoid, and it is the next thing to try here.

# 2026-08-26, attention's memory shape: the obvious fix built and lost, and
# a pattern this file can now state three times over.
#
# The entry above named the shape: a lane computes one score by walking a
# key in series, and the sixty-four lanes of a wave take sixty-four keys a
# whole cached position apart, so every step lands on sixty-four cache
# lines. The fix is the one row_product.comp uses -- read the tile the way
# the wave wants it, a position at a time with a lane to each component,
# into shared memory, and let every lane find its key there. Sixty-five
# floats to a row rather than sixty-four, so that no two lanes share a bank.
#
# It is correct: the suite passes and the twelve-token digest does not move.
# It is also slower, in every alternated pair:
#
#   110-token device prompt, keys read where they lie   0.492  0.589  0.579  0.584
#   110-token device prompt, keys staged in shared      0.678  1.073  0.771  0.752
#
# The machine carried a load of three to four from something else while
# these were taken, so the absolute numbers are inflated by about a
# half against the 0.29 s this prompt reads quiet. The pairs are alternated
# and the direction is the same in all four, at a ratio of 1.3 to 1.8, which
# is far outside anything the load explains.
#
# Why, and it is the same reason each time. The shared memory a workgroup
# takes went from two hundred and fifty-six bytes to sixteen and a half
# kilobytes. A workgroup processor has sixty-four kilobytes of it, so what
# it can hold at once fell from dozens of workgroups to three. Attention is
# not short of bandwidth -- a lane's own walk along a key is contiguous and
# every line it fetches is used sixteen times -- it is waiting, and what
# hides waiting is other workgroups. The staging bought locality the cache
# was already providing and paid for it in the only currency that mattered.
#
# That is the third time this project has staged something into shared
# memory by hand and measured a loss. The row product's shared window read
# 1.399 s against 0.516. The matrix product's staged operand read 1.499 ms
# against 0.580. Attention's staged keys read as above. Three different
# kernels, three different operands, one answer: on this part the path from
# the cache to the instruction is better than anything built on top of it,
# and shared memory is worth taking only where something is shared -- which
# is why the matrix product's weight tile, which four multiply-adds read,
# is the one staging in this program that pays.
#
# What is left for attention, and why it is not next. The other shape is to
# give a lane a component rather than a position, read the keys coalesced,
# and reduce across the lanes. The reduction wants subgroup operations,
# which this shader deliberately does not require -- it runs on a Vulkan 1.0
# device and row_product.comp records the same refusal for the same reason
# -- and without them it is a barrier a position, which is sixty-four
# barriers a tile against the two it has now.

# 2026-08-27, the nine formats still outside the tile: eight of them built,
# and the shader they were added to got slower for every format that was
# already in it. Not kept.
#
# What was built. Q4_0, Q4_1, Q5_0, Q5_1 and IQ4_NL share one shape -- a
# scale, sixteen bytes of nibbles, the low half of byte j element j and the
# high half element j + 16 -- so one branch takes all five and differs only
# in what is done to a nibble: centred on eight or sixteen, lifted by a
# minimum, given a fifth bit held apart, or read as an index into the table
# of levels. Q2_K and IQ4_XS took a branch each. Q3_K was left. All fifteen
# formats pass the device format test at sixty-four rows and a batch of
# forty, so the decodes are right.
#
# What it measured, three alternated rounds each:
#
#   Q4_0    110-token device prompt   0.532 s  ->  0.358 s
#   Q2_K    110-token device prompt   0.844 s  ->  0.813 s
#   Q8_0    110-token device prompt   0.285 s  ->  0.372 s
#
# The eight-bit format is the control. It got thirty per cent worse.
#
# It is the shader's size and not anything it runs, and that is the finding.
# With the eight new formats refused by the host -- Uses_Matrix turned back
# to the six it had, so not one line of the new code can be reached -- the
# same shader still reads 0.346, 0.427 and 0.350 against the old shader's
# 0.286, 0.285 and 0.293 in the same sitting. Twenty-one per cent for code
# that never executes.
#
# So a pipeline pays for every branch compiled into it, whether or not the
# branch is taken: the register allocation is for the whole shader and the
# occupancy that follows is for every dispatch. Writing the sixteen stores
# out rather than choosing a word from four with a chain of tests was worth
# something -- Q4_0 went from 0.406 to 0.358 -- and did not touch the
# control.
#
# That is the fourth time this file has recorded occupancy deciding a
# device question, and the first where the cause is the size of the code
# rather than the shape of a read. The three before it were shared memory
# taken by hand: the row product's window, the matrix product's staged
# operand, attention's staged keys.
#
# What it would take, named and not built. A second pipeline, so that the
# six formats that were fast keep a shader with six branches in it and the
# eight new ones get their own. The engine already makes five modules and a
# sixth is mechanical; what is not mechanical is that the tile itself -- the
# accumulators, the column loop, the operand loads and the store, about a
# hundred and twenty lines -- would then exist twice and drift. The cost of
# the duplication is real and so is the 1.45 times on Q4_0, and the trade
# wants deciding rather than assuming.
#
# One number worth keeping for whoever does. Q2_K gained three per cent
# where Q4_0 gained forty-five, and the likely reason is that a "Q2_K" file
# is a mixture like every other: much of it is Q3_K, which is the one format
# left outside, so tiling the rest of it cannot show. Q3_K would have to go
# in with them.

# 2026-08-27, the second pipeline: the same shader compiled twice, and every
# format but binary32 on the tile.
#
# The entry above priced a fourteen-branch shader and found it cost the six
# formats already on the tile twenty-one per cent for code the host would
# not let run. This is the answer it named: matrix_product.comp is compiled
# once as it stands, for those six, and once with MORE_FORMATS for the other
# eight, and the engine makes a sixth module and a sixth pipeline and binds
# whichever of the two decodes the weights it was handed. Q3_K went in with
# the eight, which the entry above said would have to happen.
#
# Three alternated rounds each, `tests speed --model M --prompt-file
# fixtures/speed-prompt.txt --max-tokens 0 --repeats 1 --backend device`,
# medians:
#
#   Q4_0    110-token device prompt   0.753 s  ->  0.411 s
#   Q2_K    110-token device prompt   0.991 s  ->  0.387 s
#   Q8_0    110-token device prompt   0.525 s  ->  0.532 s
#   Q4_K_M  110-token device prompt   0.423 s  ->  0.415 s
#   Q5_K_M  110-token device prompt   0.450 s  ->  0.446 s
#
# The last three are controls and none of them moves. Taken again in a
# second sitting after the gate: 0.811 -> 0.422, 1.014 -> 0.389, 0.529 ->
# 0.526, 0.418 -> 0.417, 0.446 -> 0.448. Same conclusion, and the controls
# hold in both.
#
# And once more from the binary that was committed, after a comment in the
# shader was reflowed and both compilations remade: Q4_0 0.423, Q2_K 0.399,
# Q8_0 0.526. The words in the package are the words that were measured.
#
# Corrected the same day. Every number above was taken with --repeats 1,
# which means every one of them is a first pass with the weights not yet on
# the device -- the tool's default is three passes and the median of them,
# which is what every other device figure in this file is. The A/B is
# unharmed: both sides were cold in the same way, alternated, three rounds.
# But the absolute seconds are about twice what the same command reports
# warm, and a reader comparing 0.526 here against the 0.280 in the two-row
# table under "The device backend" would have no way to know why. Taken
# again with the tool's own default, two alternated rounds, medians:
#
#   Q2_K    110-token device prompt   0.821 s  ->  0.295 s   2.79x
#   Q4_0    110-token device prompt   0.469 s  ->  0.311 s   1.51x
#   Q8_0    110-token device prompt   0.283 s  ->  0.276 s   control
#   Q4_K_M  110-token device prompt   0.291 s  ->  0.284 s   control
#   Q5_K_M  110-token device prompt   0.300 s  ->  0.293 s   control
#
# Same conclusion, same controls, and these are the figures the README
# carries. The lesson is narrower than it looks and worth keeping: a
# measurement can be internally sound and still not be comparable with the
# ones beside it, and --repeats 1 is how that happened here.
#
# How the two sides were built. Not two binaries from two checkouts: one
# line, `Item.Extra := Made` against `Item.Extra := Null_Handle`, so the
# "before" makes the second module and throws it away and every format it
# would have taken falls back to the row product exactly as it did at HEAD.
# That is a faithful before precisely because the first pipeline is
# compiled from the same words either way -- which is the whole claim being
# tested.
#
# Q2_K gained 2.6 times where the one-shader attempt gained three per cent.
# That is Q3_K, as the entry above predicted it would be: a "Q2_K" file is a
# mixture and much of it is Q3_K, so leaving that one format outside hid
# what tiling the rest of it was worth. The prediction is worth recording as
# having held, because most of the guesses in this file have not.
#
# A rejected shape, measured rather than argued. The first attempt put the
# decode in a shared function so that main() could be identical in both
# files. That restructuring alone -- same six formats, same arithmetic, no
# new decode anywhere -- read 0.358, 0.372 and 0.362 s against the
# committed 0.287, 0.285 and 0.283 in one sitting. Twenty-six per cent for a
# function call. So the sharing is done by the preprocessor: one text, two
# compilations, and the decode is the only part that differs.
#
# What the split costs, said plainly. Two SPIR-V modules in the binary
# instead of one, 48584 bytes against 42004. And a rule no compiler checks:
# the decode is the only part of matrix_product.comp that may differ between
# its two compilations. The tile itself -- the accumulators, the column loop,
# the operand loads, the store -- is one text and cannot drift, which was
# the objection to a second pipeline and is the reason this is a define
# rather than a second file.
#
# One tool change went with it. `tests shader` names the Ada constant for
# the compiled file rather than the source, so one source can arrive as two
# constants; the digest it records is still the source's, which is what the
# staleness check wants and is the same for both. Shaders compiled once are
# unaffected: their two names agree.
#
# The device format test now holds all fourteen tiled formats to the tiled
# tolerance at sixty-four rows and a batch of forty, and binary32 to the
# tight one, which is what proves binary32 is still refused on purpose.
#
# The device group's own published figures were NOT re-taken. The two-row
# table under "The device backend" and the llama.cpp comparison rows are
# both Q8_0, which is compiled into the first pipeline and cannot reach the
# second; it was measured as a control in both sittings here and did not
# move. Both fingerprints are restamped rather than left stale, and this
# paragraph is the answer the rule asks for.
#
# The machine: load 0.46 falling to 0.45 for the first sitting and 0.99
# rising to 1.00 for the second, both under the 1.20 this file requires.

# 2026-08-27, a generated token on the device, diagnosed and then halved.
#
# The item said "2.0 times behind and never diagnosed". It is diagnosed now,
# and the diagnosis was wrong in the way these usually are: the thing that
# looked like a bandwidth problem was not one.
#
# What was measured first. Five real models, each generating sixty-four
# tokens on the device, against their own size:
#
#   Q2_K    461 MB   58.5 ms a token    7.9 GB/s
#   Q4_0    609 MB   29.8 ms a token   20.4 GB/s
#   Q4_K_M  638 MB   68.1 ms a token    9.4 GB/s
#   Q5_K_M  747 MB  117.7 ms a token    6.3 GB/s
#   Q8_0   1171 MB   34.4 ms a token   34.0 GB/s
#
# Time per token is not a function of size. The smallest file is twice the
# time of the largest, and the achieved rate varies by five and a half times
# across formats. A kernel bound by the bus cannot do that, so the row
# product is bound by something it does per element rather than per byte.
#
# `tests device-bench` grew a per-format sweep to say so directly -- a 2048
# by 4096 resident matrix, one vector, absolute seconds rather than the
# ratio against the processor that `tests benchmark` prints, because a ratio
# cannot say which side moved and the question was entirely about one side.
# Nanoseconds an element, before:
#
#   q4_0  0.0243   q4_1  0.0275   q5_0  0.0300   iq4nl 0.0301
#   q8_0  0.0311   q5_1  0.0335   q2_k  0.0500   q3_k  0.0567
#   iq4xs 0.0572   q4_k  0.0590   q6_k  0.0635   q5_k  0.1101
#   bf16  0.1222   f16   0.1236   f32   0.1284
#
# Multiply any of those by the model's element count and the token falls
# out: q8_0 0.0311 ns predicts 34.2 ms against 34.4 measured, q5_k 0.1101
# predicts 121 against 118, q2_k 0.0500 predicts 55 against 58.5. So a
# generated token on the device IS the row product, to within the noise.
# Attention, the blends, the sampling and the submissions are all inside the
# error bar. That is worth knowing on its own: it says where every future
# measurement of a token should go.
#
# And f16 and f32 name the cause between them. Binary32 reads four bytes an
# element at 31 GB/s, which is the bus. Half precision reads two and takes
# the same time per element at 16 GB/s -- half the traffic for none of the
# time. Something fixed was being paid per element by every format.
#
# What it was: GROUP. The shader carries GROUP accumulators, reads GROUP
# vector offsets per weight and reserves 256 * GROUP floats of shared memory
# for its reduction, and GROUP is eight because a prompt reads eight vectors
# to a pass. A generated token is one vector. Seven eighths of the
# arithmetic, seven eighths of the activation reads and seven eighths of
# eight kilobytes of shared memory a workgroup were being spent on padding
# that the write at the end throws away -- and on this device the shared
# memory is the part that matters, because it is what bounds how many
# workgroups a compute unit will hold.
#
# So row_product.comp is compiled twice, the second with SINGLE, which sets
# GROUP to one; the engine binds the narrow pipeline when the batch is one
# and the wide one otherwise. The same trade matrix_product.comp makes
# between its two compilations, using the machinery built for it that
# morning, and the third place in this file where occupancy decided a device
# question.
#
# Nanoseconds an element, after, and the ratio:
#
#   q5_k  0.1101 -> 0.0278  3.96x     iq4xs 0.0572 -> 0.0233  2.45x
#   q2_k  0.0500 -> 0.0244  2.04x     q3_k  0.0567 -> 0.0282  2.01x
#   f16   0.1236 -> 0.0626  1.98x     bf16  0.1222 -> 0.0630  1.94x
#   iq4nl 0.0301 -> 0.0198  1.53x     q4_0  0.0243 -> 0.0165  1.47x
#   q4_1  0.0275 -> 0.0192  1.44x     q5_0  0.0300 -> 0.0229  1.31x
#   q4_k  0.0590 -> 0.0450  1.31x     q8_0  0.0311 -> 0.0260  1.20x
#   q6_k  0.0635 -> 0.0592  1.07x     q5_1  0.0335 -> 0.0317  1.06x
#   f32   0.1284 -> 0.1219  1.05x
#
# Binary32 barely moves, and that is the control the diagnosis wanted: it
# was the one format already at the bus, so it was the one format with
# nothing to win. Half precision, which sat beside it at the same time per
# element, doubles.
#
# On real models, three alternated rounds each, sixty-four generated,
# medians, better in every round:
#
#   Q5_K_M  7.499 s -> 2.301 s   3.26x    8.5 -> 27.8 tokens a second
#   Q2_K    4.211 s -> 1.433 s   2.94x   15.2 -> 44.7
#   Q4_K_M  4.594 s -> 3.061 s   1.50x   13.9 -> 20.9
#   Q4_0    1.970 s -> 1.324 s   1.49x   32.5 -> 48.3
#   Q8_0    2.072 s -> 1.630 s   1.27x   30.9 -> 39.3
#
# Bit-identical: the eight-bit run prints 448c2ed68ec342ee either way. The
# narrow kernel accumulates the same products in the same order into the
# same eight-lane reduction; what it drops is arithmetic on padding whose
# results were never written. And the prompt does not move -- 0.336 s
# against 0.340 -- because a prompt is not a batch of one and never binds
# the narrow pipeline.
#
# The gap to llama.cpp on a generated token goes from 2.07 times to 1.49.
#
# Two formats did not gain much and are worth naming rather than averaging
# away: Q6_K at 1.07 and Q5_1 at 1.06. Whatever binds those two is not the
# group width, and nothing here has asked what it is.
#
# The device figures were re-measured; the processor's were not, and could
# not have moved -- nothing outside the device engine and the two shader
# compilations changed.
#
# What was re-measured with it, all on 2026-08-27 and all in one sitting.
#
# The two-row table under "The device backend", both columns, each cell
# waiting for the machine to fall below 1.20 before it started. Load 0.84
# rising to 1.41 across the four runs.
#
#   6-token prompt, 12 generated       cpu 0.456 s   device 0.350 s
#     -- evaluating the prompt             0.070         0.062
#     -- generating                        0.389         0.289
#     -- processor time                    1.61          0.03
#   110-token prompt, nothing generated    0.806         0.282
#     -- processor time                    3.87          0.11
#
# The generating row is the one that moved: 0.378 s to 0.289. The prompt row
# reads 0.282 against the 0.280 it read before, which is the control -- a
# prompt is never a batch of one. The processor column moved by two and five
# per cent with no processor code changed, which is the sitting.
#
# The fifteen per-format ratios from `tests benchmark --wait 30`, device
# time over processor time, one vector a pass and eight. Every cell of the
# one-vector column fell and no cell of the eight-vector column moved:
#
#   q2_k  0.28 -> 0.13   iq4nl 0.24 -> 0.14   q5_1  0.26 -> 0.17
#   q5_0  0.26 -> 0.17   q4_1  0.26 -> 0.17   q3_k  0.41 -> 0.20
#   q8_0  0.36 -> 0.23   iq4xs 0.41 -> 0.25   q4_0  0.40 -> 0.26
#   q5_k  0.59 -> 0.30   f16   0.40 -> 0.34   q4_k  0.52 -> 0.37
#   q6_k  0.52 -> 0.49   bf16  0.68 -> 0.60   f32   0.89 -> 0.72
#
# and eight a pass, as the control: q8_0 0.08 -> 0.083, f16 0.12 -> 0.122,
# q5_k 0.15 -> 0.145, q4_0 0.07 -> 0.079. Thirty-two a pass 0.036 -> 0.039.
# The narrow kernel is bound to a batch of one and the eight-vector column
# is what says so rather than what asserts it.
#
# The llama.cpp comparison, both programs in one sitting, llama.cpp still at
# 95b8e33e1 build 10597, three rounds. The engine's rows are 110 tokens in
# 0.806 s and 64 generated in 2.077 s on the processor, 0.267 s and 1.570 s
# on the device.
#
#   model_runner, processor    136.5 t/s prompt, 30.8 t/s generating
#   llama.cpp, processor       383.6 t/s prompt, 40.2 t/s generating
#   model_runner, device       412.0 t/s prompt, 40.8 t/s generating
#   llama.cpp, device         1659.4 t/s prompt, 57.3 t/s generating
#   llama.cpp at -ngl 0        781.1 t/s, which is the device answering
#
# The gap is 2.8 and 1.3 on the processor, 4.0 and 1.4 on the device. The
# device generating row has read between 26.9 and 32.0 across twelve
# sittings of code that did not change; 40.8 is the first time it has moved
# for a reason rather than for a sitting, and the device now generates
# faster than llama.cpp does on the processor.
#
# The three-way table of where the weights are was NOT re-taken. It reports
# how much of the context the device holds under three --device-memory
# settings, which is residency and allocation; nothing in either change
# touches what is allocated or where.
#
# load: 0.84 to 1.41 for the device backend table, 0.42 to 0.61 for the
# generating rounds, 1.17 to 1.04 for the matrix-pipeline rounds, --wait 30
# for the benchmark, and 0.45 to 1.16 for the llama.cpp rows with 1.07 and
# 1.50 at the start of the two llama-bench runs, 2026-08-27.

# 2026-08-27, attention across a subgroup: nineteen per cent on the kernel,
# nothing on a prompt, four per cent on a generated token with a long
# context. Kept, and the reason the three differ is the finding.
#
# The item said the cross-lane reduction wanted operations the shader
# "deliberately does not require" because it runs on a Vulkan 1.0 device,
# and that the argument was weaker than it sounded -- the matrix product
# already needs more than 1.0 and the instance already negotiates to 1.3.
# That much was right. attention.comp is now compiled twice, the second with
# SUBGROUPS, and the engine binds it where the device reports the basic and
# arithmetic subgroup operations in a compute stage. That is a different
# question from the matrix instruction and gets a different answer: subgroup
# arithmetic is core 1.1 and asks for no extension, so the query moved out
# from under the cooperative-matrix guard it was nested in.
#
# What changed in the shader. Per tile of sixty-four scores every lane
# walked all sixty-four entries twice -- once for the largest and once for
# the sum -- which is a hundred and twenty-eight serial reads a lane against
# the sixty-four the dot product itself takes. Both become one instruction
# and a partial per subgroup. The weighted sum of values still reads the
# tile from shared memory, because that is a gather and not a reduction.
#
# The kernel alone, `tests device-bench`, A/B in one sitting:
#
#   1 head,  64 wide,  512 positions   0.000626 s -> 0.000528 s   1.18x
#   32 heads, 64 wide, 512 positions   0.000622 s -> 0.000524 s   1.19x
#   32 heads, 64 wide, 2048 positions  0.002274 s -> 0.001934 s   1.18x
#   as the engine asks it               0.000601 s -> 0.000505 s   1.19x
#   32 heads, 64 wide, 16 positions    0.000097 s -> 0.000106 s   none
#
# Sixteen positions is one tile and the call is all launch, which is why it
# does not move; the rest are nineteen per cent, four shapes, repeatable.
#
# What a run does with that: mostly nothing.
#
#   110-token device prompt        0.269 s -> 0.271 s   nothing
#   1419-token device prompt       4.377 s -> 4.268 s   inside the spread
#   64 generated after 6 tokens    1.58 s  -> 1.60 s    nothing
#   64 generated after 1419 tokens 2.401 s -> 2.305 s   1.042x, 3 of 3
#
# The last is the only one that moves, and it moves cleanly: 2.401, 2.397,
# 2.409 against 2.292, 2.305, 2.305, better in every round, and the answers
# are bit-identical -- 7ec6b755e53e16b4 either way.
#
# How much attention there is to win, priced by doubling the dispatch rather
# than by trusting the phase counter. Doubling both attention dispatch sites
# and taking the difference:
#
#   110-token prompt        + 0.033 s on 0.27 s   12 per cent
#   1419-token prompt       + 1.35  s on 4.29 s   31 per cent
#   64 generated after 6    + 0.02  s on 1.56 s    1.3 per cent
#
# A methodological note that cost an hour. The first doubling was put in the
# sequence path only and read zero, which looked like proof that attention
# was free. It was proof that a prompt does not take that path: the engine
# reaches attention through the standalone call, and there are two dispatch
# sites. Doubling one of two sites measures the one nobody uses. The phase
# counter and the doubling agree once both sites are doubled, which is what
# says the instrument is sound.
#
# So attention is nearly a third of a long prompt and the kernel is a fifth
# faster, and the prompt does not move. The reason is the workgroup count.
# A prompt dispatches one workgroup per head per position -- thirty-two by
# fourteen hundred and nineteen, some forty-five thousand of them across
# twelve compute units -- and at that occupancy the serial reductions'
# latency is hidden behind other workgroups; what is left is the memory the
# keys come out of, which the subgroup operations do not touch. A generated
# token dispatches thirty-two workgroups, one a head, and there is nothing
# to hide behind, so removing a hundred and twenty-eight serial reads a lane
# shows up directly.
#
# That is the general form and it is worth stating on its own: the same
# kernel change is worth a fifth where the device is latency-bound and
# nothing where it is saturated, and which of the two a call is depends on
# how many workgroups it makes rather than on how much arithmetic it does.
# `tests device-bench` measures the latency-bound shape by construction --
# thirty-two workgroups -- which is why it reads nineteen per cent for a
# change a prompt cannot feel. A kernel benchmark and a run are not
# measuring the same machine.
#
# Kept rather than reverted, and the case for that is narrow: it is faster
# or level everywhere, the answers do not change, and the one real shape it
# helps -- generating with a long context -- is where a chat session spends
# its time. It is not kept because of the nineteen per cent.
#
# What is left in the kernel, named and not attempted. The dot product reads
# kv[k_at + c] with the lanes a key-width apart, which is the uncoalesced
# direction; the weighted sum reads the value rows with the lanes
# consecutive, which is the good one. Fixing the first wants the key block
# staged through shared memory, and hand-staging into shared memory has now
# lost three times on this device.
#
# What was re-measured with it. The two-row table under "The device backend",
# both columns, one sitting at a load of 0.47 to 0.59:
#
#   6-token prompt, 12 generated       cpu 0.446 s   device 0.346 s
#     -- evaluating the prompt             0.071         0.058
#     -- generating                        0.374         0.288
#     -- processor time                    1.61          0.03
#   110-token prompt, nothing generated    0.769         0.287
#     -- processor time                    3.80          0.11
#
# Neither device row is meant to move and neither does: a six-token context
# leaves attention at a per cent of a token, and a 110-token prompt
# saturates the part.
#
# The llama.cpp comparison, both programs in one sitting, llama.cpp still at
# 95b8e33e1 build 10597, three rounds. The engine's rows are 110 tokens in
# 0.745 s and 64 generated in 2.066 s on the processor, 0.259 s and 1.555 s
# on the device.
#
#   model_runner, processor    147.7 t/s prompt, 31.0 t/s generating
#   llama.cpp, processor       344.4 t/s prompt, 40.0 t/s generating
#   model_runner, device       424.7 t/s prompt, 41.2 t/s generating
#   llama.cpp, device         1670.7 t/s prompt, 56.4 t/s generating
#   llama.cpp at -ngl 0        762.4 t/s, which is the device answering
#
# The gap is 2.3 and 1.3 on the processor, 3.9 and 1.4 on the device.
# llama.cpp's own processor prompt row read 383.6 in the sitting before this
# one and 344.4 in this one, with a spread of 29 on the second -- neither
# program changed and the difference is the sitting, which is why the ratio
# moved from 2.8 to 2.3 with nothing done to the processor.
#
# The fifteen per-format ratios were NOT re-taken. They come from
# Measure_Device_Ratio in tests/src/benchmarks.adb, which dispatches a
# matrix product and nothing else; no attention call is made anywhere in
# that benchmark, so no attention change can reach it. The three-way table
# of where the weights are was not re-taken either, for the reason given
# above it.
#
# load: 0.47 to 0.59 for the device backend table, 0.50 to 0.55 for the
# llama.cpp rows, 0.41 to 0.85 for the attention rounds and the doublings,
# 2026-08-27.

# 2026-08-27, a workgroup answers four queries instead of one: attention a
# half faster and a device prompt a tenth.
#
# The item was that attention reads the keys once per query position, and
# there are fourteen hundred of them. It was priced before anything was
# built. Confining the key and value reads to eight cached positions -- the
# same instructions, the same arithmetic, the same number of loads, but all
# of them cache-resident -- read attention at 1.27 s against 2.06 on a
# 1419-token prompt. So two fifths of attention was the traffic, and the
# traffic was almost entirely re-reading: the keys a group of eight heads
# shares are read by each of them, and every query position reads them
# again.
#
# What was built. attention.comp gains a QUERIES constant and is compiled a
# third time with QUERY_TILE, which sets it to four. A workgroup then holds
# four queries, and the key component a lane reads for its cached position
# is multiplied into all four instead of being read again by each. Everything
# else is the same text: QUERIES is one in the other two compilations, the
# per-query arrays are of length one there, and the loops over them have a
# single turn.
#
# A block wants a maximum and a sum per query per tile, so the tiled
# compilation requires the subgroup one -- which is why this could not have
# been built before this morning. Without subgroup operations a block would
# walk shared memory four times a tile, which is what the block exists to
# avoid.
#
# The block width, three sizes at 1419 tokens, attention alone:
#
#   two queries a workgroup    1.709 s, 1.706 s
#   four queries a workgroup   1.359 s, 1.352 s
#   eight queries a workgroup  1.657 s, 1.612 s
#
# Four. Eight loses what it gains: a query costs four accumulators, a
# running maximum, a running sum and four held components, and at eight the
# register pressure takes back more occupancy than the extra reuse buys.
# That is the fourth time occupancy has decided a device question here and
# the first where it decided a width rather than a yes or no.
#
# At four, alternated rounds:
#
#   attention alone, 1419 tokens   2.030 s -> 1.356 s   1.50x
#   110-token device prompt        0.267 s -> 0.245 s   1.09x, 3 of 3
#   1419-token device prompt       4.211 s -> 3.922 s   1.07x
#   64 generated, 6-token context  1.567 s -> 1.600 s   unchanged
#   64 generated, 1419 context     2.285 s -> 2.299 s   unchanged
#
# Generating does not move and is not meant to: one position is fewer than a
# block, so the engine binds the subgroup kernel there and the tiled one is
# never reached. Attention lands at 1.36 s against the 1.27 s the ablation
# said was the floor for this kernel's arithmetic, so the block recovered
# nearly all of the traffic there was to recover.
#
# Bit-identical, which is worth saying because it was not obvious: the long
# prompt prints 1a26d24d33b8957b either way. A block changes which
# invocation computes a dot product and not the order it accumulates it in,
# and the softmax sum is a subgroup add on both sides.
#
# A mistake worth recording, because a test suite did not catch it. Sweeping
# the block width with sed set QUERIES in both arms of its own #ifdef, so
# the untiled compilations were built with four as well -- a kernel that
# answers four queries a workgroup, dispatched one workgroup a query. Every
# one of the two hundred and eighty tests passed, because the suite's
# batches are shorter than a block and the extra queries were clamped onto
# the last real one. A repository check now reads the number out of the
# shader and requires the engine's Query_Block to equal it.
#
# What is left, and it is the larger half. The ablation's 1.27 s is 182
# Gflop of useful arithmetic in 1.27 s, which is about 145 gigaflops a
# second on a part whose matrix tile reaches 2031. Attention is now bound by
# instruction issue rather than by memory, and closing that wants the scores
# computed as a matrix product through the cooperative matrix -- which is
# what the other runtime does. That is a much larger change than this one
# and it is the next thing to try on attention, not a variation on this.
#
# What was re-measured with it. The two-row table under "The device
# backend", both columns, one sitting:
#
#   6-token prompt, 12 generated       cpu 0.449 s   device 0.354 s
#     -- evaluating the prompt             0.075         0.064
#     -- generating                        0.379         0.290
#     -- processor time                    1.63          0.03
#   110-token prompt, nothing generated    0.754         0.243
#     -- processor time                    3.81          0.09
#
# The 110-token device row is the one that moves, 0.287 to 0.243, and it is
# this change; the six-token run's prompt is six positions, which is more
# than a block, so it moves a little too.
#
# The llama.cpp comparison, both programs in one sitting, llama.cpp at
# 95b8e33e1 build 10597, three rounds. The engine's rows are 110 tokens in
# 0.738 s and 64 generated in 2.069 s on the processor, 0.251 s and 1.544 s
# on the device.
#
#   model_runner, processor    149.1 t/s prompt, 30.9 t/s generating
#   llama.cpp, processor       353.1 t/s prompt, 39.9 t/s generating
#   model_runner, device       438.2 t/s prompt, 41.5 t/s generating
#   llama.cpp, device         1652.6 t/s prompt, 56.3 t/s generating
#   llama.cpp at -ngl 0        769.2 t/s, which is the device answering
#
# The gap is 2.4 and 1.3 on the processor, 3.8 and 1.4 on the device. The
# device prompt row has gone 412.0 to 424.7 to 438.2 across three sittings
# and the last step is this change.
#
# The fifteen per-format ratios and the three-way table of where the weights
# are were not re-taken, for the reasons given against the entry above:
# neither benchmark makes an attention call, and neither touches what is
# allocated or where.
#
# load: 0.93 to 1.26 for the device backend table and the llama.cpp rows,
# 0.67 to 1.44 for the block sweep and the alternated rounds, 2026-08-27.

# 2026-08-27, attention through the cooperative matrix: built, measured
# twelve per cent slower, and not kept.
#
# The entry above ended by naming this as the next thing to try, and the
# arithmetic said it should work. With the query block in, confining
# attention's reads to a cache-resident window bought only 1.18 times
# against the 1.63 it had bought before, so the memory was mostly dealt with
# and what remained was arithmetic: 182 Gflop in about 1.2 s, which is
# roughly 153 gigaflops a second on a part whose matrix tile reaches 2031.
# Thirteen times of headroom, on an instruction this program already
# dispatches.
#
# What was built. src/shaders/attention_matrix.comp: Q times K transposed as
# one matrix product, the softmax in shared memory between them, and the
# weights times the values as another, at the sixteen-by-sixteen-by-sixteen
# half-precision shape the device offers and matrix_product.comp already
# uses. Sixteen queries a workgroup, one subgroup, and the caller binds it
# only for a head sixty-four wide or narrower whose width is a whole number
# of sixteen -- which TinyLlama is.
#
# A separate source rather than a fourth compilation of attention.comp,
# because the two share no text. That is the line this work has settled on:
# a define where only part of a kernel differs, a file where the shape does.
#
# What it measured, attention alone on a 1419-token prompt, alternated:
#
#   query block of four (kept)   1.396, 1.444, 1.397, 1.527 s
#   matrix, 16 keys a tile       1.565, 1.579, 1.622 s
#   matrix, 32 keys a tile       1.583, 1.596, 1.607 s
#   matrix, 64 keys a tile       1.858 s
#
# Twelve per cent slower at its best, and the whole prompt did not move
# outside its own spread either way.
#
# Why, and the reason is the one this file keeps arriving at. The
# instruction wants half precision and the cache is binary32, so the keys
# and the values have to be staged through shared memory to be converted;
# the softmax is not a matrix product, so the scores come out to shared
# memory and the weights go back in; and a running softmax multiplies the
# answer by a factor a row at a time, which the instruction cannot do to its
# own registers, so the accumulators come out and go back once a tile as
# well. Shared memory is what bounds occupancy on this part, and the three
# variants order themselves by exactly that and nothing else:
#
#   16 keys a tile   8768 bytes a workgroup   1.579 s
#   32 keys a tile  11328 bytes a workgroup   1.607 s
#   64 keys a tile  16448 bytes a workgroup   1.858 s
#
# A wider key tile does fewer softmax round trips per key and is slower for
# it, which rules out the round trips as the cost and leaves the room they
# need. The kernel it replaces uses one kilobyte.
#
# That is the fourth time hand-staging into shared memory has lost on this
# device, and the first where what it was staging for was the matrix
# instruction rather than a hand-written loop. The three before were the row
# product's window, the matrix product's staged operand and attention's
# staged keys. The matrix product itself remains the one place staging pays,
# and the reason has not changed: its weight tile is read by four
# multiply-adds apiece, where these operands are read once.
#
# It also changes the answers, which the row product's version does not: the
# operands are rounded to half precision, so a deep prompt printed
# 77423c8d0bfd05a3 against 7ec6b755e53e16b4. That is a cost as well as a
# consequence, and it would have needed the sweep's tolerance to carry it.
#
# What would have to be different for this to pay. A cache already in half
# precision would remove the staging of the keys and the values, which is
# two thirds of the room -- the session cache has a Halved mode and it is
# lossy, so that is a trade rather than a fix. Beyond that the answer is a
# part with more shared memory per compute unit, which is not this one.
#
# Not kept. attention.comp with a query block of four stands.

# 2026-08-27, the same kernel on a half-precision cache: the arithmetic
# works and the cache cannot. Also not kept, and the second reason is the
# one that settles it.
#
# The entry above ended by naming what would have to be different: a cache
# already in half precision, which would remove the staging of the keys and
# the values. That was measured before it was built, with a probe -- the
# same kernel with the staging deleted and the operands read from the query
# room instead, so the same count of instructions and the same matrix
# products without the room or the conversion loops. Attention read 0.99 s
# against the staged kernel's 1.65 and the kept kernel's 1.39. The estimate
# written into the entry above, that removing the staging would leave it
# still slower, was wrong by a wide margin: the staging was two fifths of
# that kernel, not a few per cent of it.
#
# So it was built properly. The device's copy of the cache is written in
# half precision -- Put_Cache converts on the way into the mapping, Reserve
# asks for two bytes an element instead of four -- and all four attention
# kernels read it as float16_t. The matrix kernel then loads its key and
# value operands straight out of the cache with no staging at all, and its
# shared memory falls from 8768 bytes a workgroup to 6656.
#
# Three alternated rounds on a 1419-token prompt, by phase:
#
#                     exact cache, query block   half cache, matrix
#   ROTATING                    0.200 s                  0.330 s
#   ATTENDING                   1.475 s                  1.031 s
#   FEEDING                     1.300 s                  1.492 s
#   accounted for               4.290 s                  4.190 s
#
# **Attention is 1.43 times faster.** The cooperative matrix does work for
# attention; the entry above was right that staging was what stopped it and
# wrong about how much staging cost.
#
# And the prompt does not move: 3.926 s against 3.919, medians of three
# alternated whole runs. Two things eat it. ROTATING gains 0.130 s, which is
# the host converting every cache row to half precision as it writes it --
# a scalar loop, and one that F16C would make nearly free. FEEDING gains
# 0.192 s reproducibly, which nothing in this change touches and is most
# likely attention's own work moving across a phase boundary, since the
# phases are host-side wall around asynchronous submissions.
#
# None of that decides it. The conformance sweep does:
#
#   sequences 28344, outside tolerance 8107, refused 0
#
# **Twenty-nine per cent of the sweep.** A half-precision cache is not a
# rounding of the operands the way the batched product's weights are; it is
# a rounding that compounds, because a key written at the first position is
# read again at every position after it. This program's tolerance is the
# tolerance every other figure here is taken against, and it is not close.
#
# That is also, in hindsight, why the session cache offers Halved as an
# option a caller asks for rather than as what it does: somebody had already
# established this and wrote it down as "lossy" in
# model_runner-llama.ads. The measurement here says how lossy.
#
# So the kernel is not kept, and it would not be kept even if the prompt had
# moved: the speed is the smaller half of the argument and the sweep is the
# larger. What would have to be different is no longer a cache -- it is a
# matrix instruction that takes binary32 operands, which this device does
# not offer, or a shape that lets the scores be computed without rounding
# what goes into them.
#
# Two things are worth keeping from it. The probe technique -- read the
# wrong operands from the right places, so the instruction count and the
# room are both right and only the answers are wrong -- priced a change
# before it was built and corrected an estimate that was badly wrong. And
# the number itself: attention through the matrix instruction is 1.43 times
# the hand-written kernel when the operands are already the precision it
# wants. That is the figure to hold against any future device whose cache
# can afford to be.

# 2026-08-27, the batched product profiled, and it is not the batched
# product. No kernel change kept; an instrument and four numbers.
#
# The item said feeding plus projecting is most of the device's remaining
# 3.9 times on a prompt, that four kernel attempts had already landed on a
# local optimum, and that this would start with a profile rather than a
# change. It did.
#
# What the budget already said, at 1419 tokens: projecting 0.746 s for 9.44
# million weights a layer, feeding 1.300 s for 34.60 million. Same kernel,
# same format, and per weight the projections run at about 790 gigaflops a
# second against feeding's 1662. A phase cannot say why, because a phase is
# four products; so `tests device-bench` grew a sweep that times one, at the
# shapes a layer actually asks for and at a batch of a hundred and
# twenty-eight.
#
#   query      2048 by 2048   0.000396 s   2714 Gflop/s    64 workgroups
#   keys        256 by 2048   0.000295 s    455 Gflop/s     8 workgroups
#   out proj   2048 by 2048   0.000529 s   2031 Gflop/s    64
#   gate, up   5632 by 2048   0.001110 s   2660 Gflop/s   176
#   down       2048 by 5632   0.000922 s   3204 Gflop/s    64
#   vocabulary 32000 by 2048  0.007440 s   2255 Gflop/s  1000
#
# **The grouped keys and values run at a sixth of everything else.** That
# shape is not a quirk of this model: grouped-query attention gives every
# modern model a projection an eighth the height of the query beside it, and
# a workgroup here takes thirty-two rows and a hundred and twenty-eight
# vectors, so two hundred and fifty-six rows against a batch of a hundred
# and twenty-eight is eight workgroups on twelve compute units.
#
# That it is the workgroup count and not the shape, said by widening the
# batch and nothing else:
#
#   256 by 2048, 128 vectors    8 workgroups    455 Gflop/s
#   256 by 2048, 256 vectors   16 workgroups    706
#   256 by 2048, 512 vectors   32 workgroups    835
#   256 by 2048, 1024 vectors  64 workgroups   1103
#
# So the fix is more workgroups at the batch a prompt actually has, which
# means a narrower vector tile. Built as two whole compilations and
# measured:
#
#                    keys    query   gate, up   vocabulary
#   128 vectors      455     2714      2660        2255
#   64 vectors       514     2461      2778        1895
#   32 vectors       575     2022      1984        1323
#
# **Twenty-six per cent on the narrow shape for twenty-five off the query
# and forty-one off the vocabulary.** Not kept, and not worth a second
# pipeline either: quadrupling the workgroups bought a quarter, so the
# workgroup count is what the batch sweep says it is and not the whole of
# what binds this shape. What else binds it is not established here.
#
# Then the number that reframes the item. Every tile product in a sequence
# dispatched twice, unchanged data, so the difference is the arithmetic and
# nothing around it:
#
#   FEEDING      1.294 s -> 1.846 s    the products are 0.552 s
#   PROJECTING   0.678 s -> 0.678 s    not this path at all
#
# **The arithmetic is forty-three per cent of the feeding phase.** The other
# fifty-seven -- three quarters of a second on a four-second prompt -- is
# what surrounds it: submissions, fences, and the activation that goes back
# to the host after every product and is uploaded again for the next, which
# model_runner-platform-device-products.ads has said in a comment since
# sequences were written: "a sequence of several still returns each result
# to the host today, and hoisting that is the next change rather than this
# one."
#
# That comment is now a measurement. The batched product is not what is left
# of the device's prompt gap; what is left is around it.
#
# Projecting not moving at all under the doubling is unexplained and is
# written down as unexplained. Either those products do not take that path,
# or the phase counter attributes their wait to the phase after them --
# the phases are host-side wall around asynchronous submissions, and this
# file has already caught one figure moving between two of them today.
#
# No source changed outside tests/src/device_bench.adb, which no figure
# group names.

# 2026-08-27, the answers nobody read: a device prompt a tenth faster for
# deleting copies.
#
# The entry above measured that the arithmetic is forty-three per cent of
# the feeding phase and that the other fifty-seven is what surrounds it, and
# quoted the comment
# model_runner-platform-device-products.ads has carried since sequences were
# written: a sequence of several still returns each result to the host.
#
# It returns all of them. Run copies every step's answer into the caller's
# target, and both callers that name several steps then read one:
#
#   Dispatch_Gated says so in a comment of its own -- "only the last of the
#   four is wanted here. The arms and the combined value are the device's
#   business and stay there" -- and they did not stay there. For a batch of
#   a hundred and twenty-eight that is the gate at 2.88 MB, the up at 2.88
#   and the combined value at 2.88, copied to the host to be stepped over,
#   against the 1.05 MB of the down projection that is read.
#
#   Attend_And_Project reads the projection and copies the blend back with
#   it, which is another 1.05 MB.
#
# Nine and a half megabytes a layer a batch. Over twenty-two layers and the
# eleven batches of a 1419-token prompt, about two and a third gigabytes of
# memcpy out of a mapping the host reads and nothing reads twice.
#
# The change is a Kept flag on a step, false where nothing on the host reads
# that step's answer, and one test in Run's download loop. The room is still
# stepped over, so what a caller indexes does not depend on what it keeps
# and no caller's arithmetic changed.
#
# Three alternated rounds, better in every one:
#
#   1419-token device prompt   3.933 s -> 3.538 s   1.11x
#   110-token device prompt    0.260 s -> 0.239 s   1.09x
#   64 generated               1.594 s -> 1.600 s   unchanged
#
# Generating does not move and cannot: one vector makes those answers a few
# kilobytes rather than a few megabytes.
#
# Bit-identical -- 1a26d24d33b8957b, 448c2ed68ec342ee -- because nothing
# about what is computed changed. This is the first change in this file that
# is pure subtraction: no kernel, no shader, no arithmetic, just three
# copies and one that were being made for nobody.
#
# By phase, and it lands where the entry above said it would:
#
#   FEEDING     1.269 s -> 1.078 s
#   ATTENDING   1.396 s -> 1.286 s
#   accounted   4.150 s -> 3.882 s
#
# The feeding phase loses 0.191 s, which is its three discarded answers, and
# attending 0.110, which is the blend. The arithmetic in both is untouched.
#
# What this says about the fifty-seven per cent: about half of it was this.
# The rest is still submissions and fences, and is still unmeasured.
#
# What was re-measured with it. The two-row table under "The device
# backend", both columns, one sitting at a load of 0.68:
#
#   6-token prompt, 12 generated       cpu 0.454 s   device 0.348 s
#     -- evaluating the prompt             0.075         0.059
#     -- generating                        0.379         0.289
#     -- processor time                    1.63          0.03
#   110-token prompt, nothing generated    0.750         0.237
#     -- processor time                    3.74          0.08
#
# And the llama.cpp comparison, llama.cpp still at 95b8e33e1 build 10597,
# three rounds. The engine's rows are 110 tokens in 0.753 s and 64 generated
# in 2.081 s on the processor, 0.237 s and 1.561 s on the device.
#
#   model_runner, processor    146.1 t/s prompt, 30.8 t/s generating
#   llama.cpp, processor       340.1 t/s prompt, 39.9 t/s generating
#   model_runner, device       464.1 t/s prompt, 41.0 t/s generating
#   llama.cpp, device         1671.2 t/s prompt, 56.3 t/s generating
#   llama.cpp at -ngl 0        764.8 t/s, which is the device answering
#
# The gap is 2.3 and 1.3 on the processor, 3.6 and 1.4 on the device. The
# device prompt row has gone 412.0, 424.7, 438.2 and now 464.1 across four
# sittings today.
#
# One measurement note. That row read 0.275 s in a first attempt at this
# sitting and 0.237, 0.234, 0.237 in three taken after the machine settled;
# 0.237 is what is recorded and the sixteen per cent between them is what a
# figure taken beside two llama-bench runs is worth.
#
# The fifteen per-format ratios and the three-way table of where the weights
# are were not re-taken: neither benchmark names a sequence, and this change
# is confined to what Run copies out of one.
#
# load: 0.68 to 1.10 for the device backend table, 0.68 to 1.50 for the
# engine's llama.cpp rows and 0.70 at the start of the llama-bench runs,
# 0.85 to 0.68 for the alternated rounds, 2026-08-27.

# 2026-08-27, the elementwise phases profiled: not dispatches, one core. Two
# changes sized and neither kept.
#
# The item said joining, normalizing and rotating are about a fifth of a
# long prompt, that elementwise work should be nearly free, and that it was
# "likely a batching-of-dispatches problem rather than a kernel one". The
# first half is right and the second is wrong.
#
# After the copies were deleted these three read 0.250, 0.195 and 0.394 s of
# a 3.882 s device prompt: twenty-two per cent, and the second largest item
# after attending. None of them is a dispatch. They are host loops in
# model_runner-llama.adb -- Join_Residual, Normalize, K.Add and
# Apply_Rotary -- run on the calling task, and a device run does not make
# them any less host work.
#
# What says so, and it is a one-line experiment:
#
#   1419-token device prompt, --threads 1   3.400 s   2.94 s of processor
#   1419-token device prompt, --threads 8   3.527 s   2.97 s of processor
#
# Eight workers change nothing, and the processor time is the same to a per
# cent. So this is **one core doing elementwise work while seven sit idle**,
# for a fifth of the run.
#
# Where the processor time goes, and a thing worth knowing on its own. A
# device prompt reports 2.98 s of processor for 3.755 s of wall. Two thirds
# of that is not the model at all: tokenizing this 1419-token prompt takes
# **2.05 s**, measured directly with `tests tokenize`, and the speed tool
# times it apart from the evaluation so it appears in no published figure.
# The user's wall clock for that run is 6.148 s, of which 3.755 is the
# prompt, 0.451 generating, 0.095 loading -- and 1.85 s is tokenizing. That
# is a larger single item than this one and nothing here has ever looked at
# it.
#
# Two changes were sized inside the kernels and neither is kept.
#
# **The dead zero-fill.** RMS_Norm opens with Target := [others => 0.0] and
# then writes every element of Target in one of two loops below, so the fill
# is a whole pass over the array thrown away -- and it is called twice a
# layer for every position, sixty-two thousand times in this prompt. Moving
# it to the refusal path is bit-exact. It is also not a win:
#
#   1419-token device prompt, normalizing   0.251 s -> 0.241 s
#   110-token processor prompt              0.748 s -> 0.759 s, worse in 3 of 3
#
# The device gains ten milliseconds and the processor loses eleven. Removing
# a write pass making a loop slower is not what anybody would predict; the
# likeliest reason is that the fill brings the lines in before the scattered
# writes below ask for them, so what looks like a wasted pass is a prefetch.
# Written down as a likely reason and not as a measurement. Four figure
# groups name model_runner-kernels.adb, so this would have cost four
# re-measurements for a quarter of a per cent in one direction and a loss in
# the other. Not kept.
#
# **The serial accumulator.** RMS_Norm sums the squares into one binary64
# accumulator left to right, which is a dependency chain the compiler cannot
# vectorize. Eight partial accumulators, summed at the end:
#
#   normalizing   0.245 s -> 0.221 s
#   joining       0.390 s -> 0.373 s
#
# Forty milliseconds on a 3.9 s prompt -- one per cent -- and it reassociates
# a sum every published digest depends on, on both backends and the
# reference path. One per cent is not worth moving every figure in this
# file. Not kept. The estimate that the chain was worth 0.18 s was wrong:
# two thousand and forty-eight floats is eight kilobytes and the loop is
# nearer memory-bound than latency-bound.
#
# What is left, named with its blocker. The three phases parallelize by
# position: every position's arithmetic is independent of every other's, so
# shares of a batch would be **bit-exact**, and Workers_CPU.Dispatch_Shares
# already exists and is already used a few lines above for attention in the
# same loop. What stops a straight substitution is Item.Post_Room: one
# scratch array of the model's width, allocated once, that Join_Residual
# writes through for every position. Shares would race on it. The change is
# scratch per share rather than per session, and it is the largest thing
# left in a device prompt after attention.
#
# No source changed.


# 2026-08-27  The attention score dot product, as an instruction
#
# The score loop is a reduction, and `### Scalar however it is written`
# above records that no arrangement of Ada makes GNAT vectorize one: the
# value blend twenty lines below it came out as mulpd and addpd because it
# is a map, and the score loop came out as mulsd and addsd in binary64 and
# mulss and addss in binary32, however it was written. This is what was
# left: a machine-code insertion, which is the mechanism the integer
# kernels already use for the same reason.
#
# **Two changes, measured separately, both kept.**
#
# 1. Model_Runner.Kernels.Head_Dot, a fused multiply-add over eight
#    binary32 pairs a turn with the eight lanes folded once at the end.
#    Blend_Exact's score nest calls it instead of accumulating in binary64.
#    Alternated rounds on the 1419-token prompt, load under 1.20 before
#    each:
#
#      round 1   13.538 s -> 11.736 s
#      round 2   14.255 s -> 11.432 s
#      round 3   13.691 s -> 11.664 s
#
#    Median 13.691 -> 11.664, fourteen and a half per cent, better in three
#    of three. Generating is level -- 2.02 s against 2.00 -- and has to be:
#    a generated token has one query row and the score loop is one dot per
#    head, not one per position.
#
# 2. The value blend in binary32. Sums was a Wide_Real_Array in all three
#    of Blend_Exact, Blend_Halved and Blend_Eighth. That loop is a map and
#    was already vectorized, so the format is the lane count and nothing
#    else: the object file goes from 34 mulpd / 32 addpd to 18 mulps / 17
#    addps and no packed binary64 at all. Seven alternated rounds, because
#    three did not clear the noise floor on their own:
#
#      11.803 -> 11.249   11.472 -> 11.345   11.549 -> 10.921
#      11.815 -> 11.269   11.553 -> 11.047   11.748 -> 11.180
#      11.728 -> 11.567
#
#    Median 11.728 -> 11.249, four per cent, better in seven of seven. Four
#    per cent is at the floor for one round and is not for seven.
#
# Both change the answers, and the sweep is the arbiter for both, as it is
# for the byte dot product: 28344 sequences, 0 outside tolerance, on each of
# the two changes separately. The sixty-four-token digest goes
# 448c2ed68ec342ee -> cf8edab322fa571f -> 1cb5fffbb21399ad; the 110-token
# prompt digest does not move at all.
#
# Where the insertion had to live. It was written into llama.adb first and
# the repository check refused it: model_runner-llama.adb interprets what a
# model file holds, so it may not `with Model_Runner.Platform` -- that is
# what keeps a container from being able to make the program read something
# else. The capability is therefore told rather than asked, exactly as
# Quantization.Use_Wide_Decoders is told: Kernels.Use_Wide_Dots, called once
# from Backend.CPU's elaboration before any model is opened. The rule found
# a real mistake rather than an inconvenience.
#
# THE SITTING. Every figure below taken 2026-08-27 back to back, each run
# waiting for the load to fall under 1.20, medians of three as the tool
# reports them.
#
#   engine, twelve tokens from the short prompt
