# 2026-08-27  The row tile at sixty-four, and the instrument's first row
#
# The device's prompt is 2.4 times behind llama.cpp and the tile product is
# where its arithmetic goes, so this looked at the tile's shape again.
#
# What suggested the change. `tests device-bench` times 16384 by 2048 at a
# batch of 128 in eight formats and they all land together:
#
#   f16 3094, bf16 3562, q8_0 4203, q6_k 4198, q5_k 3639, q4_k 4079,
#   q4_0 4181, q2_k 4290 Gflop/s
#
# Half precision decodes nothing and Q2_K has six times fewer bytes and the
# most elaborate decode of the eight. Neither the weight bytes nor the
# decode binds this, which leaves what they share. A workgroup takes
# thirty-two rows and the whole batch, so every row tile reads the batch
# again: 16384 rows is 512 row tiles and a 128-vector batch at 2048 columns
# is half a megabyte, so a quarter of a gigabyte of activation reads against
# thirty-four megabytes of weights.
#
# The change. TILE_R 32 -> 64, local_size 64 -> 128, two waves splitting the
# eight vector matrices four each, both using all sixty-four decoded rows.
# Accumulators per wave unchanged at sixteen, decode per lane unchanged at
# sixteen values, activation reads halved. Tile_Rows on the host moved with
# it; every shape TinyLlama has is still a whole number of sixty-fours.
#
#   format   thirty-two rows   sixty-four
#   f16                 3094         2142
#   bf16                3562         2536
#   q8_0                4203         2303
#   q6_k                4198         2185
#   q5_k                3639         3274
#   q4_k                4079         3358
#   q4_0                4181         3018
#   q2_k                4290         3357
#
# Worse in eight of eight, by thirty to forty-five per cent. Two sittings of
# the committed shader read within eight per cent of each other, so the loss
# is far outside what this instrument resolves. Reverted.
#
# The likeliest reason, written down as a reason and not as a measurement:
# with one wave to a workgroup a barrier() has nobody to wait for and is
# very nearly free; with two it is a real wait, twice every thirty-two
# columns, and neither wave can begin its arithmetic until the other has
# finished its half of the decode. Sixty-four such waits a row tile. What it
# bought was a quarter of a gigabyte of reads the eight figures above say it
# was not paying much for.
#
# Fifth attempt at this tile's shape and the fifth revert: KCH at 64 and at
# 128, the accumulators two against eight, TILE_V at 64 and at 32, and now
# TILE_R at 64.
#
# AND THE INSTRUMENT. While measuring the above, "query" and "out proj" in
# the shapes table turned out to be the same shape -- 2048 by 2048 at 128 --
# reading thirty-three per cent apart, in three sittings, in the same
# direction. So the table was given a control: the first row's shape again,
# measured last. One run, four readings of that one shape:
#
#   query, first in the table   3035 Gflop/s
#   out proj                    2173
#   rows 2048                   2178
#   query last, the control     2201
#
# The three that are not first agree within one and a half per cent and the
# first is forty per cent above them. The first shape a sitting measures
# reads high whichever shape it is: the sweep before it works the device far
# more lightly and it enters this one at a boost clock it cannot hold. The
# 2714 published for "query" under "### The batched product, profiled" is
# the instrument; about 2180 is the figure. The control row stays in
# tests/src/device_bench.adb.
#
# The sub-table of vector-tile widths under that section is undisturbed:
# every column there was taken the same way in its own build, so a
# comparison across a row is between two equally warm first readings.
#
# Only tests/src/device_bench.adb changed, and it is outside every figure
# group.

# 2026-08-27  The blend in registers, and the gate that was waiting for arithmetic
#
# TWO CHANGES. The first is the one the item was for; the second is the
# instrument, and it is the one that will save the most time.
#
# 1. Kernels.Blend_Run. model_runner-llama.o held no packed fused
#    multiply-add at all -- eighteen mulps and seventeen addps -- this being
#    compiled for baseline x86-64. But the fusing was the smaller half. The
#    blend is Sums (c) += Score * Values (c) over a run of components and a
#    span of positions; -O3 vectorizes the inner loop and cannot keep Sums
#    in registers across the positions, because Sums is an array the loop
#    writes and Values may alias it. So a position paid a load and a store
#    of the whole run as well as its arithmetic: forty instructions where
#    nine would do.
#
#    Eight accumulators loaded once, a position's score broadcast into eight
#    lanes, eight vfmadd231ps over the values where they lie, stored once at
#    the end. Seven alternated rounds on the 1419-token prompt:
#
#      11.459 -> 10.704   11.297 -> 10.534   11.372 -> 10.922
#      11.131 -> 10.575   11.494 -> 10.723   11.095 -> 10.797
#      11.446 -> 11.076
#
#    Median 11.372 -> 10.723, five and three quarter per cent, better in
#    seven of seven. The answers move because the fused form rounds a
#    product once where the portable one rounds it twice -- the insertion is
#    the more accurate -- and the sweep is the arbiter: 28344 sequences,
#    nothing outside tolerance. Use_Wide_Dots is now Use_Wide_Lanes.
#
# 2. THE LOAD GATE. Host_Load.Publishable asked the one-minute load average
#    whether a figure was worth publishing. A load average lags in both
#    directions and this sitting was spent watching it do both.
#
#    Held out: every figure here is taken in a sitting of runs back to back,
#    so the number the gate read was nearly always the previous run's own
#    load decaying. The gate was waiting for arithmetic rather than for the
#    machine. The six-group re-measure before this one spent most of half an
#    hour that way, and the shell scripts made it worse by waiting for 1.20
#    where the gate only wanted 1.50.
#
#    Let in: with eight spinners started on this machine the average was
#    still 1.06 three seconds later, and the gate admitted the run. That is
#    the half that mattered, and nothing here had noticed it.
#
#    So the question goes to the processors: /proc/stat sampled over a fifth
#    of a second, busy processors counted against the same Too_Busy of 1.5 --
#    the same quantity over a different window. A host that keeps no such
#    times has only the average and is left where it was. Verified three
#    ways: quiet machine at average 1.16 admitted; eight spinners refused;
#    and a machine whose spinners had just stopped, average 1.86, admitted
#    and measured in 1.1 s total, where the old gate refused it outright.
#
#    The sitting below then ran seventeen speed runs, three llama-bench runs
#    and two benchmarks back to back with no waiting at all and no refusals.
#
# A NOTE ON THE LOAD FIGURES BELOW, which changes how to read them. The gate
# no longer waits for the average to fall, so the average printed beside a
# run is often the previous runs' own load still decaying: several below
# read over 2 on a machine that had nothing on it but this program. They
# overstate the disturbance rather than understate it, which is the safe
# direction, but they are no longer comparable with the load figures in
# entries above this one. What admitted every run below was the
# busy-processor sample, not the number printed.
#
# THE SITTING. 2026-08-27, back to back, medians of three as the tool
# reports them.
#
#   engine, twelve tokens from the short prompt
#     0.453 s wall -- 0.079 evaluating, 0.374 generating, 1.64 s processor
#     the same at --arith f32: 1.322 s for 8.83 s of processor
#     fourteen threads: 0.475 s for 1.92 s, against 0.453 and 1.64 at seven
#
#   reference, four tokens
#     cpu       0.065 s prompt, 0.122 s generating
#     reference 5.691 s prompt, 3.775 s generating
#     fifty times in total, eighty-eight on the prompt, thirty-one on the
#     generation; seventeen times at --arith f32; and serial on synthetic
#     tensors 2.34x q8_0, 2.35x q4_k, 3.11x f32
#
#   device, the two-row table

#     110 tokens: cpu 0.661 s for 3.47 s of processor, device 0.166 s for

#
#   drafting, twelve tokens each
#     eight-bit 0.432 s, two-bit 1.588 s, drafted 3.547 s with 24 proposed
#     and 7 accepted -- 36 ms and 132 ms a token, 63 ms to check five
#     positions, and a threshold of about 4 ms a token for a draft to pay.
#     The drafted run prints 33f48397f89839f6, which is what the undrafted
#     one prints: the guarantee showing up in a published figure.
#
#   scaling, taskset -c 0-7 tests benchmark --wait 10
#     q8_0 one vector   1 share 2575 Me/s, 7 shares 13087, 8 shares 13559
#     q4_k one vector   1 share 2579,      7 shares 13119, 8 shares 14294
#     q8_0 thirty-two   1 share 4791,      7 shares 24346, 8 shares 26309
#     q4_k thirty-two   1 share 4767,      7 shares 24844, 8 shares 26174
#     unpinned: q8_0 one vector 12921 at seven and 13564 at eight, batched
#     22817 and 26379
#
#   outside, against llama.cpp 95b8e33e1
#     model_runner processor  110 in 0.660 s, 64 in 2.003 s
#     model_runner device     110 in 0.166 s, 64 in 1.562 s
#     llama-bench --device none   pp110 388.96, tg64 40.42
#     llama-bench -ngl 99         pp110 1644.94, tg64 56.44
#     llama-bench -ngl 0          pp110 781.93, tg64 40.16
#
# AND A CORRECTION TO THE ENTRY ABOVE. The llama.cpp comparison table was
# restamped in that sitting and the edit that wrote it failed partway and
# was never applied, while the sentences either side of it were. So one
# commit carried a table reading 141.2 and 352.0 under prose quoting 166.7
# and 389.0. The fingerprint check did not catch it and cannot: it asks
# whether a group was re-measured, not whether every number in it moved.
# The table is right now and the README says what happened.
#
# What moved and what did not. The processor's 110-token prompt reads 166.7
# tokens a second against 168.7, which is level: the blend is quadratic in
# the context and a 110-token prompt holds a hundred and sixty times less
# attention than the 1419-token one it was measured on. The worker pair
# changed sign again, five per cent worse for seventeen per cent more, which
# is the ninth reading and the fourth change of sign.

# 2026-08-27, later.  A hot part, and what the gate had been hiding
#
# The gate above stopped waiting for a load average to decay, and the wait
# it removed had been doing a second job nobody had noticed: letting the
# part cool. The first sitting under the new gate ran two benchmarks back to
# back and the second read nine per cent below the first on the same code.
#
# Measured rather than assumed, which took two goes. The first attempt
# waited four minutes because four minutes sounded right; that is the same
# mistake as the load average, one lagging proxy swapped for a guess. The
# second reads the part instead -- k10temp, /sys/class/hwmon -- and waits
# until two samples twenty seconds apart are within a degree.
#
# The same benchmark, settled against straight-after:
#
#                      settled, 47 C    straight after, 81 C
#   q8_0 Row_Dot                2739                    2399
#   q4_k Row_Dot                2688                    2400
#   one share                   2602                    2380
#   eight shares               14030                   13681
#
# The serial rows lose a tenth and the eight-share row loses two per cent.
# That is the whole finding: a hot part gives up single-core boost first,
# and the all-core figure is already at the sustained clock with nothing
# left to give up. Every figure in "### Kernels" is a serial rate and every
# figure in the scaling section is a serial rate or a ratio against one, so
# all of them are boost-sensitive and none of them said so until now.
#
# The pair below was taken settled: pinned started at 47 C and ended at 89,
# unpinned started at 50 and ended at 89.
#
#   pinned
#     q8_0 one vector   1 share 2602 Me/s, 7 shares 12955, 8 shares 14030
#     q4_k one vector   1 share 2640,      7 shares 12907, 8 shares 14176
#     q8_0 thirty-two   1 share 4759,      7 shares 24642, 8 shares 26184
#     q4_k thirty-two   1 share 4845,      7 shares 24638, 8 shares 26841
#     serial against the reference: 2.34x q8_0, 2.35x q4_k, 3.13x f32
#   unpinned
#     q8_0 one vector   7 shares 12817, 8 shares 13325
#     q8_0 thirty-two   7 shares 24445, 8 shares 26039
#
#   row dot product, ns an element: f32 0.25, q4_0 0.30, bf16 0.31, q8_0
#   0.37, q4_k 0.37, q6_k 0.38, q5_k 0.40, q3_k 0.47, q4_1 0.48, iq4xs 0.49,
#   q5_0 0.52, f16 0.55, q5_1 0.56, iq4nl 0.62, q2_k 0.69
#
# What is not done, and is the obvious next thing: the tools print the load
# and the processor seconds beside a figure and do not print the
# temperature, so a reader still cannot tell a settled figure from a hot one
# without being told in prose. Host_Load reads /proc and is where that
# belongs, beside Now and Processor_Seconds, for the reason written over
# both of them.

# 2026-08-27, later still.  The strip kernel: two wrong guesses and a null
#
# The strip kernel is fifty-five per cent of a processor prompt, aggregated
# over eight threads on the 1419-token prompt. Nothing here is kept.
#
# GUESS ONE, the register width. The insertion issues 256-bit vpdpbusd on a
# processor reporting avx512_vnni, and the plan said this needed a third
# capability and a fourth compilation. It needs neither and it is not
# available anyway:
#
#   Platform.Instructions.Byte_Products already requires avx512f, avx512bw,
#   avx512vl and avx512_vnni, and integers-deep.ads is already compiled
#   -march=x86-64-v4. Nothing was being withheld.
#
#   A register holds exactly one Q8_0 block -- 32 bytes of quants -- and
#   blocks are 34 bytes apart, so a 64-byte load straddles the next block's
#   two-byte scale. Filling a zmm with two rows instead breaks the {1to8}
#   scale broadcast: the halves want different scales, and giving them
#   different scales means a scale table eight times the size, in a loop the
#   old profile already put at a third of the kernel. The width is pinned to
#   the block and not to the instruction set.
#
# GUESS TWO, where the time is. Profiled by instruction, shares of the
# symbol:
#
#   vpxor (twelve)                              13.96 %
#   vpdpbusd (eight)                            12.81 %
#   vfmadd231ps and vcvtdq2ps                   15.11 %
#   vunpcklps valignd vmovlhps vshufps
#     vextractf32x4                             ~25 %
#   vaddps and vmovaps                           7.22 %
#
# The zeroing vpxor before each byte dot product costs more than the dot
# product. And a quarter of the symbol is shuffling -- which was guessed to
# be the eight-lane reduction that finishes a row, and is not: addr2line
# puts every hot shuffle in the loop that builds the scale table, where a
# four-turn inner loop writes Scaling and read-modify-writes Undo, an array
# -O3 will not keep in registers.
#
# THE CHANGE AND THE NULL. Row outside block, the row's four corrections in
# a local across the block loop, Undo written once at the end. Same values,
# same order -- a correction still sums over ascending blocks -- so bit for
# bit, and the digest says so. The object file's shuffle count fell from 299
# to 236 with three of the four kernels untouched.
#
#   round 1   11.111 -> 10.583
#   round 2   11.209 -> 10.816
#   round 3   10.688 -> 11.125
#   round 4   10.998 -> 11.059
#   round 5   10.877 -> 10.839
#
# Median 10.998 -> 10.839, better in three of five, digest 1a26d24d33b8957b
# either way. Level. Reverted.
#
# WHAT IT MEANS. Removing half the shuffles from a block holding a quarter
# of the samples moved the prompt by one per cent, so they were never on the
# critical path: they issue in the shadow of the byte-product loop. A
# profile says where instructions are and this file has now recorded several
# occasions where that is not where the time is -- the narrow projection,
# the phase counters, and now this.
#
# The kernel is not short of instruction slots. What it is short of is a
# question this did not ask: the four-bit file is slower than the eight-bit
# one on the same prompt (0.702 s against 0.661 for 110 tokens), so it is
# not the weight stream either, and the next measurement is the dependent
# chain inside the loop rather than anything around it.
#
# No source changed.

# 2026-08-27, last.  What a doubling cannot say, and what the bytes say
#
# The plan was the device's activation round trip, on the comment in
# model_runner-platform-device-products.ads saying a sequence of several
# still returns each result to the host. That comment is stale where it
# matters: Backend.Device's batched feed-forward already sends gate and up
# with Kept => False, chains the combining step, and returns only the down
# projection. Nine megabytes a layer at a batch of 128 already stay put.
#
# So the question became where a device prompt's time is. Instrument: double
# one kind of dispatch, leave everything else identical, measure the whole
# 1419-token device prompt. Three alternated rounds each, medians:
#
#   every tile product   2.273 -> 2.499   +0.226 s   10 %
#   attention            2.293 -> 2.333   +0.040 s    1.7 %
#   every row product    2.259 -> 2.281   +0.022 s    1.0 %
#
# And linear: four tile passes read 3.013 against 2.314, which is 0.233 s an
# extra pass against the pair's 0.226, within three per cent.
#
# Twelve per cent of arithmetic against the eighty per cent that "### The
# floor a device prompt cannot go below" measured by emptying every
# dispatch. Both are careful and they cannot both be the share.
#
# THE DOUBLING IS A LOWER BOUND, NOT A SHARE. A second dispatch of the same
# product goes into the same command buffer with no barrier after the first,
# so the device overlaps them, and it reads weights and activations the
# first pass has just pulled into cache. Both make the second pass cheaper
# than the first. The floor section on that page already says this in one
# sentence -- "doubling it adds only 0.405 rather than 0.540, because two
# dispatches in one command buffer pipeline better than one does" -- and it
# was walked into anyway, which is why it is now written twice.
#
# Emptying a dispatch is the sounder of the two, because it removes the work
# without leaving a warmed cache behind it. The arithmetic is most of a
# device prompt and the surround is a fifth, as that section said.
#
# WHAT IS NEW, and it stands on its own. If arithmetic binds the device
# prompt, what about it binds? The same prompt, the same code, three weight
# sizes:
#
#   Q8_0    1171 MB   2.292 s, 2.305 s
#   Q4_K_M   638 MB   2.342 s, 2.414 s
#   Q2_K     461 MB   2.465 s, 2.430 s
#
# Two and a half times fewer bytes is slower, not faster, and the order is
# the same in both rounds. The device prompt is not bound by weight traffic
# at any of these sizes -- which agrees with what tests device-bench says
# about the tile in isolation (every format within a few per cent at six
# times the byte range) and now says about a whole run as well. The cheaper
# formats do more work per byte and the table is that cost showing through.
#
# Three scratch builds, all discarded. No source changed.

# 2026-08-28  The exponential, as eight lanes instead of a call
#
# The softmax called the library's exponential once an element, in binary64,
# and a processor prompt is heads times positions times positions of them. A
# profile of the 1419-token prompt put __ieee754_exp_fma at 2.89 per cent,
# exp at 2.01, Numerics.Exp at 1.24 and Kernels.Softmax at 2.38 -- eight and
# a half per cent between them.
#
# A call cannot be vectorized and this shape can: it is a map, every element
# independent of every other, which is the one thing -O3 does without being
# asked. Kernels.Exponentiate replaces the call with the standard
# decomposition -- e^x as two raised to x over the logarithm of two, the
# whole part built as an exponent field and the fraction by a degree five
# polynomial -- in binary32.
#
# TWO THINGS STOPPED IT VECTORIZING and both had to go, which is the part
# worth keeping:
#
#   'Truncation is a call into System.Fat_Flt.Attr_Float, not an
#   instruction. Adding three halves of two to the twenty-third and taking
#   it away again rounds to the nearest whole number in two additions.
#
#   Integer (Whole) carried an overflow check, which is a branch. The floor
#   at eighty-seven bounds the value, so the check is suppressed in the body
#   with the reason written there.
#
# With both gone the object file holds 18 addps and 16 mulps where it held
# mulss and addss and two calls. Four lanes rather than eight, because the
# baseline target is x86-64 and that is what an xmm holds.
#
#   round 1   10.854 -> 10.484
#   round 2   10.770 -> 10.593
#   round 3   10.611 -> 10.392
#   round 4   11.129 -> 10.316
#   round 5   11.029 -> 10.480
#
# Median 10.854 -> 10.480, three and a half per cent, better in five of
# five. The profile said eight and a half and the clock says three and a
# half, which is the same lesson this file keeps recording: samples are not
# seconds.
#
# The answers move -- a degree five polynomial in binary32 against the
# library's binary64 -- and the sweep is the arbiter: 28344 sequences,
# nothing outside tolerance. The sixty-four token digest goes
# 3740ed87be385f2d -> cf8edab322fa571f. A test holds the two against each
# other to a few parts in a million over the range a softmax hands it, and
# names the floor as the case that matters: below it the exponent would wrap
# rather than saturate, and a score far behind the leader would come back
# ahead of it.
#
# THE SITTING. 2026-08-28, back to back, medians of three.
#
#   engine, twelve tokens: 0.458 s wall -- 0.075 evaluating, 0.380
#     generating, 1.64 s processor; at --arith f32 1.335 s for 8.71 s;
#     fourteen threads 0.466 s for 1.88 s against 0.458 and 1.64 at seven
#
#   reference, four tokens: cpu 0.069 + 0.121, reference 5.698 + 3.798 --
#     fifty times in total, eighty-three on the prompt, thirty-one on the
#     generation, sixteen times at --arith f32
#
#   device: six tokens twelve generated, cpu 0.474 s (0.082 + 0.391, 1.68 s
#     processor) against device 0.349 s (0.057 + 0.291, 0.03 s); 110 tokens
#     cpu 0.659 s for 3.49 s of processor against device 0.157 s for 0.17 s
#
#   drafting: eight-bit 0.436 s, two-bit 1.550 s, drafted 3.397 s with 24
#     proposed and 7 accepted -- 36 ms and 129 ms a token, 50 ms to check
#     five positions, threshold about 7 ms a token
#
#   outside: model_runner 110 in 0.657 s and 64 in 2.003 s on the processor,
#     0.157 s and 1.563 s on the device; llama-bench --device none pp110
#     393.01 tg64 40.37, -ngl 99 pp110 1616.22 tg64 56.46, -ngl 0 pp110
#     783.92 tg64 40.44
#
# The processor's prompt reads 167.4 tokens a second against 166.7, which is
# level: this change is quadratic in the context like the two before it, and
# a 110-token prompt holds a hundred and sixty times less attention than the
# 1419-token one it was measured on. Where it shows is the long prompt, and
# there it is three and a half per cent.
#
# AND A SECOND CORRECTION OF THE SAME KIND AS THE LAST. The engine figures
# were found still reading 0.470 s and 1.68 s from two sittings before: the
# restamp that should have moved them was in a script that failed partway
# and never wrote, exactly as the llama.cpp table's did. Both are right now,
# and every edit in this sitting was applied and verified one at a time
# rather than in a batch that can abort halfway.

# 2026-08-28, later.  What the strip kernel is short of
#
# The entry before last said the strip kernel is not short of instruction
# slots and that what it is short of is a different measurement. Here it is,
# and then an attempt to act on it that did not survive. Nothing kept.
#
# THE COUNTERS, on the 1419-token processor prompt:
#
#   instructions              710,328,910,301
#   cycles                    235,724,212,048     IPC 3.01
#   stalled-cycles-frontend     3,247,003,680     1.4 % of cycles
#   cache-misses                2,826,595,918     0.4 % of instructions
#
# The machine is not stalling. That is the explanation of the null in the
# entry before last: at three instructions a cycle with slack to spare, the
# shuffles removed there were filling slots something else had left idle.
#
# THE ABLATION. One extra vpdpbusd into the chain of eight -- the same
# operand into the same accumulator, so the answers are wrong and the shape
# is exactly right. Alternated:
#
#   eight dot products   224,715,403,239 cycles
#   nine dot products    222,091,588,782
#   eight again          221,821,034,125
#
# An eighth more byte products costs no measurable cycles: less than the
# spread between two identical runs. Worth noting how nearly this was
# missed -- the wall clock read better in two of four rounds and worse in
# two, which is drift, and only the cycle counter resolved it.
#
# So the loop has slack and the binder is latency, not throughput: each
# accumulator's chain is vpxor, vpdpbusd, vcvtdq2ps, vfmadd231ps -- four
# deep -- and eight of them run independently.
#
# THE REMEDY, AND IT LOST. Two Q8_0 blocks a turn with sixteen accumulators
# rather than eight, so the odd and even blocks stay independent to the
# reduction, with the cursors advancing 68 and 64 instead of 34 and 32 --
# which halves the loop's own overhead as well. Landed doubled, the
# reduction summing both accumulators, and an odd block count sent to the
# fallback.
#
#   round 1   10.426 -> 10.872
#   round 2   10.538 -> 10.943
#   round 3   10.516 -> 10.802
#   round 4   10.686 -> 10.856
#   round 5   10.594 -> 10.626
#
# Median 10.538 -> 10.856, three per cent worse, worse in five of five.
# More independent chains and less loop overhead, and slower. Reverted.
#
# The likeliest reason, written down as a reason and not as a measurement:
# the loop body went from about thirty-nine instructions to about
# seventy-eight, and something that was holding the shorter one stops
# holding the longer. The device side of this file has met that shape --
# eight formats compiled but unreachable cost the six reachable ones
# twenty-one per cent.
#
# So: the strip kernel has issue slack and cannot be given more independent
# work without losing more than the slack is worth. What is left to try is a
# shorter chain rather than more of them, and nothing here says whether one
# exists.
#
# No source changed.

# 2026-08-28, later.  The fold, paid once for eight scores instead of eight
#
# After the exponential landed, a fresh profile of the 1419-token prompt put
# Kernels.Head_Dot at twelve per cent -- the second largest symbol in the
# program, and one written three commits earlier.
#
#   rows_by_strips  60.67 %      blend_run        4.23 %
#   head_dot        12.00 %      softmax          2.69 %
#   blend_exact      5.27 %      exponentiate     1.64 %
#
# THE PREMISE, CHECKED BEFORE ANYTHING WAS WRITTEN. A dot product is eight
# fused multiply-adds and then a fold -- vextractf128, vaddps, two vhaddps,
# vmovss -- which is a serial chain of about twenty cycles behind arithmetic
# worth eight. Removing the fold entirely gives wrong answers and says what
# it costs:
#
#   with the fold     10.501 s      without    10.076 s
#
# Four per cent of the whole prompt, faster in five of five. So the fold is
# worth about that much to stop paying once a score.
#
# THE CHANGE. Kernels.Head_Scores computes eight scores at once: eight
# accumulators, one a key, and the whole sixty-four-wide query head held in
# eight more registers across all of them. A key then costs eight fused
# multiply-adds reading it where it lies, and the eight folds become one
# twelve-instruction reduction -- vhaddps in pairs, then pairs of those,
# then the two halves added -- with two dependent chains instead of eight.
# The query is loaded once a run rather than once a score.
#
# WHERE IT IS CALLED FROM, which is the part that took thought. Neither
# obvious order works. Position outside head reads the key cache once, which
# is what an earlier entry here won, but asks for one score at a time. Head
# outside position lets eight keys share a fold and walks the whole cache
# again for every head. Eight positions at a time has both: the eight key
# rows are eight kilobytes for this architecture and stay in the nearest
# cache while all thirty-two heads read them.
#
#   round 1   10.286 -> 9.326
#   round 2   10.098 -> 9.383
#   round 3   10.528 -> 9.383
#   round 4   10.255 -> 9.638
#   round 5   10.528 -> 9.607
#
# Median 10.286 -> 9.383, eight and eight tenths per cent, better in five of
# five. The largest single change in this file since the batched product.
#
# Not bit for bit with Head_Dot and it cannot be: a lane's products are
# summed in the same order and the eight lanes are folded in a different
# one. The sweep is the arbiter as before -- 28344 sequences, nothing
# outside tolerance -- and a test compares every score of a run against the
# one Head_Dot gives for the same key, which catches a permutation of the
# fold where a total would not.
#
# THE SITTING. 2026-08-28.
#
#   engine, twelve tokens: 0.455 s wall -- 0.078 evaluating, 0.377
#     generating, 1.65 s processor; --arith f32 1.304 s for 8.62 s;
#     fourteen threads 0.460 s for 1.88 s against 0.455 and 1.65 at seven
#
#   reference, four tokens: cpu 0.067 + 0.125, reference 5.671 + 3.783 --
#     forty-nine times in total, eighty-five on the prompt
#
#   device: six tokens twelve generated, cpu 0.445 s (0.071 + 0.370, 1.61 s
#     processor) against device 0.356 s (0.064 + 0.291, 0.04 s); 110 tokens
#     cpu 0.626 s for 3.44 s against device 0.140 s for 0.16 s
#
#   drafting: eight-bit 0.440 s, two-bit 1.534 s, drafted 3.589 s with 24
#     proposed and 9 accepted -- 37 ms and 128 ms a token, 87 ms to check
#     five positions. At that check cost no draft pays at all, which is the
#     eighth reading of a threshold that has read 17, 10, 6, 9, 6, 4 and 7
#     ms a token before this. The conclusion has never changed and the
#     number never settles, for the reason that section gives.
#
#   outside: model_runner 110 in 0.624 s and 64 in 2.017 s on the processor,
#     0.140 s and 1.568 s on the device; llama-bench --device none pp110
#     391.58 tg64 40.35, -ngl 99 pp110 1658.34 tg64 56.44, -ngl 0 pp110
#     762.81 tg64 40.33
#
# The processor's prompt reads 176.3 tokens a second against 167.4, and the
# gap to llama.cpp on a prompt goes 2.35 to 2.22. Unlike the three changes
# before it this one does show on the 110-token prompt as well, because a
# fold is paid once a score whatever the context length is.

# 2026-08-28, last.  The serial half
#
# The plan was a shorter dependent chain in the strip kernel. It does not
# survive: the chain is per block because the scale is per block, and a
# scale every thirty-two quants is what Q8_0 is -- sharing one across
# blocks means requantizing, which is a different format and not a
# different kernel. The same wall the 512-bit idea hit. And the ablation in
# the entry above already argues the chain is not binding, since lengthening
# it by a whole extra vpdpbusd cost no measurable cycles.
#
# So the question became one none of the four attempts on that kernel had
# asked: how much of a prompt can seven workers reach at all. The same
# 1419-token prompt at one, two, four and seven:
#
#   workers   wall        processor   speedup   efficiency
#   1         34.606 s    35.58 s     1.00x     100 %
#   2         16.860 s    45.45 s     2.05x     103 %
#   4         12.249 s    50.77 s     2.83x      71 %
#   7          9.540 s    53.12 s     3.63x      52 %
#
# Amdahl fitted to the four and seven worker points gives a serial fraction
# of fifteen per cent and predicts 3.63x at seven, which is what it reaches.
# The prompt is at its ceiling and the wall clock is fully explained by the
# part that does not share.
#
# THAT PART IS THE LARGER HALF. Fifteen per cent of the one-worker time is
# 5.19 s and the whole seven-worker prompt is 9.54 s, so fifty-four per cent
# of it is one core working alone and forty-six is seven cores together.
# Halving the strip kernel -- which four changes have failed to do -- would
# take 9.54 s to about 7.4. Removing the serial part would take it to 4.35.
#
# WHY FOUR CHANGES WENT TO THE WRONG PLACE. perf reports where instructions
# are, summed over eight threads. By that measure the strip kernel is
# sixty-one per cent and the serial loops are a few per cent each. The
# serial part is one thread's worth of samples and more than half of the
# clock, and no profile taken this way can say so. That is the fourth
# instrument this file has had to correct, after the phase counters, the
# load average, and the doubling.
#
# The two-worker row is superlinear -- 2.05x on two workers, three seconds
# faster than the model -- which is a second core's share of cache. The fit
# is to four and seven for that reason, and fifteen per cent should be read
# as the number explaining those two rather than a constant of the program.
#
# A NOTE ON HOW NEARLY THIS WAS SPOILED. The first attempt at this sweep ran
# beside two `tests check` runs and every one of its twelve runs was refused
# by the gate rewritten earlier today. That is the gate working: it declined
# to publish a figure taken on a machine this session was itself keeping
# busy, and the numbers above were taken again with nothing else running.
#
# No source changed.

# 2026-08-28  The gate's activation, and two mistakes about the instrument
#
# THE MEASUREMENT THAT NAMED IT. The entry above put fifteen per cent of a
# prompt in a part seven workers cannot reach. Profiling by thread says
# which part: the main thread is busy 9.46 s of a 9.54 s prompt and each
# worker 6.24 s, and outside the strip kernel the main thread carries 8.7
# per cent of all samples against a worker's 3.2. Named, those are
#
#   quantize_vectors                     1.54 %   main thread only
#   SiLU and the exponentials it calls   ~2.0 %   main thread only
#   Multiply, mat_mul_range_packed       0.63 %
#   Apply_Rotary, RMS_Norm, Add          0.25 %
#
# On one thread, so their cost is paid whole on the clock rather than
# divided among eight.
#
# THE FIRST REMEDY WAS WRONG AND ITS FAILURE FOUND THE RIGHT ONE. If the
# submitting task carries more than a worker, give it a smaller share:
# halves rather than a whole, the rest spread over the workers. Measured,
# that is three per cent worse in five of five. The reason is that the
# serial work happens *between* dispatches and not inside one, so shrinking
# the submitter's share overlaps it with nothing and simply gives the
# workers more to do. Reverted.
#
# WHAT WAS KEPT. SiLU is Value / (1 + exp (-Value)) in binary64 with a
# library call an element. It now goes through the same polynomial the
# softmax got earlier today, hoisted into a body-local Raised so there is
# one copy of the constants, inline and with the checks suppressed inside
# it -- putting the suppression in the callers instead left an overflow
# check and a call in the loop and cost both of them their vectorization,
# which the object file caught before any measurement did. Clamped at both
# ends rather than one, because SiLU's argument is unbounded where a
# softmax's is not.
#
#   long prompt    9.384 -> 9.057   three and a half per cent, 5 of 5
#   110 tokens     0.843 -> 0.810   three and nine tenths per cent, 4 of 4
#
# The sweep is the arbiter: 28344 sequences, nothing outside tolerance.
#
# AND TWO MISTAKES ABOUT THE INSTRUMENT, both nearly published.
#
# The 110-token prompt read 0.84 s in the alternation above and 0.62 in the
# sitting before it, and the conclusion drawn was that removing the load
# average wait from the gate had removed an accidental cool-down. It had
# not. Two copies of the sitting script were running at once -- one left
# from an earlier launch -- and the second was competing with the first.
# The part sat at 95 C while they ran and fell to 63 within twenty-five
# seconds of killing them. Contention, not temperature.
#
# That is the third measurement this session spoiled by running something
# beside it, and the gate caught two of the three by refusing.
#
# The other: this file has a command for running the repository checks
# alone -- `tests check --repository` -- which was there the whole time and
# was not read. Five minutes of conformance was run after every restamp for
# no reason. It is in the usage line.
#
# THE SITTING, on a part that had stopped cooling: it settled at 53 C
# before the first run, against the 95 C of the contended one, and nothing
# was refused.
#
#   engine, twelve tokens: 0.425 s wall -- 0.067 evaluating, 0.362
#     generating, 1.59 s processor; --arith f32 1.136 s for 8.34 s;
#     fourteen threads 0.452 s for 1.89 s against 0.425 and 1.59 at seven
#
#   reference, four tokens: cpu 0.063 + 0.122, reference 5.726 + 3.840 --
#     fifty-two times in total, ninety-one on the prompt
#
#   device: six tokens twelve generated, cpu 0.439 s (0.073 + 0.367, 1.62 s
#     processor) against device 0.339 s (0.057 + 0.281, 0.02 s); 110 tokens
#     cpu 0.601 s for 3.37 s against device 0.155 s for 0.17 s
#
#   drafting: eight-bit 0.449 s, two-bit 1.616 s, drafted 3.456 s with 24
#     proposed and 7 accepted -- 37 ms and 135 ms a token, 36 ms to check
#     five positions, threshold about 11 ms a token
#
#   outside: model_runner 110 in 0.599 s and 64 in 1.965 s on the
#     processor, 0.155 s and 1.546 s on the device; llama-bench --device
#     none pp110 383.17 tg64 40.20, -ngl 99 pp110 1652.78 tg64 56.44,
#     -ngl 0 pp110 758.85 tg64 40.30
#
# The processor's prompt reads 183.6 tokens a second and the gap to
# llama.cpp on a prompt is 2.09 times, from 2.5 when the day began.

# 2026-08-28  The packing, shared
#
# quantize_vectors was the largest of the main-thread-only items the entry
# above named: 1.54 per cent of the program's samples and all of them on
# one thread of eight, which is about nine per cent of the clock.
#
# CHECKED FIRST, because the last two wins came from checking and the loss
# came from not. The loop is already vectorized -- 16 cvttps2dq, 16 addps,
# 8 mulps -- and its two calls are cold check handlers rather than anything
# per element. So there were no lanes to win and the lever was parallelism.
#
# WHY IT IS ITS OWN DISPATCH. Packing runs before the workers are woken and
# cannot be folded into the product's shares: that job is cut by rows of
# the weight matrix and every worker needs all of the activation, so none
# can start until the whole of it is packed. But its own loop is over
# blocks, and a block is independent of every other -- its own scale, its
# own bytes, its own total. So Quantize_Blocks takes a range of them and
# Prepare_Packed dispatches shares of the block count before posting the
# product.
#
# The bound is 256 blocks. A wake and a barrier are not free and a run of a
# few blocks is packed faster than a pool can be told about it; a generated
# token is one vector and is the case the bound is really keeping out.
#
#   round 1   9.221 -> 8.478      round 4   8.911 -> 8.350
#   round 2   8.506 -> 8.258      round 5   8.947 -> 8.401
#   round 3   8.546 -> 8.139
#
# Median 8.911 -> 8.350, six and three tenths per cent, better in five of
# five. Bit for bit -- the digest is cbf29ce484222325 either way -- because
# splitting independent blocks between tasks cannot change what any of them
# writes. A test asserts exactly that, cutting a run into four uneven
# pieces including an empty one and requiring the bytes, scales and totals
# to equal the whole run's.
#
# THE SITTING, on a part that settled at 53 C, nothing refused.
#
#   engine, twelve tokens: 0.423 s wall -- 0.062 evaluating, 0.361
#     generating, 1.59 s processor; --arith f32 1.193 s for 8.37 s;
#     fourteen threads 0.463 s for 1.89 s against 0.423 and 1.59 at seven
#
#   reference, four tokens: cpu 0.062 + 0.124, reference 5.662 + 3.763 --
#     fifty-one times in total, ninety-one on the prompt; serial on
#     synthetic tensors 2.34x q8_0, 2.34x q4_k, 3.11x f32
#
#   device: six tokens twelve generated, cpu 0.446 s (0.069 + 0.373, 1.61 s
#     processor) against device 0.340 s (0.056 + 0.283, 0.03 s); 110 tokens
#     cpu 0.534 s for 3.38 s against device 0.170 s for 0.17 s
#
#   drafting: eight-bit 0.435 s, two-bit 1.626 s, drafted 3.305 s with 24
#     proposed and 7 accepted -- 36 ms and 136 ms a token, 7 ms to check
#     five positions, threshold about 18 ms a token. Tenth reading of a
#     number that has never settled and a conclusion that has never moved.
#
#   scaling, pinned: q8_0 one vector 2566 at one share, 13238 at seven,
#     14338 at eight; thirty-two per pass 4803, 24404, 26841. Unpinned
#     12722 and 13672 with one vector, 24456 and 26605 batched.
#
#   kernels, row dot product in ns an element: f32 0.25, q4_0 0.30, bf16
#     0.31, q8_0 0.37, q6_k 0.38, q4_k 0.38, q5_k 0.41, q3_k 0.47, iq4xs
#     0.49, q4_1 0.49, q5_0 0.53, f16 0.54, q5_1 0.57, iq4nl 0.62, q2_k
#     0.70
#
#   outside: model_runner 110 in 0.532 s and 64 in 1.968 s on the
#     processor, 0.170 s and 1.539 s on the device; llama-bench --device
#     none pp110 386.43 tg64 40.40, -ngl 99 pp110 1657.52 tg64 56.37,
#     -ngl 0 pp110 770.65 tg64 40.43
#
# The processor's prompt reads 206.8 tokens a second and the gap to
# llama.cpp on a prompt is 1.87 times -- under two for the first time, from
# 2.5 when the day began.

# 2026-08-28, last.  The wide byte product: 2.12x in isolation, slower in place
#
# The strip kernel is sixty-nine and a half per cent of a processor prompt
# and four changes have failed to move it. What had never been asked is
# whether the instruction itself is the ceiling.
#
# THE PROBE, a scratch program outside the repository timing nothing but
# byte dot products, four independent chains, two hundred million turns:
#
#   256-bit vpdpbusd   152 giga-multiply-adds a second   0.168 s
#   512-bit vpdpbusd   324                               0.158 s
#
# The same instruction count in the same time for twice the bytes: 2.12x,
# agreeing to six tenths of a per cent across rounds. It is NOT
# double-pumped, which the 2026-08-27 entry on the 512-bit idea guessed
# without measuring and got wrong.
#
# And the kernel had room. 3.1e12 multiply-adds in a 1419-token prompt over
# eight cores is about 67 giga a core inside this symbol against the
# instruction's 152; it issues thirty-nine instructions for two hundred and
# fifty-six multiply-adds where the peak is one for thirty-two.
#
# THE KERNEL, AND IT LOST. Two rows' blocks into one register with
# vinserti64x4 taking its second operand from memory, the activation
# duplicated into both halves with vbroadcasti64x4, and the per-block scale
# -- a broadcast for one row and impossible as one for two -- expanded from
# the existing eight-float table by vpermps rather than by storing sixteen
# floats a vector, which would have made the table a quarter of a megabyte
# on the stack.
#
#   round 1   8.271 -> 8.823      round 4   8.790 -> 9.228
#   round 2   8.275 -> 9.243      round 5   8.327 -> 9.265
#   round 3   8.130 -> 9.180
#
# Median 8.275 -> 9.228, eleven and a half per cent worse, worse in five of
# five. Bit for bit -- cbf29ce484222325 -- because the products and their
# grouping into lanes are unchanged.
#
# AND THE COUNTERS RULE OUT THE OBVIOUS EXCUSE:
#
#                cycles      instructions     IPC     time
#   256-bit      190.1 G     550.0 G          2.89    9.051 s
#   512-bit      206.7 G     532.5 G          2.58    9.718 s
#
# Fewer instructions in more cycles. And cycles a second is 21.27 G against
# 21.00 -- one and a third per cent HIGHER on the wide build -- so there is
# no AVX-512 frequency penalty on this part for this code, which was the
# first thing to suspect and is not what happened.
#
# What happened is that the instructions feeding the wide dot product cost
# more than the dot products they save: vpermps is a cross-lane permute and
# there are four of those a block, beside four vbroadcasti64x4 and a
# vinserti64x4.
#
# THE SHAPE OF THIS RESULT is the device's cooperative matrix again -- an
# instruction worth having that the layout cannot feed. Making it pay means
# interleaving several rows' quants so that one wide load is one wide
# operand, which is what the other runtime's repacked formats are for, and
# that is a change to what a file becomes at load rather than to a loop. It
# is the largest thing this file has left to try on the processor and it is
# not started.
#
# No source changed.

# 2026-08-28  The interleaving, priced and not built
#
# The entry above named the interleaved layout as the way to reach the wide
# byte product. Done at four-byte granularity it removes the last
# cross-lane instruction too: if a 512-bit lane alternates between two rows
# then the scale operand is [sA, sB, sA, sB, ...], which is a
# vbroadcastf32x2 from memory rather than the vpermps that cost the attempt
# before this. The activations would carry each four-byte group twice,
# written by the packing, which is parallel now and cheap.
#
# That is the loader, the packer, the kernel and a fallback path. So it was
# priced first: both loop shapes written against synthetic data, sixty-four
# blocks of a panel repeated, nothing in the engine touched.
#
#   two rows in two 256-bit registers, as committed   127, 130 giga-MACs/s
#   interleaved into one 512-bit, no permutes         137, 137
#
# Five per cent. Twenty-five instructions a block against thirty-nine, the
# cross-lane permute gone and the row pairing free, for a twentieth. Five
# per cent of an inner loop that is about half the kernel, on a kernel that
# is seventy per cent of a prompt, is under two per cent of a prompt. NOT
# BUILT, and the hour it took to price it against the day it would have
# taken to build is the argument for pricing things.
#
# AND THE PRICING SAYS MORE THAN ITS RESULT. That loop reaches 127 on hot
# data where the real kernel manages about 67 -- the same instructions at
# half the speed. The inner loop is therefore already at four-fifths of
# what vpdpbusd does in isolation (152), and half of this kernel's time is
# somewhere else. The annotation agrees: forty-seven per cent of the symbol
# in the inner loop, about a third in the scale-table build.
#
# WHICH LEAVES A CONTRADICTION, and it is the sharpest open question here.
# The 2026-08-27 entry halved the scale-table build's shuffle count and
# measured level, and concluded those instructions overlap the dot products
# and cost nothing. Both cannot be true: either the build is a third of the
# symbol and halving it should have shown, or it is not and the missing
# half is somewhere neither measurement has looked. Settling it is one
# ablation -- take the build out entirely, accept wrong answers, and see
# what the kernel does without it.
#
# No source changed.

# 2026-08-28  The second loop, of the same length
#
# Two entries above disagreed. One halved the scale-table build's shuffles,
# measured level, and concluded the block was free. The other priced the
# interleaved layout and found the inner loop already at four-fifths of the
# byte product's isolated peak, which leaves half this kernel somewhere
# else. Both cannot be right.
#
# THE ABLATION. Build the table for the first panel only: wrong answers,
# but valid floats -- an uninitialised table reads as denormals and times
# nothing honest -- and the build's cost divided by the panel count.
#
#   round 1   8.456 -> 6.700      round 4   8.328 -> 6.841
#   round 2   8.058 -> 6.829      round 5   7.869 -> 6.700
#   round 3   7.798 -> 6.541
#
# Median 8.058 -> 6.700. Seventeen per cent of a prompt, 1.358 seconds,
# better in five of five. The scale table is not free and never was.
#
# AND THE ARITHMETIC THAT SHOULD HAVE COME FIRST reconciles the two
# measurements. For a 2048-row matrix the build runs 1024 panels of 64
# blocks of 8 -- 512 thousand iterations -- and the insertion beside it runs
# 512 thousand byte dot products. The build is not overhead around the inner
# loop. It is a second loop of the same length, four or five instructions an
# iteration against the dot product's four. Halving one kind of instruction
# inside it was never going to show against the whole prompt, and removing
# the loop shows at once.
#
# That is the second time this file has misread a null. The first was
# reading a profile's instruction shares as time shares; this is reading a
# null on one kind of instruction as a null on the block that holds it. The
# rule that survives both: an ablation prices a block, a profile does not.
#
# WHAT IT COMPUTES is Scale (row, block) * Vector_Scale (block, vector),
# eight products a block, stored and then read back one at a time by the
# insertion as a {1to8} broadcast. The products are irreducible -- the
# insertion needs every one of them. Computing them once a panel, writing
# them to memory and reading them back a few instructions later is not, and
# that is where the seventeen per cent is.
#
# Nothing kept: the ablation gives wrong answers and exists to price the
# block. It is the largest single item measured on the processor since the
# byte dot product itself.
#
# No source changed.

# 2026-08-28  The scale table, as two loops
#
# The ablation above priced the build at seventeen per cent of a prompt.
# What it computes -- eight products a block, a weight scale times an
# activation scale -- is irreducible, because the insertion reads every one
# of them. What was not irreducible was the shape.
#
# The inner four turns did two things at once: a store into Scaling and a
# read-modify-write into Undo. The second is what stopped the nest
# vectorizing, and it is why halving the shuffles in it did nothing --
# there was nothing wrong with the shuffles.
#
# Apart, the first is a map and the second an accumulation, and -O3 takes
# both four at a time. And the correction now multiplies by Vector_Undo --
# the block's scale times its total, worked out once a call rather than
# once a panel -- so a turn multiplies by one number where it multiplied by
# two.
#
#   round 1   8.383 -> 8.214      round 4   8.116 -> 8.064
#   round 2   8.369 -> 7.760      round 5   8.264 -> 8.062
#   round 3   8.436 -> 7.909
#
# Median 8.369 -> 8.062, three and seven tenths per cent, better in five of
# five. That is about 0.31 s of the 1.358 the ablation priced: a quarter of
# the block, and the other three quarters are the products and their stores
# that the insertion needs.
#
# Not bit for bit: (Scale * Vector_Scale) * Vector_Total became Scale *
# (Vector_Scale * Vector_Total), the same three numbers in a different
# order. The sweep is the arbiter -- 28344 sequences, nothing outside
# tolerance.
#
# The same restructuring measured level on 2026-08-27 and was reverted. The
# difference is the second multiply: splitting the loops alone left every
# turn multiplying twice, and the entry that recorded that null did not
# know the block was worth seventeen per cent.
#
# THE SITTING, on a part that settled at 51 C, nothing refused.
#
#   engine, twelve tokens: 0.438 s wall -- 0.066 evaluating, 0.364
#     generating, 1.60 s processor; --arith f32 1.181 s for 8.35 s;
#     fourteen threads 0.473 s for 1.92 s against 0.438 and 1.60 at seven
#
#   reference, four tokens: cpu 0.067 + 0.125, reference 5.678 + 3.758 --
#     forty-nine times in total, eighty-five on the prompt; serial 2.35x
#     q8_0, 2.34x q4_k, 3.16x f32
#
#   device: six tokens twelve generated, cpu 0.438 s (0.072 + 0.364, 1.60 s
#     processor) against device 0.325 s (0.049 + 0.278, 0.03 s); 110 tokens
#     cpu 0.538 s for 3.29 s against device 0.148 s for 0.16 s
#
#   drafting: eight-bit 0.433 s, two-bit 1.535 s, drafted 3.575 s with 24
#     proposed and 9 accepted -- 36 ms and 128 ms a token, 85 ms to check
#     five positions, and at that cost no draft pays. Eleventh reading of a
#     number that has never settled and a conclusion that has never moved.
#
#   scaling, pinned: q8_0 one vector 2565 at one share, 13170 at seven,
#     14358 at eight; thirty-two per pass 4805, 24533, 26840. Unpinned
#     13250 and 13600 with one vector, 24514 and 24889 batched.
#
#   kernels, ns an element: f32 0.25, q4_0 0.30, bf16 0.31, q8_0 0.36, q4_k
#     0.38, q6_k 0.39, q5_k 0.41, q3_k 0.47, q4_1 0.49, iq4xs 0.50, q5_0
#     0.53, f16 0.55, q5_1 0.57, iq4nl 0.63, q2_k 0.70
#
#   outside: model_runner 110 in 0.536 s and 64 in 1.967 s on the
#     processor, 0.148 s and 1.545 s on the device; llama-bench --device
#     none pp110 389.20 tg64 40.39, -ngl 99 pp110 1648.99 tg64 56.50,
#     -ngl 0 pp110 776.28 tg64 40.36
#
# The processor's prompt reads 205.2 tokens a second against 206.8 in the
# sitting before, which is level: this change is worth three and a half per
# cent on a 1419-token prompt and the 110-token one is a tenth its length,
# with a tenth its blocks to build scales for.

# 2026-08-28  One multiply-add against thirty-two
#
# The scale table's remaining cost, split by two ablations that each keep
# one half of the block and drop the other. Four rounds, three builds
# alternated inside each round so drift touches all three alike.
#
#   as committed                              8.113 s
#   the table's multiply and its load gone    7.646 s   0.466 s,  5.7 %
#   the correction's accumulation gone        6.186 s   1.927 s, 23.7 %
#
# The correction is a quarter of the prompt. It is one multiply-add per
# block, row and vector, beside a byte dot product that does thirty-two: a
# thirty-second of the arithmetic taking half the time. That is a symptom
# and not a cost, and addr2line names it -- the four turns write
# Undo (At_Undo + Vector), an array read and written in place, and -O3
# builds a shuffle network around it. vunpcklps, valignd and vaddps come to
# eighteen per cent of the symbol for four additions.
#
# TWO WAYS AROUND IT, NEITHER OF WHICH WORKS.
#
#   Row outside block, so the four corrections live in registers. Halves
#   the shuffles -- vunpcklps 95 to 48, valignd 72 to 36 -- and measures
#   eight per cent worse in five of five, because the scale table is then
#   written with a stride of eight floats in two passes where it was
#   written straight through. Getting one of the two right is worse than
#   getting neither, which is also why the same restructuring measured
#   level on 2026-08-27.
#
#   Block outermost with both inner loops written out, eight named scalars
#   across the whole block loop and the table still written in order. The
#   object file comes back with exactly the baseline's shuffle counts --
#   94, 72, 64, 50 -- and it measures level, better in one of three. GNAT
#   is vectorizing across the block loop and not within the four turns, and
#   neither shape reaches that.
#
# WHAT IS LEFT is to compute the correction where the code is not the
# compiler's. The insertion already reads the scale table a block at a time
# and could accumulate Sum over blocks of Scaling times Vector_Total with
# one more broadcast and one more fused multiply-add a dot product. That is
# about a wash by instruction count and it moves eighteen per cent of a
# symbol out of a shuffle network, which is the whole argument for it.
#
# No source changed.

# 2026-08-28  And the reduction, which completes the accounting
#
# The last unpriced block of the strip kernel: the eight lanes the
# insertion leaves, summed into a binary64 accumulator once a row-vector
# pair. Ablated by summing one lane instead of eight, which keeps the
# structure and the store so that nothing downstream goes dead.
#
#   as committed          7.953 s
#   one lane not eight    7.643 s     0.310 s, 3.9 %
#
# Less than the 8.6 per cent of samples addr2line put at that line, which
# is the same lesson this file keeps writing down.
#
# THE ACCOUNTING IS NOW COMPLETE for the symbol that is sixty-nine per cent
# of a processor prompt:
#
#   the bias correction              23.7 %   1.927 s
#   the table's multiply and load     5.7 %   0.466 s
#   the Landed reduction              3.9 %   0.310 s
#   the byte dot products             the rest
#
# AND TWO OF THE THREE WANT THE SAME FIX. The insertion holds its eight
# accumulators in registers when the block loop ends. It could fold them
# there -- the twelve-instruction reduction Head_Scores already uses, two
# dependent chains instead of eight separate folds -- and it could
# accumulate the correction with one more broadcast and one more fused
# multiply-add a dot product. One change, twenty-seven and a half per cent
# of a prompt in reach, and both blocks out of the shuffle network that
# neither Ada restructuring could escape.
#
# No source changed.

# 2026-08-28  Both blocks, moved into the insertion
#
# The accounting said the correction was 23.7 per cent of a prompt and the
# reduction 3.9, and that both were shuffle networks and widening loops -O3
# built around Ada that no arrangement of Ada could talk it out of. Both
# are now the insertion's, which is the one place the code is not the
# compiler's.
#
# THE CORRECTION, in three instructions a block. It wants, for each of the
# eight row-vector pairs, the sum over blocks of the scale times the
# block's total. The insertion already reads those eight scales -- one a
# dot product, as a {1to8} broadcast. Read instead as one thirty-two byte
# vmovups they are eight lanes in the order [row0 v0..v3, row1 v0..v3], and
# the totals written twice over at eight to a block line up lane for lane.
# So one vmovups, one vfmadd231ps into an accumulator, and all eight
# corrections advance together.
#
# THE FOLD, in twelve at the end: vhaddps in pairs, then pairs of those,
# then the two halves added -- the reduction Head_Scores already uses, two
# dependent chains where Ada had eight widening loops. The accumulators
# moved from ymm16..23 to ymm8..15 because vhaddps has no EVEX encoding and
# cannot reach the high sixteen, which the assembler said plainly.
#
#   round 1   8.339 -> 7.378
#   round 2   7.825 -> 6.953
#   round 3   8.144 -> 7.005
#
# Median 8.144 -> 7.005: fourteen per cent, better in three of three. The
# largest single change measured on the processor in this file. Against a
# ceiling of 27.6 the two blocks were priced at, recovering fourteen is
# what the three instructions a block and twelve a panel predict.
#
# The answers move -- (Scale * Vector_Scale) * Total where it was Scale *
# (Vector_Scale * Total), and a tree fold where there was an ascending
# binary64 sum -- and the sweep is the arbiter: 28344 sequences, nothing
# outside tolerance. The sixty-four token digest goes to 1cb5fffbb21399ad;
# the 110-token prompt digest does not move.
#
# THE SITTING, on a part that settled at 51 C, nothing refused.
#
#   engine, twelve tokens: 0.426 s wall -- 0.060 evaluating, 0.367
#     generating, 1.55 s processor; --arith f32 1.204 s for 8.40 s;
#     fourteen threads 0.458 s for 1.85 s against 0.426 and 1.55 at seven
#
#   reference, four tokens: cpu 0.068 + 0.123, reference 5.650 + 3.771 --
#     forty-nine times in total, eighty-three on the prompt; serial 2.33x
#     q8_0, 2.32x q4_k, 3.10x f32
#
#   device: six tokens twelve generated, cpu 0.425 s (0.059 + 0.366, 1.55 s
#     processor) against device 0.337 s (0.053 + 0.283, 0.04 s); 110 tokens
#     cpu 0.434 s for 2.65 s against device 0.182 s for 0.19 s
#
#   drafting: eight-bit 0.434 s, two-bit 1.519 s, drafted 3.433 s with 24
#     proposed and 7 accepted -- 36 ms and 127 ms a token, 64 ms to check
#     five positions, and at that cost no draft pays.
#
#   scaling, pinned: q8_0 one vector 2629 at one share, 13144 at seven,
#     14343 at eight; thirty-two per pass 4795, 24527, 27041. Unpinned
#     12933 and 13696 with one vector, 23398 and 26332 batched.
#
#   kernels, ns an element: f32 0.25, q4_0 0.31, bf16 0.31, q8_0 0.37, q4_k
#     0.37, q6_k 0.38, q5_k 0.40, q3_k 0.48, q4_1 0.49, iq4xs 0.49, q5_0
#     0.53, f16 0.54, q5_1 0.57, iq4nl 0.62, q2_k 0.71
#
#   outside: model_runner 110 in 0.432 s and 64 in 1.982 s on the
#     processor, 0.182 s and 1.543 s on the device; llama-bench --device
#     none pp110 384.99 tg64 40.43, -ngl 99 pp110 1660.83 tg64 56.50,
#     -ngl 0 pp110 773.70 tg64 40.43
#
# THE PROCESSOR'S PROMPT READS 254.6 TOKENS A SECOND and the gap to
# llama.cpp on a prompt is 1.51 times, from 2.5 when this began. The
# device's prompt row reads 604.4 against 743.2 in the sitting before,

# 2026-08-28  Three loops written out, and a slower day

#   Re-measured after the strip driver's scale-table loop and two of

#   three on the 1419-token prompt: 7.739 / 7.845 / 7.812 / 7.867 before
#   against 7.478 / 7.878 / 7.299 / 7.632 after -- three and a half per cent
#   on the median, better in three of four, and 469.3 G instructions against
#   456.7. No digest moves. The 110-token prompt is level either way, which
#   is the shape of the work: attention grows with the square of the context
#   and two thirds of what this touches is a hundred and sixty times smaller
#   there.
#
#   Refused in the same sitting: two blocks per five-hundred-and-twelve bit
#   byte dot product. Seven per cent fewer instructions for twenty per cent
#   more cycles -- 469.3 G / 156.5 G against 435.6 G / 188.0 G. The part
#   double-pumps, so the width buys nothing on the arithmetic and the scale
#   plumbing the wide shape needs is new work. Reverted; the paragraph in the

# 2026-08-28  The scalar loops nobody had looked at, and three refusals
#
#   Re-measured after the tile write-back's index checks were suppressed and
#   the quantizer's magnitude-and-finiteness pass was written out as lanes.
#   Alternated four rounds of three on the 1419-token prompt: 7.739 / 7.667 /
#   7.418 / 7.709 before against 6.845 / 6.878 / 7.016 / 6.885 after -- ten
#   and a half per cent on the median, better in four of four, and 410.2 G
#   instructions against 390.2. No digest moves. Mat_Mul_Range_Packed went
#   from 2.8 per cent of a prompt to 1.3 and Quantize_Blocks from 3.0 to 1.8.
#
#   Three things measured and not kept.
#
#   Prefetching the next position's four lines in Blend_Run: 6.927 s against
#   6.846, better in two of four, which is nothing. Two positions a turn in
#   the same kernel: 6.729 against 6.925, worse in four of four, on fewer
#   instructions. Both were run at the suspicion that Blend_Run's seven per
#   cent of samples against two and a half of instructions is memory latency,
#   and both say it is not that -- so the KV layout change they were run to
#   justify is not justified, and the seven per cent stands unexplained.
#
#   A strip of twelve, priced by counting: 4 weight instructions, 96 dot
#   products, 8 corrections and 5 of loop is 113 for 24 dots against 77 for
#   16, which is 4.71 per dot against 4.81. Two per cent of the kernel for a
#   shape needing fifteen of the sixteen general-purpose registers. The
#   corrections grow with the strip, which is what makes eight against two
#   rows a local optimum.
#
#   A sixteen-bit load for the half-precision scales, three instructions
#   where six: 390.2 G to 386.5, and the symbol went 7.0 per cent to 7.3.
#   That loop is bound by its thirty-four byte stride, not by the
#   instructions around the load. Reverted.
#
#   Four sittings of llama.cpp on an unchanged binary now read 385.0, 347.8,
#   326.0 and 334.4 tokens a second on a prompt -- a thirteen per cent spread
#   with nothing in it but the machine. This program read 254.6, 226.3,

# 2026-08-28  Three questions asked of the hardware
#
#   No code changed, so no figure group moved. What changed is what is known
#   about them.
#
#   Misses by symbol, whole prompt, perf record on the event rather than on
#   cycles. The run takes 2.53 G last-level misses, 19.0 G first-level misses
#   on 172.9 G loads and 12.2 M page-table walks.
#
#                        LLC     L1     TLB
#     blend_run         68.1%  19.0%   8.2%
#     head_scores       19.9%   6.3%  11.8%
#     rows_by_strips     3.3%  70.8%  14.1%
#     rows                  -      -  20.8%
#
#   Two thirds of everything fetched from memory is fetched by the value
#   blend, on two and a half per cent of the instructions. That is why the
#   prefetch and the two-a-turn unroll of the commit before both did nothing:
#   both hide latency and this is bytes. The value cache of a 1419-token
#   prompt is 1.4 MB a layer, 32 MB across the model, against 16 of
#   last-level cache. The strip kernel is the mirror image and the
#   reassuring half of the table.
#
#   The thirty-four byte stride, ablated by setting it to thirty-two: 6.629 s
#   against 6.976, no gain, so misaligned loads cost nothing measurable here.
#   And the scale extraction a repack would delete is already done once per
#   share and not once per tile -- hoisted to the caller it measured 390.2 G
#   instructions against 413.5, worse, the conditional slice copying once a
#   tile. A cache across batches is the only version that saves anything:
#   about 128 MiB for this model, perhaps six per cent, and the weights stop
#   being the file's own pages.
#
#   Exponentiate doubled: 6.629 s against 6.985, five and a half per cent of
#   the wall for two and three quarter per cent more instructions.
#
#   All three taken back to back, three rounds each, on the machine the
#   sitting above was taken on.

# 2026-08-28  The narrow context, and the lanes that were not the arithmetic
#
#   Two changes. Blend_Run_Halved and Head_Dot_Halved read a half-precision
#   context wide, where nothing did before: --kv-cache f16 on the 1419-token
#   prompt goes 26.9 s to 7.849, against 6.683 at full precision -- four
#   times slower to one and a sixth. q8 is the same fix again and has not had
#   it: 18.263 s. Halving the bytes did not halve the time, which is a third
#   piece of evidence that the value blend is not short of bandwidth.
#
#   And Exponentiate written out over eight lanes: 390.2 G instructions
#   against 381.9, alternated 6.801 s against 6.754 over four rounds, better
#   in three.
#
#   The polynomial in it is deliberately not fused, and the reason is worth
#   the paragraph. Fused it rounds once a term instead of twice -- more
#   accurate and a different answer. The conformance sweep passed it at 28344
#   sequences and the suite at 286 tests. What caught it was this sitting:
#   the drafted run's digest went 33f48397f89839f6 to 7d3e2df2d776ba62 and
#   the sixty-four token run's 3248ac1bb7011de0 to 1cb5fffbb21399ad. A
#   drafted run is published as producing exactly the text the model produces
#   alone, and it does so because a batched evaluation and a token-at-a-time
#   one agree to the last bit; rounding the exponential differently moved a
#   near-tie across. Unfused, every digest here is the one that was there.
#
#   Only five groups moved. The kernels and scaling groups do not list
#   model_runner-kernels.adb and were left as they were measured.
#
#   The sitting, figure by figure:
#
#   engine, twelve tokens: 0.436 s wall (0.067 + 0.371), 1.58 s processor,
#     0.078 s loading; at --arith f32 1.394 s for 8.90 s; at --threads 15
#     0.488 s for 1.83 s
#
#   reference, four tokens: cpu 0.060 + 0.127, reference 5.813 + 3.856 --
#     fifty-two times in total, ninety-seven on the prompt; serial 2.33x
#     q8_0, 2.27x q4_k, 3.02x f32

#   device: six tokens twelve generated, cpu 0.451 s (0.065 + 0.375, 1.62 s
#     processor) against device 0.347 s (0.056 + 0.291, 0.03 s); 110 tokens
#     cpu 0.436 s for 2.49 s against device 0.166 s for 0.19 s

#   drafting: eight-bit 0.439 s, two-bit 1.808 s, drafted 3.801 s with 24

#     no draft pays.
