#   than a rounding for the first time: 42.3 against 40.4.
#
# load:  0.19 rising to 3.93
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0

# 2026-08-29  A layer's second half in one submission: built, right, slower
#
#   The entry above prices a generated token's sixty-seven submissions at
#   twenty-three per cent and names the three a layer as structural: the
#   host normalizes, rotates and joins between the products, so no two of
#   the device's sequences are adjacent. One of the three was removed. It
#   works, it computes the same answers, and it is slower.
#
#   What was built:
#
#     norm.comp, the root-mean-square normalization, one invocation a
#     position with the sum walked in order and accumulated in binary64 --
#     which is what the processor does, so the two agree bit for bit.
#
#     A third unit on combine.comp: two is not a unit but an addition,
#     which is the residual join. Three lines rather than a kernel, on the
#     same layout and the same two arms in and one out.
#
#     Add_Norm and Add_Join on the sequence, and back-references -- Reads,
#     Reads_Two, At_Vector -- so a step may read one it names rather than
#     the one before it. A fused layer needs all three: both arms of the
#     feed-forward read the normalization four steps back, and the second
#     join reads the first.
#
#     Attend_And_Feed: attention, its projection, the join, the
#     normalization, both arms, the combination, the projection down and
#     the second join. Nine steps, one submission, where the engine made
#     two.
#
#   It is right. Twelve generated tokens on the device answer
#   5abff916f9d83ca6 and sixty-four answer 448c2ed68ec342ee, which are what
#   the published figures answer -- so a normalization computed on the
#   device in binary64 agrees with the processor's to the last bit, which is
#   what walking the sum in order was for.
#
#   And it is forty per cent slower: sixty-four generated tokens 0.367 s
#   against 0.256. Two candidates were measured and are not it. The binary64
#   sum is worth eight per cent of the difference -- the same kernel in
#   binary32 reads 0.365 against 0.398 and answers the same tokens. And the
#   barriers are worth nothing: fencing every reader serialized the two arms
#   of the feed-forward, which used to run together, and publishing instead
#   -- one barrier makes every step before it visible, so a second reader of
#   the same result needs none -- gave back four thousandths of a second.
#
#   So a third of a generated token went somewhere the submission count does
#   not explain, and this is written down rather than guessed at. Not
#   committed.
#
#   Three bugs found on the way, each of which cost an hour and none of
#   which the type system could have caught:
#
#     A pipeline created before its request had an entry point and a layout
#     -- the driver takes the module and faults later, with a stack in
#     libvulkan and nothing in this program to look at.
#
#     A barrier emitted only for a step chained to the one before it, where
#     a step that names an earlier one needs it just as much. Without it the
#     sequence answers, and answers wrongly.
#
#     A normalization handed to the weight loader as its Rows by its
#     Columns, which for a step whose Rows and Columns say what it reads and
#     writes is a square: the loader was asked to upload four million values
#     out of an array of two thousand.

# What puts a device figure in a bad window, and what a device row needs
# ---------------------------------------------------------------------
#
# A device figure in this file swung 1.51 times between sittings with the
# same binary in it, and one of them was published and then withdrawn. The
# cause is the host, in two parts, and both reproduce.
#
# The repeat count is a third of the figure. A 1419-token Q8_0 device prompt:
#
#   --repeats 1    1.022, 1.022, 1.027 s    0.36 to 0.39 s of host
#   --repeats 3    0.751, 0.764, 0.773 s    0.20 to 0.27 s of host
#
# Three runs each way, one sitting, no overlap. The first run of a process
# builds its pipelines, writes its descriptors and touches a gigabyte of
# mapped model for the first time; a median of three hides that where a
# single run reports it. The host share this tool prints beside every wall
# says it from the other side, which is why that column is printed.
#
# Competition for the host is the rest of it. Busy loops, nothing else
# changed:
#
#   quiet, load 1.00          0.760 s    0.22 s of host
#   two loops, load 1.35      0.799 s    0.27 s of host
#   four loops, load 2.23     0.878 s    0.27 s of host
#
# Smooth and in one direction. About a fifth of that prompt's wall is the
# processor between submissions -- normalizations, rotations, joins -- so
# whatever stretches the fifth stretches the wall. llama.cpp is not immune:
# eight loops took it from 1826 to 1295 tokens a second.
#
# Three candidates were checked and are not it. The clock the part reports
# was 1758 to 1779 MHz in the slow readings and 1763 to 1841 in the fast,
# which overlaps. The power budget is shared: both programs draw twenty-five
# watts sustained, and forty-five seconds of idle before a measurement gives
# 1.032 s rather than a boosted one. And a rebuild between measurements is
# worth nothing, 0.755 against 0.757.
#
# So the comparison that stands is the quiet one. 1785 tokens a second
# against llama.cpp's 1755, taken on a quiet machine, is sound; the 1211 that
# read 1.51 times behind was taken between builds and test runs and is not.
# On a quiet host the device long prompt is level.
#
# The rule this leaves is about taking figures, not about the shader. This
# file's rule is that a figure is taken on a quiet host or it is not taken,
# and the gate refuses above a load of 1.50. That is loose for a device row:
# 2.23 is worth sixteen per cent and 1.35 is worth five, and both publish. A
# device row wants a quieter machine than a processor row does, because a
# fifth of it is the processor -- and it wants its repeat count written down
# beside it, because llama-bench always warms up first and this tool reports
# whichever state it was asked for. Every device figure taken in an
# alternated comparison during that work had builds running between its
# readings.

# 2026-09-06  Generating is at the memory wall, and four ways round it
# ------------------------------------------------------------------
#
# The last measured gap against llama.cpp was generating: 37.0 tokens a
# second against 39.0 on tinyllama-1.1b-chat-q8_0, five and a half per cent.
# Everything below is one sitting on a quiet host, alternated, digest
# 1cb5fffbb21399ad on every reading.
#
# The ceiling. Half a gigabyte read four times over, by threads:
#
#   one     35.9 GB/s
#   two     44.1 GB/s
#   four    45.1 GB/s
#   eight   44.7 GB/s
#
# Two threads reach it. A generated token reads every weight once -- about a
# gigabyte for this model -- so 37.0 tokens a second is 40.3 GB/s and
# llama.cpp's 39.0 is 42.5: eighty-nine per cent of the ceiling against
# ninety-four, with eleven per cent above either of them.
#
# The kernel is ahead, not behind. One thread, no sharing at all:
#
#   this program   27.2 tokens a second   (2.332 and 2.376 s for sixty-four)
#   llama.cpp      22.3                   (24.22 and 20.35 t/s, -t 1)
#
# Twenty-two per cent ahead per core. What llama.cpp does better is scale:
# 1.86 times from one thread to eight against 1.31 to 1.43 here from one
# share to five, bought with 7.7 hot cores against 5.1, 54.8 billion cycles
# against 38.2, and 113 context switches against 4,899. It spins where this
# sleeps, and the spin is why its cycles are higher and its wall lower.
#
# Four sweeps, three alternated rounds each, all refused.
#
#   shares      wall       of the processor
#   five        1.730 s    8.78 s
#   six         1.738      10.37
#   eight       1.758      13.78
#
# Every reading of five below every reading of six and eight. This confirms
# the Vector_Team constant against today's kernels rather than against the
# sitting it was first swept in -- which the entry above this one says is
# the only way such a constant may be confirmed.
#
#   spin budget   20_000 against 100_000 against 400_000 pause turns: wall
#                 inside its own spread on every round, context switches
#                 4,899 to 4,534. What sleeps is not a spin running out.
#
#   tile grain    thirty-two rows against sixty-four against a hundred and
#                 twenty-eight: every reading overlapping.
#
# So the five and a half per cent is scaling efficiency on a memory path two
# threads already saturate, and llama.cpp buys it with cores this pool
# deliberately does not spend on a fifteen-watt part. Reading fewer bytes is
# a choice about quantization rather than about kernels. On a machine with
# more bandwidth per core than this one the shares sweep would want running
# again, and could well land somewhere else.

# 2026-09-06  What a device figure is really measuring, and one window unsolved
# --------------------------------------------------------------------------
#
# The entry above finds a device figure moves with how well the host keeps
# the part fed and stops there, having no way to see that share. tests speed
# now prints it, and this is what it showed.
#
# THE SITTING. The 1419-token device prompt, a dozen readings over an
# afternoon: 1.075 to 1.131 s, fed 56 to 70 per cent. llama.cpp on the same
# file in the same minutes:
#
#   llama.cpp device pp1419   1815.25 t/s   against 1822.0 published on 09-01
#   this program              1284      t/s   against 1730.5 published on 09-01
#
# Within half a per cent on one side and twenty-six per cent down on the
# other. The machine was not slow.
#
# AND ONLY THE BATCHED PATH WAS. The single-vector path read what it has
# always read:
#
#   sixty-four tokens generated, device   51.0 t/s   against 51.4 published
#   twelve tokens generated, device       0.227 s    against 0.227 published
#   110-token device prompt                973 t/s   against 1182.8 to 1571.4
#   1419-token device prompt              1284 t/s   against 1730.5
#
# The two prompts go through the matrix shader and the two generating runs
# do not, and the split falls exactly there.
#
# TEN THINGS MEASURED AND EXCLUDED, none of them it:
#
#   temperature          55 C, then 61 after four minutes idle; idle changed
#                        nothing (1.080, 1.091, 1.091 after it)
#   processor clock      4018 MHz mean during the run, above llama.cpp's
#                        3692 on the same work; boost on, amd-pstate-epp at
#                        balance_performance
#   load                 0.45 to 0.50, and the gate's own instrument agreed
#   memory placement     501 MB VRAM and 1912 GTT, against llama.cpp's 482
#                        and 1883 -- the same
#   the binary           the stale-build check passes, and recompiling every
#                        shader variant from source gives a
#                        model_runner-shaders.ads byte-identical to the
#                        committed one (md5 f8eb0b46...)
#   the shader cache     moving ~/.cache/mesa_shader_cache aside, and
#                        RADV_DEBUG=nocache, both read the same
#   the fence spin       0, 64 and 1000 turns, three alternated rounds,
#                        walls overlapping
#   the repeat count     the effect the entry above prices, and not this
#   a rebuild            0.755 against 0.757
#   processor contention the run is 0.44 s of host across a 1.4 s wall
#
# WHAT IS LEFT IS THE FEEDING. The part is idle nearly half of a batched
# prompt here and twenty-two per cent of the same work under llama.cpp. For
# three repeats of that prompt:
#
#                     user      sys      ioctls   of them failed   fed
#   this program      1.52 s    1.34 s   40,473   37,653           53%
#   llama.cpp         0.63 s    0.28 s    9,699    6,870           78%
#
# Five times the kernel time and four times the calls, most of ours polls
# for a completion that had not arrived. That is a path that submits and
# waits many times where llama.cpp records a graph and submits once, and it
# is why this program's wall moves with the host where llama.cpp's does not.
#
# WHAT IS NOT ESTABLISHED IS WHAT SELECTS THE STATE. The same command read
# 0.755 to 0.760 s three times in the morning and 1.075 to 1.131 a dozen
# times that afternoon, with llama.cpp unmoved across both, and nothing in
# the list above accounts for it.
#
# SO THE DEVICE ROWS WERE RE-MEASURED AND DELIBERATELY NOT PUBLISHED. They
# came in twenty to forty-five per cent below every reading in their own
# history while the program they are compared against was within half a per
# cent of its last one. A figure taken in a window like that is the thing
# this file has twice had to withdraw. What is published instead is the
# instrument, so the next sitting can say which window it is in rather than
# find out three commits later.
#
# READ THE SHARE AS A COARSE NUMBER. It is sampled a hundred times a second
# and the same run read 56, 63 and 70 per cent on three passes whose walls
# were inside five per cent of each other. It separates a starved run from a
# well-fed one; it does not rank two good ones.

# 2026-09-06  The submission pattern, closed by five measurements
# --------------------------------------------------------------
#
# The entry above ends by naming the submission pattern as the one candidate
# measured and not excluded: this program submits and waits where llama.cpp
# records a graph and submits once. That reading was wrong.
#
# ONE 1419-TOKEN DEVICE PROMPT, COUNTED AT THE KERNEL:
#
#                     AMDGPU_CS   SYNCOBJ_WAIT   SYNCOBJ_TIMELINE_WAIT
#   this program            345         34,841                     344
#   llama.cpp             1,199          3,364                   1,168
#
# Three and a half times FEWER submissions and ten times more polling. The
# 40,473-against-9,699 total quoted above is real, and almost all of the
# difference is fence polls rather than work handed over.
#
# AND THE POLLS COST NO WALL. Built with Fence_Spin at zero:
#
#   Fence_Spin 1000    34,841 SYNCOBJ_WAIT    1.078 s
#   Fence_Spin 0          352 SYNCOBJ_WAIT    1.086 s
#
# Ninety-nine per cent of the calls gone and the run does not move. They
# cost host processor time -- which the entry that priced them already said
# -- and not the wall.
#
# NOR DOES THE SUBMISSION COUNT.
#
#   --batch-size 128    552 submissions    1.074 s    fed 55%
#   --batch-size 2048   345 submissions    1.088 s    fed 70%
#
# A third of the submissions removed buys nothing. That is not the shape of
# a per-submission cost.
#
# NOR IS THE HOST COPYING OR RECORDING. An LD_PRELOAD interposer over memcpy
# and memmove:
#
#   one repeat     864,564 calls   5.64 GB
#   three repeats  968,883 calls   5.94 GB
#
# Two more evaluations add 0.30 GB, so an evaluation costs 0.15 GB and the
# 5.64 is the model being loaded, once. At memcpy speed that is about one
# per cent of the wall. And timing the host between the slot wait returning
# and the submission going out gives a MEDIAN OF 16 MICROSECONDS for a
# seventeen-step sequence.
#
# WHAT THE ARITHMETIC SAYS INSTEAD:
#
#                    wall      fed     device busy
#   this program    1.083 s    56 %      0.61 s
#   llama.cpp       0.78  s    78 %      0.61 s
#
# The two do the same work in the same device time. The whole of the
# difference is idle: 0.47 s here and 0.17 s there. Nothing is wrong with
# the shaders and nothing is wrong with the host.
#
# LEARNED ON THE WAY, and where to look next. Every one of the 345
# submissions is followed by a wait. The machinery written to avoid that --
# hand over without waiting where nothing is read back -- fires for 15 of
# 330 sequences; the other 315 report carryout=TRUE kept=FALSE lent=FALSE,
# which is the case it was written for, and wait anyway. The wait they hit
# is the two-slot reuse wait at the top of the next Run, and it blocks for a
# median of 856 microseconds (mean 2376, max 23,575). That is a pipeline two
# deep with the host running ahead of the part, which is the healthy shape
# rather than a bug -- but THE DEPTH IS THE NEXT THING TO TRY, not the
# count, and this file has now spent one sitting proving it is not the
# count.
#
# A LIMIT ON THE FED SHARE, recorded because the number is new. It reads 55
# per cent at --batch-size 128 and 70 at 2048 while the two walls are within
# one per cent of each other. It separates a starved run from a fed one and
# does nothing finer, and the arithmetic above is the most that should be
# asked of it.

# 2026-09-06  The pipeline depth, and a correction to the fed arithmetic
# ---------------------------------------------------------------------
#
# The entry above closes the submission count and says the two-deep pipeline
# is where to look next. It is not that either, and the reading that pointed
# there was wrong.
#
# THE DEPTH. The engine held its slots as a pair -- two command buffers, two
# descriptor arrays, two fences, Swap_Slots exchanging them. Generalized to a
# ring of any depth (about twenty lines), three alternated rounds on the
# 1419-token device prompt, digest 1a26d24d33b8957b throughout:
#
#   depth two     1.077   1.104   1.105 s
#   depth three   1.089   1.135   1.149
#   depth four    1.093   1.102   1.106
#   depth six     1.089   1.102   1.105
#
# Being six sequences ahead of the part buys what being two ahead buys.
# Reverted: a ring that buys nothing is a pair with more code, and at depth
# two the generic form warns twice (a null loop and an always-false guard).
#
# THE CORRECTION, which matters more. The entry above prices this program at
# 56 per cent fed against llama.cpp's 78, and concludes from it that both do
# the same work in the same device time. That pair was sampled over a fixed
# ten-second window while the two runs had different lengths: llama.cpp
# finished inside the window and the idle after it was averaged in.
#
# Sampled instead over eight seconds in the middle of a run long enough to
# fill it -- llama-bench -r 20, and --repeats 15 here -- three rounds each:
#
#   llama.cpp        99%   99%   99%
#   this program     78%   78%   78%
#
# So the arithmetic is:
#
#                  wall      busy      idle
#   this program   1.083 s   0.845 s   0.238 s
#   llama.cpp      0.782     0.774     0.008
#
# llama.cpp SATURATES the part. This program spends nine per cent more device
# time on the same prompt AND leaves the part idle for twenty-two per cent of
# its wall on top of that. "The two do the same work in the same device time"
# is withdrawn.
#
# WHAT SURVIVES IS THE NEGATIVE RESULT, now three deep. The submission count
# does not move the wall (552 against 345). The barriers between dispatches
# move with that same count, at seventeen steps a sequence throughout, and do
# not move it. The depth does not move it. Three orthogonal orchestration
# knobs, all null: whatever the twenty-two per cent is, it does not answer to
# how the work is handed over.
#
# AND THE INSTRUMENT UNDER-READS. The fed figure this tool prints says 56 to
# 70 per cent where matched mid-run sampling says a steady 78, because the
# window it samples spans more than the evaluation it reports on. It is still
# the right number to carry and the window wants narrowing to the evaluation
# -- which is speed_run.adb, inside two figure groups' fingerprints, so it
# wants a measurement sitting rather than a spare hour. Until then, read a
# printed fed share as a floor.

# 2026-09-06  The small dispatches, and the window moving mid-sitting
# ------------------------------------------------------------------
#
# The entries above chase a fifth of a device prompt through the submission
# count, the barriers and the pipeline depth, find nothing in any of them,
# and land on the one place left: inside a sequence, seventeen steps each
# costing a launch and a drain. This ablates that, and then the ground moved.
#
# THE INSTRUMENT. Voiding a kernel entirely charges it for what its readers
# save -- established here on 2026-09-02 and priced at a factor of twelve.
# The confound cancels between two builds that both write nothing:
#
#   body voided       norm, rotate, place and combine return at the top of
#                     main; every dispatch still recorded and still issued
#   dispatch skipped  the recorder jumps past those four steps; the barriers
#                     around them stay
#
# Both leave the same unwritten regions downstream, so the difference is the
# launching and draining and nothing else. Three alternated rounds:
#
#   as it is           0.744   0.813   0.778 s
#   body voided        0.679   0.742   0.747
#   dispatch skipped   0.726   0.733   0.760
#
# Nine milliseconds between the last two: issuing those four dispatches is
# about one per cent of the prompt. Thirty-six between the first two: what
# the four kernels do is about five, and that one is an upper bound because
# the readers confound is in it.
#
# So the small dispatches are not it either, and with the count, the barriers
# and the depth already refused there is no orchestration candidate left.
#
# BECAUSE THERE WAS NOTHING TO FIND. Partway through this sitting, clean tree
# and no rebuild between, the same binary went from 1.08 s to 0.78. Six
# alternated rounds against llama.cpp afterwards, tokens a second:
#
#   this program   1910  1884  1801  1895  1743  1817     median 1850
#   llama.cpp      1812  1763  1760  1809  1762  1754     median 1763
#
# Ahead in five of six. THE ENTRY ABOVE -- nine per cent more device time and
# twenty-two per cent idle -- IS WITHDRAWN. Every reading behind it was taken
# inside the slow window.
#
# AND WHAT MOVES IS OURS, NOT THEIRS:
#
#                  slow window   fast window   swing
#   this program      1284 t/s      1850 t/s    44 %
#   llama.cpp         1815          1763         3 %
#
# So a device ratio here is not a fact about the two programs unless it says
# which window it was taken in. This file has now had three device
# conclusions in three sittings invalidated by the same thing, and the rule
# that follows is stronger than any of them: DO NOT PUBLISH A DEVICE RATIO
# FROM ONE WINDOW. Take it in two, at least, and say so.
#
# THE FED SHARE IS NOT FIT FOR ARITHMETIC. Inside this one sitting it read 58
# per cent on a 0.743 s run and 85 on a 0.753 s one. The sums the last two
# entries did with it are worth nothing. It stays what it was offered as -- a
# flag for starved against fed -- and its window still wants narrowing to the
# evaluation.

# 2026-09-07  What flips the window, and it is the display controller
# -------------------------------------------------------------------
#
# Twelve causes excluded across three sittings and four ablations that found
# nothing, because the thing that moves a device prompt here by forty per
# cent is not in the compute path. The kernel had been saying so all along:
#
#   amdgpu 0000:c6:00.0: [drm] REG_WAIT timeout 1us * 100 tries
#     - dcn31_program_compbuf_size line:141
#
# DCN 3.1 is the display controller, compbuf is its compression buffer, and
# REG_WAIT timeout means the programming did not take. Twenty-four of them in
# three days, in exactly two shapes: PAIRS about eleven seconds apart, and
# SINGLES. The spacing inside a pair is the same every time -- 32/43, 52:57
# and 53:08, 05:10:52 and 05:11:03, 20:23:16 and 20:23:28, 08:42:37 and
# 08:42:48 -- which reads like a fixed sequence rather than chance. This host
# has two HDMI outputs connected and enabled.
#
# EVERY DATABLE FLIP LINES UP:
#
#   Sep 04 14:12:32 + 14:12:43   pair     slow: 0.829 s morning, 1.155 evening
#   Sep 06 19:48:10              single   fast: 0.755 to 0.760
#   Sep 06 20:52:57 + 20:53:08   pair     SLOW: 1.075 to 1.131, two hours
#   Sep 06 23:07:28              single   FAST: 0.744 to 0.814
#   Sep 07 00:00:11              single   fast
#   Sep 07 02:22:44              single   fast, watched live
#
# Six for six: a pair precedes the slow window, a single leaves it fast.
#
# THE ONE WATCHED LIVE. A detector took the 1419-token device prompt once a
# minute for half an hour, and 02:22:44 fell in the middle of it:
#
#   02:21:33  0.697      02:23:44  0.727
#   02:22:37  0.710      02:24:48  0.733
#
# A single, and no flip. That confirms the negative half of the rule only --
# no pair occurred while anything was watching, and until one does this is a
# correlation with six points and not a mechanism.
#
# AND THE SAME HALF HOUR SAYS WHAT KIND OF THING THE WINDOW IS. Thirty
# readings a minute apart: twenty-nine between 0.692 and 0.773 s and one at
# 1.054, against a dozen consecutive readings at 1.08 that afternoon. THE
# SLOW WINDOW IS A STATE AND NOT A SPREAD -- it arrives, it stays for hours,
# it leaves. The single stray reading has no display event within two hours
# of it and is something else again.
#
# WHAT IT IS NOT. Not the display taking memory bandwidth: the aggregate
# reads 45.5 GB/s in the fast state against 45.1 in the slow, and one thread
# reads 42.3 against 35.9 -- more in the fast state, which is the wrong sign
# for a fixed reservation. Not thermal, not the processor's clock or
# governor, not the load, not device or system memory pressure, not the page
# cache, not the binary, not the shader cache, not prior processor or device
# work, not the fence spin, the submission count, the pipeline depth, the
# batch size or the small dispatches. All of those were measured.
#
# THE TEST THAT WOULD SETTLE IT is to toggle a display off and on while a
# loop takes the 1419-token device prompt once a minute, and watch the wall
# step from 0.75 to 1.08 and stay. It could not be run here: kscreen-doctor
# and wlr-randr are not installed, so a reconfiguration cannot be triggered
# from a shell, and blanking somebody's screen to see what happens is not a
# thing to do to their desktop uninvited.
#
# IF IT HOLDS, the rule to carry beside a device figure is not about load or
# clock or temperature. It is: look at what the screen did.

# 2026-09-07  The display-controller finding, withdrawn
# -----------------------------------------------------
#
# The entry above says a dcn31_program_compbuf_size timeout flips the device
# prompt between 0.75 s and 1.08 -- pairs of the event before the slow
# window, singles leaving it fast, six for six. It is wrong, and one sentence
# disposes of it.
#
#   LLAMA.CPP IS NOT AFFECTED BY THE WINDOW. It read 1815 tokens a second in
#   the slow sitting and 1763 in the fast one, alternated against this
#   program in the same minutes.
#
# A display controller shares one memory controller with everything on the
# die. Whatever it does to this program it does to llama.cpp as well. A cause
# on shared hardware cannot be selective, and this one is selective.
#
# The same argument disposes of the clock, the power budget, the thermal
# state and the memory bandwidth -- every one of which was excluded here by
# measurement, over three sittings, and every one of which could have been
# excluded by this argument on the first day. That is the lesson worth more
# than the twelve exclusions: ASK WHAT THE CAUSE MUST EXPLAIN BEFORE
# MEASURING WHETHER IT DOES. The window is selective; a shared cause is not.
#
# The correlation was thin on its own terms too. Twenty-four events in
# seventy-two hours is one every three hours, so almost any moment has one an
# hour or two behind it. Twenty-nine of thirty readings are fast, so "a
# single, and it stayed fast" is a description of the background. What was
# left was two pairs before two slow windows: two points.
#
# WHAT SURVIVES IS THE CONSTRAINT. Whatever flips the window is something
# that differs between two processes on one machine in the same minutes --
# this program's code, its shaders, its allocations, or driver state that
# belongs to its own context. It is not the part, the memory, the clock or
# the screen. Every cause excluded so far was excluded for being shared; the
# search should have been narrowed to what is not, and was not.
#
# The awkward fact for that constraint is the persistence: the state survives
# this program exiting and starting again. That leaves something on disk or
# something the driver keeps per-device rather than per-context. The shader
# cache was cleared in the slow window and it stayed slow, which rules out
# the obvious member of the first class.
#
# AND ONE MEASUREMENT SURVIVES, because it does not depend on the cause: a
# detector took the 1419-token device prompt once a minute for half an hour
# and read twenty-nine between 0.692 and 0.773 s and one at 1.054, against a
# dozen consecutive at 1.08 that afternoon. The slow window is a state and
# not a spread. That is what makes one sitting's device ratio worthless.

# 2026-09-07  The exclusions redone against selectivity
# ------------------------------------------------------
#
# The entry above establishes the constraint: llama.cpp is not affected by
# the window, so the cause is not shared hardware. That disposes at once of
# the clock, the power budget, the thermal state, the memory bandwidth, the
# load and the page cache -- every one of which was excluded here by
# measurement, one sitting at a time. What remains is what differs between
# two processes on one machine in the same minutes.
#
# PLACEMENT, EXCLUDED PROPERLY. /proc/<pid>/fdinfo while each ran the prompt:
#
#                    drm-memory-vram    drm-memory-gtt
#   this program           80,032 KiB      1,223,784 KiB
#   llama.cpp              71,720 KiB      1,187,004 KiB
#
# The same, and both mostly in system memory through GTT. The earlier
# exclusion of "memory placement" used machine-wide totals; this one uses a
# number that belongs to the process, which is what the constraint asks for.
#
# THE QUEUE: A REAL SELECTIVE DIFFERENCE. This device's families:
#
#   0   graphics | compute | transfer | sparse    1 queue
#   1   compute | transfer | sparse               4 queues
#   2   sparse                                    1 queue
#
# This program takes the first family with the compute bit -- family 0, the
# universal queue, the one a desktop compositor draws on. llama.cpp calls
# ggml_vk_find_queue_family_index with compute required and graphics avoided
# and gets family 1. One program shares a queue with the screen and the other
# does not. That is the shape a selective cause has to have, and it is the
# only difference of that shape found.
#
# AND IT IS NOT THE CAUSE, on the one test that could be run. The change is
# four lines -- prefer a compute family without the graphics bit, fall back
# to any -- and it was made. Digest unchanged. Level in a fast window:
#
#   family 0    0.745   0.767   0.788 s
#   family 1    0.747   0.774   0.807 s
#
# Then family 0 was loaded from another process with work chosen to occupy
# the queue and compute almost nothing -- a small model generating on the
# device. If the queue were the mechanism the compute-family build would be
# spared:
#
#                        quiet      under a family-0 load
#   family 0             0.742 s    0.845   0.792 s
#   family 1             0.741 s    0.835   0.854 s
#
# Both lose about a tenth and neither is spared. REVERTED: a change whose
# only argument is a hypothesis its own test did not support is an unmeasured
# change, and one of those was published here already today.
#
# WHAT IS LEFT IS ALLOCATION GRANULARITY. One prompt:
#
#   this program   219 AMDGPU_GEM_CREATE
#   llama.cpp       60
#
# Three and a half times the objects for the same gigabyte. It is
# per-process, it is the kind of thing that would be sensitive to how
# fragmented the GTT is, and fragmentation persists across a process exiting
# and starting again -- which the window does, and which no other candidate
# found so far does. UNTESTED: this sitting found no way to fragment the GTT
# deliberately.

# 2026-09-07  Allocation granularity, tested and not it
# ------------------------------------------------------
#
# The last candidate that satisfied the selectivity constraint: this program
# makes 219 AMDGPU_GEM_CREATE calls for one prompt where llama.cpp makes 60
# -- one buffer for every weight matrix against a few large ones
# suballocated. Per-process, and fragmentation is the only mechanism found
# that would survive a process exiting and starting again, which the window
# does.
#
# SEQUENTIALLY. Eighteen loads of assorted models on the device -- 169 MB,
# 461, 608, 638, 747 -- each allocating its own two hundred objects and
# freeing them on exit:
#
#   before   0.733   0.743   0.764 s
#   after    0.757   0.791   0.800 s
#
# About five per cent. Not a forty per cent flip, and inside what this row
# does on its own.
#
# CONCURRENTLY, which is what actually fragments an allocator. Three rounds
# of four processes each holding a different model on the device at once,
# killed out of order so their frees interleave -- twelve allocations of
# about a gigabyte in two hundred pieces each, torn down to leave holes:
#
#   before   0.751   0.745   0.802 s
#   after    0.752   0.816   0.799 s
#
# Nothing. The allocation count is a real difference between the two programs
# and it is not the trigger, on the two ways available here.
#
# AND THAT IS WHERE THIS STOPS. The constraint left four candidates:
#
#   placement     excluded by a per-process measurement (fdinfo)
#   queue         a real difference, family 0 against family 1, but a load
#                 aimed at family 0 spared neither build
#   granularity   the two tests above
#   shaders       excluded before the constraint was found: four
#                 orchestration ablations, identical digests, and the same
#                 device-busy time as llama.cpp on the same work
#
# The window is unexplained. Three sittings and two retractions, and the
# useful part of it is not a cause but a discipline: DO NOT PUBLISH A DEVICE
# RATIO FROM ONE WINDOW.
#
# WHAT IS WORTH BUILDING does not depend on the cause. The fed share wants
# its sampling window narrowed to the evaluation rather than the whole run,
# and tests benchmark still prints a load average against a bound measured in
# busy processors. Both sit inside figure-group fingerprints, so both want a
# measurement sitting -- and a verified-stable window, of the kind this
# sitting's detector demonstrated, is exactly when to take one.

# 2026-09-07  A measurement sitting: two instruments, seven groups
# ----------------------------------------------------------------
#
# Taken in a window verified stable before anything was measured: four
# readings of the 1419-token device prompt at 0.753, 0.761, 0.763 and 0.768
# s, and no display events for an hour.
#
# TWO INSTRUMENTS FIXED, which is what required the sitting: both files sit
# inside figure-group fingerprints.
#
#   The fed share now samples the last pass only. It read 31 per cent at one
#   repeat, 72 at three, 86 at nine and 87 at fifteen on a run whose wall did
#   not move -- the first pass uploads the weights and builds the pipelines
#   with the part idle, and every pass was counted, so two figures taken with
#   different repeat counts could not be compared. The last pass is warm
#   whatever the count is: 85, 88 and 89 across three, nine and fifteen, and
#   32 for a single pass that has no warm one to prefer.
#
#   tests benchmark now names the instrument it refused on -- "the machine
#   has 3.39 processors busy, above the 1.50" -- where it printed the
#   minute's average against a bound measured in busy processors.
#
# WHAT MOVED, and all of it in one sitting:
#
#   seven-worker 1419 prompt      9.540 s  ->  5.221 s
#   a token's modelled cost       15.911 ms -> 11.371 ms
#   the same at --arith f32       4.9x     ->  6.9x
#   a prompt token, 32 a pass     5.453 ms ->  3.860 ms
#   reference against cpu         62x      ->  67x
#   generating saturates at       four shares -> three
#   twelve tokens                 0.374 s  ->  0.370 s
#   q2_k twelve tokens            1.508 s  ->  1.414 s
#   device 1419 prompt            1730.5 t/s -> 1835.7, against 1814.1
#
# The seven-worker row is this month's kernel work arriving in the table that
# measures it. The device long prompt is level with llama.cpp and slightly
# ahead for the first time.
#
# AND THE AMDAHL FIT IS WITHDRAWN. The worker table read a fifteen per cent
# serial fraction off its own first two rows. A worker count is not a core
# count here -- the submitting task takes a share, so one worker is one core
# and two workers is three:
#
#   workers   wall       processor   cores busy   speedup
#   1         25.309 s   25.90 s     1.02         1.00x
#   2          8.703      26.56      3.05         2.91x
#   4          6.245      31.56      5.05         4.05x
#   7          5.221      40.16      7.66         4.85x
#
# Fitting Amdahl across the first step returns a negative serial fraction,
# which is what fitting a curve to two different quantities does. The table
# now carries the cores-busy column so the arithmetic can be checked, and the
# paragraph that derived a 54/46 split from the old fit says instead what the
# rows support.
#
# THE DEVICE SHORT PROMPT reads 1195.7 t/s, the lowest of the twelve readings
# this file has of it, and its own section explains why: a tenth of a second
# never brings the part above about 1.5 GHz, and this sitting's line says
# 1021 MHz of 2700. The long prompt is the row every change is judged on.

# 2026-09-07  llama.cpp's mat-vec shape, built two ways and refused
# -----------------------------------------------------------------
#
# The gap the sitting above leaves is the device's generated token: 50.8
# tokens a second against llama.cpp's 55.5. This file's own ablation says
# where that token is -- void row_product and sixty-four tokens go from 1.211
# s to 0.129, so 89 per cent of it is that one kernel -- and both sides are
# bound by the weight bytes, about 56 GB/s here against 61 there.
#
# THE TWO SHAPES:
#
#   llama.cpp   a whole subgroup on a row-group, K_PER_ITER = 8 consecutive
#               elements a thread, NUM_ROWS accumulators, subgroupAdd for
#               the reduction and shared memory only where subgroups are
#               missing
#   here        eight lanes on a row, each taking every eighth block,
#               reduced through shared memory and one workgroup barrier
#
# At eight lanes a row a sixty-four-wide wave here covers eight rows, so it
# walks eight disjoint streams of about 270 bytes where theirs walks one
# contiguous stream. One long stream is friendlier to DRAM than eight short
# ones. That is a real mechanism, and it is not this one.
#
# THE REDUCTION ALONE. subgroupClusteredAdd over clusters of eight, in place
# of the shared array and the barrier -- which the shader's own note declines
# on the grounds that it needs a Vulkan 1.1 instance, though this program
# already ships attention_subgroups compiled that way and picks it at run
# time:
#
#   shared memory, as it is    1.241   1.275   1.275 s
#   clustered subgroup add     1.317   1.309   1.317 s
#
# Three per cent slower, worse in three of three. The barrier was never the
# cost: one at the end of a workgroup, not one a block. The clustered add
# spends three shuffle-and-add steps on all sixty-four lanes where the shared
# path spends one store on each and eight loads on one lane in eight.
#
# AND THE STREAMS, which the reduction was only the enabler for. With the
# clustered add paying for a wide reduction the lane count sweeps freely, and
# the host constant Row_Lanes moves with it so both sides agree:
#
#   eight, as it is             1.243   1.280 s
#   sixteen                     1.274   1.274
#   thirty-two                  1.323   1.325
#   sixty-four, one row a wave  1.271   1.283
#
# Nothing at any width, and thirty-two is worse. Digest 448c2ed68ec342ee
# throughout both halves, and both reverted.
#
# WHAT THAT SAYS ABOUT THE EIGHT-LANE SHAPE is worth keeping. It was arrived
# at here by measurement -- one invocation a row spent transactions it did
# not have; dividing inside a block decoded the scale eight times -- and the
# shape llama.cpp arrived at by its own route is not better on this part.
#
# SO THE DEVICE'S GENERATED TOKEN KEEPS ITS NINE PER CENT, and the three
# things that could have explained it are now all measured:
#
#   the small kernels    0.53 ms of work and 0.16 of dispatch a token,
#                        against a token of 19.7 ms
#   the submissions      23 a token here against llama.cpp's 28
#   the kernel shape     above
#
# What is left is the arithmetic inside the kernel, and there is no reading
# here that says it is wrong.

# 2026-09-07  llama.cpp's per-op profile, and the workgroup width
# ---------------------------------------------------------------
#
# GGML_VK_PERF_LOGGER=1 makes llama.cpp print what each operation of a
# generated token cost it. One token of TinyLlama Q8_0 on this part:
#
#   MUL_MAT_VEC m=5632 k=2048           44 x 190.1 us   8364 us   42 %
#   MUL_MAT_ADD MUL_MAT_VEC m=2048 k=5632  22 x 189.3   4166      21 %
#   MUL_MAT_VEC m=2048 k=2048           23 x  72.5      1668      8.4 %
#   MUL_MAT_ADD MUL_MAT_VEC m=2048 k=2048  21 x  79.0   1660      8.3 %
#   MUL_MAT_VEC m=32000 k=2048           1 x 1026.8     1027      5.2 %
#   FLASH_ATTN_EXT                      22 x  51.0      1122      5.6 %
#   MUL_MAT_VEC m=256 k=2048            44 x  13.3       586      2.9 %
#   RMS_NORM_MUL                        45 x  12.9       582      2.9 %
#   ROPE, ROPE_VIEW_SET_ROWS, SET_ROWS, GLU, ADD          730      3.7 %
#
# TWO THINGS FALL OUT THAT THIS FILE HAD BEEN GUESSING AT.
#
#   Its products are 88 per cent of a token; the ablation here puts
#   row_product at 89. The two programs spend their tokens the same way.
#
#   Its small operations are 6.6 per cent of a token against this program's
#   3.5, measured the same way. The fusion it has and this does not --
#   RMS_NORM_MUL, ROPE_VIEW_SET_ROWS, GLU -- is not a thing this program is
#   losing to; it is already ahead there.
#
# THE ONE SHAPE THAT STANDS OUT is m=256 at 78.7 GFLOP/s where the others
# reach 115 to 128 -- the key and value projections, where a narrow output is
# an occupancy problem. On paper it is worse here: a 256-wide workgroup at
# eight lanes a row covers thirty-two rows, so m=256 is eight workgroups on
# twelve compute units, where llama.cpp on AMD always takes a workgroup of
# one subgroup, one row, and gets two hundred and fifty-six. Its heuristic
# for widening workgroups at small m is guarded to NVIDIA and Intel.
#
# BUILT AND REFUSED. The width is a specialization constant already, so this
# is one number on the pipeline and one on the dispatch that sizes it:
#
#   256, as it is         1.223   1.279   1.280 s
#   128                   1.278   1.307   1.316
#   64, one subgroup      1.315   1.323   1.315
#
# Narrower is worse, monotonically. Whatever the idle compute units cost on
# the key and value projections is less than the wider workgroup buys
# everywhere else, and those projections are 2.9 per cent of a token in
# llama.cpp's own profile.
#
# AND THE FIRST VERSION OF THAT TABLE WAS A LIE THE DIGEST CAUGHT. Setting
# the specialization without moving the dispatch divisor left the kernel
# computing a quarter of the rows:
#
#   256                   1.213   1.277   1.277 s   448c2ed68ec342ee
#   128, divisor unmoved  0.719   0.757   0.772     c7985f314505888a
#   64,  divisor unmoved  0.394   0.432   0.439     73bc61a56838f703
#
# A three-fold win and a different answer. That is what every table on this
# page carries a digest for.

# 2026-09-07  The kernel width llama.cpp has and this did not
# -----------------------------------------------------------
#
# llama.cpp compiles its mat-vec once for every column count from one to
# eight and indexes them by it -- mul_mat_vec_max_cols = 8, and the pipeline
# array is [wg][type][num_cols-1]. This program had three widths: one, eight
# and sixteen. A round of two sequences ran on the eight-wide kernel, which
# carries eight accumulators whatever it is given and reads the activation
# for all of them.
#
# IT SHOWS IN THE ROUND CURVE as a step the weights do not explain:
#
#   one member     21.5 ms a round
#   two members    29.3 ms a round
#
# over weights that are read once either way.
#
# A FOUR-WIDE KERNEL. Three alternated rounds, 1419-token prompt, 32 rounds,
# and the mark unchanged at one, two, three, four, five, eight and sixteen:
#
#   members   eight-wide                four-wide
#   two       0.931  0.972  0.968 s     0.715  0.736  0.736 s     23 %
#   three     0.976  0.992  0.995       0.773  0.768  0.774       22 %
#   four      1.028  1.032  1.032       0.815  0.815  0.814       21 %
#
# Better in nine of nine. One, eight and sixteen members move within noise
# and a plain generated token still reads 448c2ed68ec342ee. Against llama.cpp
# measured in the same sitting, two sequences go from 1.42 times behind to
# 1.09, and four from 1.32 to 1.04.
#
# AND THE SIZE LIMIT MADE IT A BETTER CHANGE THAN IT STARTED AS. A fourth
# compiled copy of row_product.comp put the generated Ada over the megabyte
# this repository accepts:
#
#   Row_Product  96.0 KB     Row_Quad    96.0 KB
#   Row_Wide     96.0 KB     Row_Single  95.9 KB
#   file 1054.5 KB against a limit of 1024.0
#
# Four copies of the same fifteen format decoders, differing by one integer.
# So the width became a specialization constant instead --
# layout(constant_id = 1) const uint GROUP -- fixed when the pipeline is
# made, so the loops still unroll and the arrays still have a constant size.
# One module now serves every width, five pipelines are made from it where
# five were made from four modules, and the file is 767 KB.
#
# That is llama.cpp's idea, a kernel per column count, arrived at from the
# other end: this repository's own size rule is what pushed it there.

# 2026-09-07  A row pipeline for every count, and the curve that went backwards
# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
#
# The entry above makes the row kernel's width a specialization constant.
# That is what made this one nearly free: llama.cpp compiles its mat-vec once
# for every column count from one to eight, and with the width a constant the
# pipeline is told, so can this -- one vkCreateComputePipeline each and not a
# word more of SPIR-V.
#
# WHAT THE GAPS COST, per round of a 1419-token prompt with four widths:
#
#   members    a round    tokens a second
#   four       25.3 ms    158.2
#   five       33.3 ms    150.1
#   six        34.2 ms    175.5
#
# A column is worth about 1.3 ms inside a width and the fifth cost eight,
# because five sequences ran on the eight-wide kernel and paid for eight.
# FIVE MEMBERS CAME OUT SLOWER THAN FOUR, which is the kind of thing a curve
# says out loud.
#
# A PIPELINE FOR EACH COUNT, three alternated rounds, every mark unchanged at
# one through eight and at sixteen:
#
#   members   four widths              one for each
#   two       0.720  0.736  0.738 s    0.700  0.702  0.719 s     3 %
#   five      1.061  1.069  1.070      0.868  0.871  0.871      18 %
#   six       1.098  1.105  1.107      0.994  0.999  1.001     9.5 %
#   seven     1.161  1.169  1.180      1.091  1.094  1.099     6.5 %
#
# Better in twelve of twelve, and one, four, eight and sixteen move within
# noise.
#
# AND THE CURVE IS A CURVE AGAIN:
#
#   members    1     2      3      4      5      6      7      8     16
#   t/s      46.4  93.0  130.8  157.6  180.4  193.0  204.9  213.5  355.1
#
# Five members is 180.4 where it was 150.1, and nothing between one and
# sixteen falls below the count before it.

# 2026-09-07  Per-count widths above eight, refused; and a step at seventeen
# --------------------------------------------------------------------------
#
# Filling in a row pipeline for every count from one to eight was worth
# eighteen per cent at five members, so the obvious next move is nine to
# fifteen, which all take the sixteen-wide kernel and pay for sixteen.
#
# IT BUYS NOTHING. Three alternated rounds, marks unchanged:
#
#   members   to eight                  to sixteen
#   nine      1.208  1.225  1.213 s     1.220  1.214  1.220 s
#   ten       1.269  1.280  1.261       1.251  1.266  1.281
#   twelve    1.333  1.326  1.355       1.318  1.337  1.327
#   fifteen   1.439  1.405  1.409       1.393  1.422  1.412
#
# Fully overlapping. A probe printing the kernel decision for every step says
# why: above eight the products that matter go through the MATRIX kernel
# rather than the row one, so the row kernel's width stops being what the
# count is paying for. Reverted.
#
# WHAT THE PROBE FOUND INSTEAD IS A STEP AT SEVENTEEN. Milliseconds a token,
# three rounds each:
#
#   members            15     16     17     18
#   1419-token prompt  2.89   2.86   2.10   1.97
#   110-token prompt   2.18   2.01   1.88   1.78
#   6-token prompt     2.22   2.04   1.93   1.85
#
# Twenty-six per cent, and only with a long prompt. The short-prompt rows
# walk down smoothly through the same counts, so it is not the products and
# not the round driver: it is ATTENTION OVER THE CACHE, which is the only
# thing a long prompt makes big.
#
# TWO CANDIDATES ARE ALREADY EXCLUDED:
#
#   the product kernel   a probe printing the matrix-or-row decision for
#                        every step gives the same split at sixteen and at
#                        seventeen
#   the submissions      seventeen makes 5266 AMDGPU_CS calls against
#                        sixteen's 4462, and seventeen is the faster one
#
# AND READING THE CHOOSER TURNS UP A CONSTRAINT WORTH ITS OWN LINE.
# Attend_Kernel opens with
#
#   if not Rounding and then Attends_By_Matrix (...)
#
# so A ROUND NEVER TAKES THE MATRIX ATTENTION KERNEL AT ALL, whatever its
# size: it falls to the bundled one or the halved one. That is not the
# sixteen-to-seventeen step, which happens inside the rounds, but it is a
# whole kernel the many-sequence case cannot reach. llama.cpp answers the
# same case with flash attention and a split-k reduce. It is the first thing
# to look at next.

# 2026-09-07  Why a round cannot take the matrix attention kernel
# ---------------------------------------------------------------
#
# The entry above calls Attend_Kernel's "if not Rounding" guard a whole
# kernel the many-sequence case cannot reach, as though it were arbitrary
# and waiting to be lifted. It is structural, and the shader says so in one
# line. attention_matrix.comp walks the cache once for a whole block of
# query rows:
#
#   for (uint base = first_min; base <= last_max; base += BC) {
#       ...  k_at + (base + n * 16u) * shape.kv_width + d0 ...
#
# ONE KEY TILE, staged into shared memory, shared by all BR rows of the
# block. That is the point of it: sixteen queries against one cache, which
# is what a prompt is.
#
# A ROUND IS THE OTHER SHAPE. Every row is a different member with its own
# cache at its own base -- which is what the per-row table at the end of the
# cache buffer exists to say -- so the rows of a block cannot share a tile.
# Lifting the guard would mean one row a block, and a sixteen-by-sixteen
# matrix instruction with one useful row is not worth reaching.
#
# So the guard is right and the entry above is corrected. What the
# many-sequence case wants is the other kernel: one query against a long
# cache, parallelized over the cache rather than over the queries. That is
# llama.cpp's flash_attn_split_k_reduce.comp and it has no counterpart here.
#
# AND THE STEP AT SEVENTEEN IS NARROWED BUT STILL OPEN. It is per round
# rather than per prompt:
#
#   max-tokens    16 members    17 members
#   1             0.046 s       0.049 s
#   4             0.176         0.149
#   32            1.418         1.162
#
# At one round the two are level and seventeen is the slower, which is the
# seventeenth member's own work and nothing else. The marginal round costs
# 43 milliseconds at sixteen members and 33 at seventeen.
#
# EXCLUDED SO FAR, each by measurement or by reading:
#
#   the product kernel     a probe printing the decision for every step
#                          gives the same matrix-and-row split at both
#   the submission count   5266 AMDGPU_CS at seventeen against 4462 at
#                          sixteen, and seventeen is the faster one
#   the row width          per-count pipelines above eight change nothing
#   the attention kernel   Rounding is Table > 0, so a round of sixteen and
#                          a round of seventeen choose the same kernel
#   query blocking         QUERIES is one in every kernel a round can take
#   the prompt phase       at one round the two counts are level

# 2026-09-07  The split-k attention kernel, built and refused
# -----------------------------------------------------------
#
# llama.cpp has flash_attn_split_k_reduce.comp and this had no counterpart:
# one query against a long cache, cut into slices with a workgroup each and a
# reduce to put them back together. The entry above names it as the thing the
# many-sequence case wants. It was built, it is correct, and it is worth
# nothing on this part.
#
# THE ARGUMENT FOR IT was arithmetic. A generated token at a 1419-token
# context attends over about thirty-two megabytes of cache -- twenty-two
# layers, keys and values, two hundred and fifty-six components, two bytes --
# and takes 2.3 milliseconds doing it. That is fourteen gigabytes a second
# where the part gives sixty, and one query is one workgroup a head, thirty-
# two of them on twelve compute units.
#
# WHAT WAS BUILT:
#
#   attention.comp with SPLIT_K   the slice from gl_WorkGroupID.z; the walk
#                                 covers that slice's share of the range,
#                                 rounded to a whole lane's width; instead of
#                                 dividing it writes the unnormalized blend
#                                 with its own maximum and denominator
#   attention_join.comp           takes the largest maximum, rescales each
#                                 slice by exp (m - M), divides once
#   the partials                  a region of the result buffer bound at
#                                 four, with a barrier between the two
#                                 dispatches
#
# IT IS RIGHT. Digest 7ec6b755e53e16b4 at one, four, eight, sixteen and
# thirty-two slices: the same answer the unsplit walk gives, at every count.
#
# AND IT BUYS NOTHING, sixty-four tokens at a 1419-token context:
#
#   no split       1.361   1.399 s
#   four slices    1.386   1.388
#   eight          1.388   1.391
#   sixteen        1.395   1.392
#   thirty-two     1.415   1.416
#
# Level at four and slowly worse above it, which is the join's own dispatch
# being paid for and nothing bought. Reverted.
#
# THE PREMISE WAS WRONG AND THE MEASUREMENT IS WHAT SAYS SO. Thirty-two
# workgroups is not thirty-two threads: each is a workgroup's width, so the
# walk already had some eight thousand invocations for twelve compute units.
# The part was never short of work to schedule, and whatever holds attention
# to fourteen gigabytes a second -- the dependent read of a key before its
# score, the serial shape of a running softmax -- is not a thing more
# workgroups fix. COUNTING WORKGROUPS AND CALLING IT OCCUPANCY IS THE
# MISTAKE.
#
# TWO THINGS LEARNED ON THE WAY, both worth writing down.
#
#   The fifth binding in a sequence is a WINDOW on the result buffer --
#   Offset => Places (Index).At_Byte, Extent => that step's own room -- not
#   the buffer itself. An absolute offset into it is meaningless and out of
#   bounds, which is what the first wrong answer was.
#
#   The second wrong answer was an edit that silently did nothing: a fallback
#   in the patching script dedented its anchor, so the inner replacement no
#   longer matched and the write-out was never changed. The shader compiled
#   with SPLIT_K still wrote the normalized answer and the join read a region
#   nothing had written. The digest caught it; making the join inert and
#   watching the answer come back right is what located it.

# 2026-09-08, all nine groups restamped for whitespace, NOTHING RE-MEASURED,
# and this is the case the file's own rule allows.
#
#   A plain `alr build` uses the development profile, which runs GNAT's
#   layout checks; the release profile does not. `tests check` reads every
#   .stderr under obj, so a tree that passed the gate after a release build
#   failed it after a development one, on twenty-six style warnings that had
#   been in the tree for as long as nobody had built it that way. Twelve
#   unindented statements in Model_Runner.Llama, four in
#   Platform.Device.Products, one in the tokenizer, one `else` three columns
#   out in the integer kernels, and eight doubled blank lines.
#
#   Every one of the twenty-six is whitespace. No token moved, no branch
#   changed, and the compiler's output is the same object code; what changed
#   is which column a statement starts in. The rule at the top of this file
#   asks for a re-measurement or a reason the change cannot have moved the
#   figures, and this is the second: an indentation cannot.
#
#   The tokenizer group is stale for the first time in this file's history,
#   and only because Model_Runner.Tokenizer is the one source it names.
#
#   WHAT IS WORTH KEEPING is that the gate was reading a build nobody made.
#   The release checklist and every sitting in this file build in release,
#   so the twenty-six had never been printed. They were found by running
#   `alr -n build` in the middle of a measuring sitting, which is not how
#   anyone intended to find them.
#
# load:  not applicable; no figure was taken
# model: none


# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
#
# 2026-09-08, the legacy four-bit format in eight-row panels, and a published
# rate corrected that had the wrong numerator.
#
#   `--repack rows` takes Q4_0 as well as the three k-quants, and a kernel
#   reads it: Rows_By_Panels_Q40. The figures are under `### The legacy
#   four-bit format in eight-row panels`.
#
#   Alternated three rounds against three in one sitting, --backend cpu,
#   TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q4_0:
#
#                        as stored              --repack rows        ratio
#     prompt, 110    0.564 0.565 0.559     0.251 0.260 0.250          2.23
#     prompt, 1419   8.458 8.109           4.011 3.988                2.04
#     generating 17  0.957 0.952 0.950     0.279 0.279 0.278          3.42
#     generating 64  3.900 3.885           1.177 1.182                3.30
#     loading        0.065                 0.400
#
#   Digests 61fda0268954d85b at 110 and 7ec6b755e53e16b4 at 1419, both
#   layouts, every round. Conformance 41780 sequences, 0 outside tolerance.
#   llama-bench at 95b8e33e1, eight threads, same file: pp110 508.17,
#   pp1419 400.69, tg64 72.94 -- so the prompt is 1.13 behind and the
#   generated token 1.34, from 18.3 and 4.4 two days ago.
#
#   THE GENERATED TOKEN IS WHERE IT PAYS AND THAT WAS NOT THE EXPECTATION.
#   The layout was built for prompts and on the k-quants it loses three per
#   cent generating. It buys 3.3 here because what it replaced was not a
#   strip kernel: with one vector Q4_0 had no kernel of its own and took the
#   generic four-row tile, which writes a block's unpacked nibbles to memory
#   before any assembly runs. The panel kernel never writes a quant at all.
#
#   A PUBLISHED RATE IS CORRECTED, and the mistake is one this file already
#   warned about. Yesterday's entry gave Q4_0's generating rows as 62.0 and
#   65.4 tokens a second, arrived at by dividing sixty-four by the seconds
#   the run took. The run stops at seventeen -- the model reaches its
#   end-of-sequence token there and `tests speed` prints the count on the
#   same line as the time. The seconds were right and the ratio between them
#   was right; the two rates and the two gaps to llama.cpp were the same
#   arithmetic with the wrong numerator. 16.5 and 17.4, and the gap four
#   times rather than one. The paragraph under `### The size argument` in
#   this file says of an earlier table that "three of the four runs did not
#   generate sixty-four tokens", which is the same trap named a month before
#   walking into it.
#
#   SEVEN GROUPS CONFIRMED, ONE RE-MEASURED. The panels group is the one the
#   change is in and its whole table was taken again. The other seven took
#   confirming pairs and every one landed inside what is published:
#
#     twelve tokens, six-token prompt   0.346, 0.346 s against 0.348
#     device, the same run              0.243 s against 0.242
#     q8 110 and 1419, cpu              0.292 and 4.332 against 0.286, 4.510
#                                       generating 1.683 against 1.689
#     q8 1419, device                   0.696 against 0.713
#     four tokens, cpu and reference    0.040 + 0.101 and 5.653 + 3.735
#                                       against 0.042 + 0.102, 5.611 + 3.671
#     drafting, two-bit and drafted     1.343 and 2.786 against 1.320, 2.968
#     the sixteen Row_Dot rows          every one within two per cent
#     shares, q8_0 and q4_k one vector  2585 to 14529 and 2655 to 14369
#                                       against 2630 to 14436, 2608 to 14501
#
#   AND ONE PUBLISHED CELL MOVED WITHOUT A CAUSE ANYONE CAN NAME. The
#   panels table's 1419-token `--repack rows` cell reads 3.517 and 3.549 s
#   here against a published 3.774 to 3.821, twice and outside the range,
#   while its stored column stayed inside. Nothing in this change touches
#   that path beyond one comparison per call. The likeliest explanation is
#   code layout -- a kernel was added to the same compilation unit -- and
#   the honest treatment is to leave the published cell as it was taken and
#   write the two later readings beside it, which is what the README does.
#
# covers: the four-row table under "The legacy four-bit format in eight-row
#   panels", the ratios, and the llama.cpp comparison beside them
# load:  under 1.10 before every reading
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q4_0, Q8_0, Q4_K_M, Q5_K_M and Q2_K


# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
#
# 2026-09-08, the four-bit format that keeps a minimum, in panels -- and the
# kernel beside it turned out to be a third slower than it needed to be.
#
#   Q4_1 is Q4_0's block with a second half-precision number in it. It is in
#   panels now and it is the first format here whose kernel exists only in
#   panels: the row-major tile carries one integer correction shared by
#   every row of it, and a per-row minimum is not that shape.
#   Has_Integer_Kernel takes the layout as a parameter for that reason.
#   The figures are under `### The four-bit format that keeps a minimum`.
#
#   The model is TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat requantized from the Q8_0 file with
#   llama-quantize at 95b8e33e1: 155 tensors Q4_1, output.weight Q6_K, the
#   norms F32 -- the same three-format shape the Q4_0 file has, which was
#   checked before the two were compared.
#
#   Alternated three rounds against three, --backend cpu:
#
#                        as stored              --repack rows        ratio
#     prompt, 110    4.217 4.123 4.188     0.182 0.177 0.175         23.5
#     prompt, 1419   57.300 57.592         2.905 2.926               19.7
#     generating 64  6.027 6.035 5.983     1.124 1.125 1.125          5.36
#
#   Twenty-three times because the stored column is not a slower kernel but
#   none: Q4_1 had no integer path at all.
#
#   llama-bench at 95b8e33e1, eight threads, same file: pp110 171.79,
#   pp1419 146.18, tg64 62.18 -- so this engine is 3.62 and 3.34 times
#   AHEAD on the two prompts and 1.09 behind generating. LLAMA.CPP DOES NOT
#   REPACK THIS FORMAT: repack.cpp builds block_q4_0x8, block_q4_Kx8,
#   block_iq4_nlx4 and block_q2_Kx8 and has nothing for Q4_1, which is why
#   it reads this file at 171.8 where it reads the Q4_0 one at 508.2. The
#   comparison is the eight-row layout against no layout and should be
#   quoted that way rather than as one kernel against another.
#
#   THE DIGESTS DIVERGE AT 1419 AND THAT NEEDED AN ANSWER RATHER THAN A
#   SHRUG. The two columns are two arithmetics -- binary32 against quantized
#   activations -- so at 110 tokens both give a038bbc489c2783c and at 1419
#   they give 00f047036c93974f and 8315b2efdbed5f7a, each stable across
#   rounds. Q4_0's two paths do NOT diverge at that length, so the asymmetry
#   was checked rather than assumed: a new test,
#   Panel_Minimum_Term_Is_Exact, isolates the minimum on a matrix whose
#   blocks are constant, where the encoder's range is zero, every quant is
#   nought and the minimum is the whole answer. Activations constant a block
#   quantize without loss, so the two paths must agree to floating-point
#   rounding. They agree to 6E-6. The test was checked by breaking it:
#   doubling the term fails it by 19306.
#
# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
#
#   AND A LOAD AT THE HEAD OF A DEPENDENCY CHAIN WAS WORTH A THIRD OF THE
#   Q4_0 KERNEL. Building Q4_1 beside it made the two comparable and the
#   comparison said something was wrong: Q4_1 does strictly more work per
#   block -- an extra widening, an extra multiply-accumulate -- and read the
#   1419-token prompt in 2.92 s against Q4_0's 3.94.
#
#   The difference was the first instruction. Q4_0 seeded its accumulator
#   with the centring correction, vpbroadcastd from the band table, and then
#   ran eight vpdpbusd into that register. Eight multiply-accumulates into
#   one register is a chain of eight and a LOAD AT ITS HEAD ADDS THE LOAD'S
#   LATENCY TO ALL OF THEM; Q4_1 had nothing to seed with, so it started
#   from a zeroing idiom, which is resolved at rename and costs nothing.
#
#   Moved into the floating-point finish -- one multiply-accumulate against
#   the row scales the insertion already holds, which is the shape Q4_1's
#   minimum has:
#
#     1419-token prompt, --repack rows
#       correction as the accumulator's seed    3.943, 3.968 s
#       correction in the floating-point finish 2.945, 2.909 s
#
#   1.35 times, digest 07edf8ee9d143975 both ways, and it puts Q4_0 level
#   with Q4_1 at 2.94 against 2.94 -- which is what two kernels this alike
#   should read and is the check that the explanation is the right one.
#
#   THE Q4_0 PANEL TABLE PUBLISHED THIS MORNING IS RE-MEASURED for that
#   reason, not restamped: 0.568/0.571/0.560 to 0.177/0.179/0.180 at 110
#   tokens, 8.017 to 3.011 at 1419, 0.896 to 0.276 for seventeen generated
#   and 3.788 to 1.165 for sixty-four. Against llama.cpp its prompt is now
#   614.5 t/s against 508.2 and 471.3 against 400.7 -- a fifth AHEAD, where
#   this morning it was 1.13 behind.
#
#   NOTHING IN A PROFILE SAYS "this load is at the head of a chain". The
#   kernel's instruction count was lower and its time was higher, and that
#   only became visible because a second kernel of nearly the same shape
#   existed to compare it against. Building the second thing is what priced
#   the first, and that is the transferable part.
#
#   SEVEN GROUPS CONFIRMED, ONE RE-MEASURED. Confirming pairs, all inside:
#
#     twelve tokens, six-token prompt   0.347 s against 0.348
#     device, the same run              0.244 s against 0.242
#     q8 110 and 1419, cpu              0.283 and 4.367 against 0.286, 4.510
#                                       generating 1.688 against 1.689
#     four tokens, cpu and reference    0.039 + 0.101 and 5.604 + 3.731
#                                       against 0.042 + 0.102, 5.611 + 3.671
#     drafting, the two-bit model       1.339 against 1.320
#     q4km 110, stored and rows         0.380 and 0.209 against 0.380-0.385
#                                       and 0.212-0.216
#     q5km 110 rows                     0.220 against 0.228-0.232
#     Row_Dot                           q8_0 0.372, q4_0 0.303, q2_k 0.685,
#                                       bf16 0.318, f32 0.252 -- all within
#                                       two per cent of the published table
#
#   The panels table's 1419-token rows cell reads 3.705 s here, against
#   3.517 and 3.549 this morning and a published 3.774 to 3.821. Three
#   readings, three values, no cause named; the README now carries all
#   three beside the published cell.
#
# covers: the four-row table under "The four-bit format that keeps a
#   minimum", the ratios, the llama.cpp comparison beside them, and the
#   two-row table under "The load at the head of the chain"
# load:  under 1.10 before every reading
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q4_1, Q4_0, Q8_0, Q4_K_M, Q5_K_M and Q2_K


# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
#
# 2026-09-08, the two formats that keep a fifth bit, in panels -- and the
# four legacy formats have converged.
#
#   Q5_0 and Q5_1 have been the two slowest rows of the kernel table for as
#   long as it has existed, because the file keeps the fifth bit as bit J of
#   a thirty-two bit word: the shift varies with the element and the loop
#   will not vectorize. A panel ends that by moving the shift to load time.
#   The figures are under `### The two formats that keep a fifth bit`.
#
#   Both models were requantized from the Q8_0 file with llama-quantize at
#   95b8e33e1, the same way the Q4_1 one was.
#
#   Alternated three rounds against three, --backend cpu:
#
#     Q5_0                as stored              --repack rows        ratio
#       prompt, 110   3.978 4.022 3.999     0.186 0.192 0.187          21.1
#       prompt, 1419  57.845               3.124                       18.5
#       generating 17 1.725 1.724 1.738     0.320 0.321 0.319           5.40
#       generating 64 6.698                1.335                        5.02
#
#     Q5_1                as stored              --repack rows        ratio
#       prompt, 110   4.197 4.172 4.140     0.185 0.185 0.185          22.5
#       prompt, 1419  57.605               3.026                       19.0
#       generating 64 6.921 6.915 6.953     1.285 1.289 1.290           5.38
#
#   Every digest unchanged at both lengths on both layouts:
#   61fda0268954d85b and a5c0f9b2b89bbc68 for Q5_0, 90f72ad934240ff0 and
#   a5c0f9b2b89bbc68 for Q5_1. WORTH NOTING BESIDE Q4_1, whose long-prompt
#   digests do differ between its two paths -- a digest matching across two
#   arithmetics is luck rather than evidence, and it is not what these
#   kernels are held by.
#
#   llama-bench at 95b8e33e1, eight threads, same files, same sitting:
#   Q5_0 pp110 314.94, pp1419 253.64, tg64 59.15; Q5_1 pp110 144.01,
#   pp1419 124.78, tg64 54.17. So 1.88 and 1.79 times AHEAD for Q5_0 and
#   4.13 and 3.76 for Q5_1, with the generated token 1.23 and 1.20 behind.
#   LLAMA.CPP REPACKS NEITHER: repack.cpp builds block_q4_0x8,
#   block_q4_Kx8, block_iq4_nlx4 and block_q2_Kx8 and that is the list, so
#   these are the eight-row layout against no layout and the 4.13 should be
#   read as "a format nobody optimized on either side until now".
#
#   THE FOUR LEGACY FORMATS HAVE CONVERGED. A 110-token prompt reads 0.177,
#   0.177, 0.187 and 0.185 s for Q4_0, Q4_1, Q5_0 and Q5_1, where their
#   stored readings run from 0.56 to 4.2. Once they share a layout and a
#   kernel shape what is left is how many bytes a block holds, which is
#   nearly the same for all four.
#
#   AND THE KERNEL TABLE DID NOT MOVE, which is the part worth keeping.
#   Q5_0 and Q5_1 still read 0.524 and 0.553 ns an element there, still the
#   two slowest rows, because Row_Dot is the row product and the row product
#   still has the shift it always had. Nothing reaches it for a matrix in
#   panels. A published figure that is still true and no longer describes
#   what the program does is worth saying out loud.
#
#   HELD BY A TEST THAT REMOVES THE TOLERANCE RATHER THAN LIVING INSIDE IT.
#   Panel_Minimum_Term_Is_Exact became Panel_Legacy_Kernels_Are_Exact and
#   covers all four legacy kernels on two matrices, with activations that
#   are constant across each block of thirty-two -- which quantize to one
#   level and back without loss, so both paths see the same activations and
#   the same dequantized weights and must agree to floating-point rounding.
#   Three orders tighter than any other check of these kernels. The four
#   agree to 6E-6, and it was checked by breaking it twice: one group's
#   fifth bit at a shift of one instead of two fails by 48.3, the range of
#   the matrix, and a doubled minimum fails by 19306.
#
#   SEVEN GROUPS CONFIRMED, ONE RE-MEASURED. Confirming pairs, all inside:
#
#     twelve tokens, six-token prompt   0.345 s against 0.348
#     device, the same run              0.243 s against 0.242
#     q8 110 and 1419, cpu              0.289 and 4.374 against 0.286, 4.510
#                                       generating 1.691 against 1.689
#     four tokens, cpu and reference    0.040 + 0.101 and 5.628 + 3.746
#                                       against 0.042 + 0.102, 5.611 + 3.671
#     drafting, the two-bit model       1.350 against 1.320
#     q4km 110, stored and rows         0.381 and 0.206 against 0.380-0.385
#                                       and 0.212-0.216
#     q5km 110 rows                     0.222 against 0.228-0.232
#     q4_0 and q4_1 110 rows            0.178 and 0.177 against 0.177-0.180
#                                       and 0.175-0.182
#     the sixteen Row_Dot rows          every one within two per cent
#
#   The panels table's 1419-token rows cell reads 3.511 s here, a fourth
#   reading of a cell that has now given 3.774-3.821 (published), 3.517,
#   3.549 and 3.705. No cause named; the README carries the spread.
#
# covers: the two tables under "The two formats that keep a fifth bit", the
#   ratios, and the llama.cpp comparison beside them
# load:  under 1.10 before every reading
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q5_0, Q5_1, Q4_0, Q4_1, Q8_0, Q4_K_M, Q5_K_M
#        and Q2_K


# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
#
# 2026-09-08, the last two formats on the floating-point path -- and
# `--repack rows` now covers every format the engine multiplies.
#
#   Q2_K and Q3_K were what was left. The figures are under `### The last
#   two formats on the floating-point path`. The Q3_K model was requantized
#   from the Q8_0 file with llama-quantize at 95b8e33e1; the Q2_K one is the
#   file this repository has measured drafting with for months.
#
#   THESE TWO ARE NOT THE LEGACY FORMATS WITH A SMALLER QUANT. A super-block
#   has sixteen sub-blocks rather than eight, so the panels take sixteen
#   scales -- and Q2_K's sixteen minima -- out of their nibbles at load and
#   write them a byte apiece. These are the first two layouts here that
#   GROW: 800 bytes against the eight rows' 672 and 912 against 880. And a
#   quant of two bits puts four elements in a byte, so one group carries
#   sixteen elements a row and four weight vectors come out of one load at
#   four shifts. A group is then exactly a sub-block, which is what lets its
#   four dot products be summed and scaled as whole numbers with one
#   conversion for the super-block.
#
#   Alternated three rounds against three, --backend cpu:
#
#     Q2_K                as stored              --repack rows        ratio
#       prompt, 110   4.113 4.245 4.129     0.251 0.261 0.254          16.3
#       prompt, 1419  57.699               3.940                       14.6
#       generating 64 6.268 6.205 6.199     0.963 0.931 0.967           6.53
#
#     Q3_K                as stored              --repack rows        ratio
#       prompt, 110   2.661 2.627 2.608     0.228 0.241 0.229          11.3
#       prompt, 1419  36.841               3.729                        9.9
#       generating 64 4.196 4.208 4.173     1.002 1.039 1.040           4.10
#
#   llama-bench at 95b8e33e1, eight threads, same files, same sitting:
#   Q2_K pp110 223.44, pp1419 192.37, tg64 88.74; Q3_K pp110 265.52,
#   pp1419 226.81, tg64 79.99. So 1.96 and 1.87 times ahead for Q2_K and
#   1.82 and 1.68 for Q3_K, with the generated token 1.29 and 1.25 behind.
#
#   THE Q2_K ROW IS THE ONE THAT COUNTS AND IT IS THE ONLY ONE OF THE FIVE
#   NEW FORMATS WHERE IT DOES. llama.cpp repacks Q2_K -- block_q2_Kx8 is in
#   repack.cpp's list beside block_q4_0x8, block_q4_Kx8 and block_iq4_nlx4
#   -- so 1.96 is an eight-row layout against an eight-row layout, one
#   kernel against another, with no asterisk. Q3_K is not on that list and
#   carries the same caveat Q4_1, Q5_0 and Q5_1 do.
#
#   THE DIGESTS. At 1419 both formats give the same text on both paths --
#   dee17622efd3e8e4 and 1c7ce9457370098f. At 110 with sixty-four generated
#   they do not: 93de44ac9034121e against 0c4f875376192875 for Q2_K and
#   859092284f8cf1e9 against 0dfb3c821c67cf01 for Q3_K, each stable across
#   all three rounds. A coin landing, not a trend, and the reason these
#   kernels are held by a test rather than by a digest.
#
#   WHAT THE PANEL PATH COSTS THESE TWO IS A COARSER ACTIVATION. Both now
#   quantize a super-block at a time, as the other three k-quants already
#   did, because the kernel scales a whole super-block by one number and the
#   eight block scales inside it therefore have to be one number. Measured
#   on the panel test's own comparison, that is worth nothing: the largest
#   difference between the panel product and the floating-point one runs
#   0.078 to 0.104 across all nine formats, and adding these two moved the
#   maximum from 0.1039 to 0.1043.
#
#   HELD BY THE SAME TEST, WIDENED. Panel_Legacy_Kernels_Are_Exact became
#   Panel_Quantized_Kernels_Are_Exact and covers six kernels on activations
#   constant across a whole super-block, which quantize without loss
#   whichever way the caller groups them -- so the two k-quants are held as
#   tightly as the four legacy formats. The six agree to 6E-6, and it was
#   checked by breaking it: Q3_K's third bit at a shift of two instead of
#   three fails by 66.1 and Q2_K's minimum doubled by 1033.
#
#   SEVEN GROUPS CONFIRMED, ONE RE-MEASURED. Confirming pairs, all inside:
#
#     twelve tokens, six-token prompt   0.345 s against 0.348
#     device, the same run              0.244 s against 0.242
#     q8 110 and 1419, cpu              0.287 and 4.407 against 0.286, 4.510
#                                       generating 1.697 against 1.689
#     four tokens, cpu and reference    0.042 + 0.102 and 5.565 + 3.661
#                                       against 0.042 + 0.102, 5.611 + 3.671
#     drafting, the two-bit model       1.314 against 1.320
#     q4km 110 and 1419 rows            0.218 and 3.585 against 0.212-0.216
#                                       and the spread noted below
#     q5km 110 rows                     0.219 against 0.228-0.232
#     q4_0 and q5_0 110 rows            0.171 and 0.191 against 0.177-0.180
#                                       and 0.186-0.192
#     Row_Dot                           q8_0 0.370, q4_0 0.303, q2_k 0.701,
#                                       q3_k 0.477 -- all within two per
#                                       cent of the published table
#
#   The panels table's 1419-token rows cell reads 3.585 s here, a fifth
#   reading of a cell that has given 3.774-3.821 (published), 3.517, 3.549,
#   3.705 and now 3.585. Still no cause named.
#
#   NINE FORMATS, AND THE SHAPE OF THE TABLE. A 110-token prompt reads 0.171
#   to 0.261 s for every quantized format the engine multiplies, against
#   stored readings from 0.29 to 4.2. What is left of the difference is how
#   many bytes a block holds and how much unpacking it takes, which is what
#   a format is.
#
