#   in its source list and gained one guard: a chunk above the batch bound
#   is refused by name rather than reported as "nothing was scored". The
#   published table was taken at a chunk of 512, which the guard admits.
#
#   THE TABLE ITSELF WAS CHECKED FOR STABILITY, at twice the corpus:
#
#     format    1,020 positions   2,040 positions
#     q4_k_m       0.044413          0.041877
#     q4_0         0.051893          0.050035
#     q4_1         0.060082          0.057414
#     q3_k         0.091220          0.093752
#     mxfp4        0.120443          0.125466
#
#   Three to six per cent either way and every ordering kept, including the
#   two the README makes a point of: q4_k_m below q4_0 below q4_1, and mxfp4
#   above q3_k.
#
# load:  the quantizing is not a timing and was not gated; the stability
#        readings went through the gate as the table did
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0, written out five ways

# 2026-09-09, the k-quants, and what a search costs two implementations.
#
#   WHAT WAS ADDED. Q6_K and Q4_K to tests quantize, which is where the
#   entry above left off: those two do not encode by a rule, they SEARCH.
#   Q6_K tries nineteen candidate inverse scales for each run of sixteen and
#   keeps the best weighted least-squares fit; Q4_K tries twenty-one for each
#   run of thirty-two and fits a scale AND a minimum at each, then quantizes
#   the eight scales and eight minimums it found by a further rule.
#
#   THE QUESTION THE ENTRY ABOVE RAISED, and the answer:
#
#     format   same   differing   bytes apart of 619,094,016
#      q8_0     201       0                0
#      q4_0     201       0                0
#      q4_1     201       0                0
#      q5_0     201       0                0
#      q5_1     201       0                0
#      q6_K     201       0                0
#      q4_K     194       7             1,081
#
#   Q6_K SEARCHES AND STILL AGREES EXACTLY, across 156 tensors of this
#   model: two implementations comparing floating-point error sums could
#   have parted at any near-tie and never did.
#
#   Q4_K PARTS SEVEN TIMES, BY 1.7 PARTS PER MILLION. The first is
#   output.weight, eight bytes of thirty-six million. That is the shape of a
#   handful of near-ties and not of a rule read differently, which would
#   differ everywhere. Two things were ruled out: compiling the unit with
#   -ffp-contract=off changes nothing, so it is not a multiply and an add
#   being folded; and the count is stable run to run, so it is not anything
#   unrepeatable.
#
#   AND WHAT THOSE BYTES ARE WORTH, measured with the instrument two entries
#   above, one file against the other as its baseline:
#
#     divergence 0.000000 nats, top token agreed 100.0 per cent, 1,020
#     positions
#
#   Nothing the model believes turns on them.
#
#   THE COST OF SEARCHING, for a reader deciding whether to wait: q4_0 takes
#   17 s over this model and q6_K takes 220, q4_K 250. The search is
#   nineteen or twenty-one passes over every run where a rule is one.
#
#   NO FIGURE GROUP AND NO RESTAMP. What is above is a comparison of bytes
#   and not a timing, and it is reproducible from the two commands quoted in
#   the README.
#
# load:  not gated; the quantizing is not a timing. The divergence reading
#        went through the gate.
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0, written out seven ways

# 2026-09-09, an importance matrix collected and consumed, and the last five
# formats.
#
#   THE MATRIX. tests imatrix collects one -- for every weight matrix, the
#   sum over a corpus of the square of each activation that met each of its
#   columns -- and tests quantize --imatrix uses one. llama.cpp's
#   llama-imatrix and its --imatrix, and the weighted path is a different
#   algorithm rather than the plain one with weights: thirty-seven candidate
#   scales from -0.9 by 0.05 against twenty-one from -1.0 by 0.1, and the
#   sub-block scales fitted by a second search rather than divided by their
#   largest.
#
#   WHAT IT BUYS. Q4_K --pure, divergence from the eight-bit model over
#   1,020 positions:
#
#     no matrix                                0.061860   84.2 % top token
#     llama.cpp's matrix, held-out text        0.029365   89.5
#     llama.cpp's matrix, the measured corpus  0.027507   89.9
#     this collector, held-out text            0.027716   90.9
#     this collector, the measured corpus      0.026051   91.0
#
#   TWICE THE ACCURACY, and the held-out rows are the ones to read. A matrix
#   collected on the text the divergence is measured on flatters itself, and
#   the gap between the two rows of each pair -- six or seven per cent, the
#   same for both collectors -- is how much. The held-out text is a second
#   59 KB of this repository's prose that the corpus does not contain.
#
#   OURS IS NOT THEIRS AND CANNOT BE. Two matrices from two forward passes
#   hold different sums, so every quantized tensor differs: 156 of 156, 3.9
#   per cent of the file. The check is therefore the divergence and not the
#   bytes, and it says ours does the same job slightly better. The likely
#   reason is in the counts: ours weights 155 matrices where theirs weights
#   154, because theirs excludes the output projection and ours records it.
#
#   THE SEAM AND WHAT IT COSTS. Llama.Watcher: a session tells something the
#   name of every matrix it is about to multiply by. A view carries an
#   address and no name, so Resolve writes the pair down -- it is the one
#   moment the two are in the same place. The cost is a null check a
#   product, and the twelve-token figure was alternated to say so:
#
#     0.341 s wall / 0.298 generating, three rounds, digest 33f48397f89839f6
#
#   which is the published figure unmoved. The five groups listing llama.adb
#   are restamped on that measurement rather than on an argument.
#
#   AND THE LAST FIVE FORMATS: Q2_K, Q3_K, Q5_K, IQ4_NL, IQ4_XS. Twelve
#   quantized formats now, which is every one llama-quantize writes for this
#   model, against llama-quantize itself:
#
#     format    same   differing   bytes apart
#      q8_0      201       0            0
#      q4_0      201       0            0
#      q4_1      201       0            0
#      q5_0      201       0            0
#      q5_1      201       0            0
#      q2_K      201       0            0
#      q6_K      201       0            0
#      iq4_nl    201       0            0
#      iq4_xs    201       0            0
#      q3_K      190      11           14
#      q5_K      193       8          129
#      q4_K      194       7        1,081
#
#   NINE EXACT, THREE OFF BY PARTS PER MILLION -- 0.03 for q3_K, 0.17 for
#   q5_K, 1.7 for q4_K. The three that part are the ones whose search is
#   finest: Q4_K and Q5_K fit a scale and a minimum at each of many
#   candidates, and Q3_K moves individual levels while the fit improves.
#   Q2_K searches too and agrees exactly, as do Q6_K and both table formats.
#
#   THE QUALITY GROUP is restamped rather than re-measured: quantizer.adb is
#   not in its list, and what changed in perplexity_run.adb is nothing.
#
# load:  the quantizing and collecting are not timings and were not gated;
#        the divergence readings and the twelve-token figure went through
#        the gate
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0, written out twelve ways

# 2026-09-09, the mixtures people actually ship.
#
#   WHAT THEY ARE. llama-quantize does not write one format through a file:
#   asked for Q4_K_M it writes Q4_K mostly, lifting attention's values and
#   the feed-forward's down projection to Q6_K in particular layers and the
#   output projection throughout. Every comparison in the entries above
#   passed --pure to turn that off, which measured the encoders honestly and
#   left the recipes unwritten. tests quantize --format q4_k_m writes them.
#
#   AGAINST llama-quantize WITH NO --pure, tensor by tensor:
#
#     recipe    above the base   same   differing   bytes apart
#     q2_k_m         67           197        4           7
#     q3_k_s          1           190       11          14
#     q3_k_m         67           194        7           7
#     q3_k_l         67           194        7           7
#     q4_k_s          7           200        1       1,022
#     q4_k_m         21           200        1       1,022
#     q5_k_s          1           201        0           0
#     q5_k_m         21           201        0           0
#
#   TWO OF THE EIGHT ARE EXACT, and q5_k_m is the interesting one: pure
#   Q5_K had eight tensors differing by 129 bytes and the mixture has none,
#   because it lifts to Q6_K precisely the tensors those near-ties landed
#   on. The counts above the base were predicted from the transcription
#   before the sweep ran -- 1, 21 and 67 for the three unrun rows -- and
#   came back as predicted, which is the transcription checking itself.
#
#   NOTHING ABSENT IN ANY ROW, which is the whole claim: every tensor gets
#   the type llama.cpp gives it. What is left is the parts-per-million
#   disagreement the k-quant searches already had, now landing on whichever
#   tensors a recipe sends to those formats.
#
#   AND A FAULT A WELL-FORMED FILE HIDES. The policy asks which layer a
#   tensor is in and llama.cpp answers with a running counter -- right for
#   llama.cpp, which walks a list its loader ordered by layer. A reader
#   walking the file walks the order the converter wrote, and for this model
#   that is LEXICOGRAPHIC: blk.0, blk.1, blk.10, blk.11, and blk.2 twelve
#   places later. Counting put a tenth of the layers' extra bits on the
#   wrong ones -- twenty tensors of two hundred and one:
#
#     before   180 the same, 1 differing, 20 absent
#              first odd blk.10.ffn_down.weight ours Q4_K theirs Q6_K
#     after    200 the same, 1 differing,  0 absent
#
#   The file was exactly the right size, loaded, and generated text. Nothing
#   short of a byte comparison against another implementation would have
#   said so, and the counter is the kind of mistake that reads as correct.
#
# load:  the quantizing is not a timing and was not gated
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0, written out in four mixtures

# 2026-09-09, what a mixture buys, and a comparison that is still not one.
#
#   Q4_K, divergence from the eight-bit model, 1,020 positions:
#
#     ours, --pure                 0.061860   84.2 % top token
#     ours, the q4_k_m mixture     0.040723   87.6
#     a vendor's own q4_k_m        0.044413   87.9
#
#   A THIRD LESS DIVERGENCE for twenty-one tensors' worth of extra bits,
#   which is 1.52 times. The first two rows are like for like: both written
#   here, both from the eight-bit model, both measured against it.
#
#   THE THIRD ROW IS NOT COMPARABLE TO THE FIRST TWO. A file written here is
#   quantized from q8_0 and measured against q8_0, so it shares that
#   rounding with the baseline and is flattered by it; the vendor's file
#   came from higher precision and its divergence counts two roundings. Ours
#   reading lower is mostly evidence of a shared source, and saying
#   otherwise would be the same mistake the quality table already carries a
#   caveat about. What would repair it is a half-precision file of this
#   model, which is not here.
#
#   WHAT THE THIRD ROW DOES ESTABLISH: it reproduces the 0.044413 published
#   in the quality table to the digit, several sittings later, on the same
#   corpus. The instrument repeats.
#
# load:  through the gate before each reading; the part at 54 to 92 C
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0 and Q4_K_M

# 2026-09-09, what a dispatch costs, and where the fixed cost went.
#
#   The 3.3 ms a token that does not depend on the context -- thirteen and a
#   half per cent of a generated token -- was attributed to sixty-seven
#   submissions; the submission count then fell to twenty-three and the
#   attribution was withdrawn. The cost stayed measured and unexplained. It
#   is priced here directly, by appending one redundant normalization to
#   every layer: twenty-two more dispatches a token, reading what the layer
#   produced and writing where nothing reads it.
#
#     twelve tokens, generating          mean
#     as it is      0.219  0.217  0.218  0.2180
#     +22 a token   0.221  0.223  0.220  0.2213
#
#   THE RANGES DO NOT OVERLAP and the output digest is 5abff916f9d83ca6 in
#   all six rounds, which is the point of choosing a step nothing reads.
#   3.3 ms over twelve tokens is 0.275 ms a token over twenty-two
#   dispatches: 12.5 MICROSECONDS A DISPATCH.
#
#   AND A LAYER DISPATCHES ABOUT FIFTEEN TIMES -- a normalization, three
#   projections, two rotations, two placements, attention, the attention
#   output, the feed-forward normalization, gate and up, the combination,
#   the projection down; both joins fold into the products before them and
#   dispatch nothing. Twenty-two layers is about 330 a token, and 330 at
#   12.5 us is 4.1 ms -- the same quantity the regression against context
#   found, arrived at from the other end. THE FIXED COST IS THE DISPATCHES.
#
#   SO THE CEILING on removing dispatches is 3.3 of 18.2 ms, EIGHTEEN PER
#   CENT of a generated token, and that is where the device's remaining
#   1.07 against llama.cpp lives. What is available cheaply is much less:
#   of llama.cpp's three fusions, add+rms_norm is the join folding already
#   done here and rms_norm+mul is norm.comp carrying the gain. The one left
#   is rope_set_rows -- 22 to 44 dispatches, 0.275 to 0.55 ms, 1.5 to 3.0
#   per cent. Not built.
#
#   THE FIRST ATTEMPT AT THIS WAS WRONG in a way worth recording. The
#   redundant step was appended after the layer's final join, which made it
#   the layer's last step, and a layer hands back its last step -- so the
#   probe changed the answer. The digest moved, which is how it was caught.
#   Moved one place earlier the digest holds, and only then does the timing
#   mean what it says.
#
# load:  through the gate before each of the six rounds, alternated
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q4_K_M on the device, 22 layers

# 2026-09-09, a mixture and a matrix are nearly independent, and the fourth
# cell found a bug the other three could not.
#
#   Q4_K against the eight-bit model, 1,020 positions, four files:
#
#                       no matrix        this collector's matrix
#     --pure            0.061860 84.2 %  0.026051 91.0 %
#     the q4_k_m mix    0.040723 87.6    0.018332 91.8
#
#   BOTH CUTS HOLD IN THE PRESENCE OF THE OTHER. The matrix is worth 2.37
#   times alone and 2.22 with the mixture; the mixture 1.52 alone and 1.42
#   with the matrix. Multiplying the single gains predicts 0.0261 x 0.0407
#   / 0.0619 = 0.01714 and the corner measures 0.01833 -- SEVEN PER CENT
#   SHORT OF INDEPENDENT, the overlap of two mechanisms that both end up
#   spending bits on the same tensors. Together, 0.0619 to 0.0183 is 3.4
#   times, and 84.2 to 91.8 per cent on the top token.
#
#   AND THE FOURTH CELL CAUGHT WHAT THE OTHER THREE COULD NOT.
#   Encode_Weighted had a weighted path for Q4_K and none for Q6_K, and a
#   recipe spends its extra bits in Q6_K -- so the only corner that is both
#   mixed and weighted quantized its twenty-one lifted tensors as though no
#   matrix had been given:
#
#     before   180 the same, 21 differing, 24,693,024 bytes apart
#     after    200 the same,  1 differing,      1,022 bytes apart
#
#   which is the mixture's own figure without a matrix. Pure-with-a-matrix
#   never reaches Q6_K and mixture-without-one has no matrix to ignore;
#   neither cell alone could see it. The fix is one argument, llama.cpp's
#   make_qx_quants (16, 32, x, L, 1, qw) against the same call with a null
#   weight, and the two encode paths now share one body.
#
#   TWENTY-FOUR MEGABYTES OF DIFFERENT BYTES MOVED THE DIVERGENCE FROM
#   0.018484 TO 0.018332 -- four tenths of a per cent. The number was very
#   nearly right while the code under it was wrong, which is the argument
#   for comparing files byte by byte against another implementation rather
#   than trusting that a good reading means a correct one.
#
#   NO GROUP IS RESTAMPED for this: the fix is in quantizer.adb, which is a
#   tool that writes files rather than a source any published timing depends
#   on, and it is in no group. The engine reading those files is unchanged.
#
# load:  through the gate before each reading; the part at 56 to 90 C
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0, written out four ways

# 2026-09-09, the entry above is wrong, and a fusion built and refused.
#
#   THE DISPATCH PRICE IS WITHDRAWN. The entry two above prices a dispatch
#   at 12.5 us from a probe of twenty-two redundant NORMALIZATIONS a token,
#   and reads 330 dispatches a token as 4.1 ms -- the fixed cost from the
#   other end. The same probe with a redundant CACHE WRITE instead, the
#   layer's keys put in the cache a second time, same values same places,
#   twenty-two more dispatches a token and no fold in any of them:
#
#     twelve tokens, generating                          mean
#     as it is       0.219 0.218 0.220 0.218 0.220 s     0.2190
#     +22 writes     0.220 0.220 0.219 0.219 0.218       0.2192
#
#   NOTHING. 0.2 ms over twelve tokens is the noise. The bound this puts on
#   a dispatch of that shape is under two microseconds and the estimate is
#   under one, so 330 a token is half a millisecond at worst rather than
#   four. THE FIXED COST IS NOT THE DISPATCHES and is unexplained again --
#   which is now twice it has been attributed and twice withdrawn, and the
#   second withdrawal at least cost a measurement rather than an argument.
#
#   WHAT THE 12.5 US ACTUALLY WAS: a normalization. One workgroup over
#   2,048 elements, an eight-step fold with a barrier at each, on a part
#   with twelve compute units -- a twelfth of the machine, serializing
#   inside it. A cache write is one workgroup over 256 elements and no
#   fold. Two probes of the same dispatch count, one costing 3.3 ms over
#   twelve tokens and the other nothing, say the price of a step here is
#   the work inside it and not the call, and that ONE WORKGROUP WITH A FOLD
#   IN IT is the expensive shape. That is what to look for next.
#
#   AND ROPE_SET_ROWS, BUILT AND REFUSED. llama.cpp's remaining fusion, the
#   rotation and the cache write in one kernel. Built: rotate_place.comp,
#   the two shaders' bodies in one invocation, writing the turned vector
#   where the rotation wrote it and into the cache besides; a placing step
#   that folds into the rotation before it the way a join folds into the
#   product before it, staying in the sequence so an index a caller wrote
#   still means what it meant; two more push words (Stride and At_First,
#   the block 32 bytes to 40), a fifth binding for the cache where a
#   product binds a folded join's residual, its own module and pipeline.
#
#   CORRECT: conformance 41,780 sequences, 0 outside tolerance, and twelve
#   tokens answer 5abff916f9d83ca6. Mutating the fused kernel's cache write
#   moves that digest to 27a57e62042c83c1, which is how the path was shown
#   to be the one running rather than assumed to be.
#
#     twelve tokens, generating                                    mean
#     two kernels   0.219 0.220 0.219 0.221 0.216 0.220 0.220 0.220  0.21938
#     one kernel    0.219 0.219 0.216 0.219 0.220 0.219 0.221 0.220  0.21913
#
#   0.25 ms over twelve tokens, fourteen times smaller than the 1.5 per
#   cent the dispatch argument promised, inside a spread of five. REFUSED,
#   as the split-k reduce was: the words are in the history, not the tree.
#
#   Also written down because it will be wanted again: the sixteen compiled
#   shader constants were reproduced byte for byte from their sources, so
#   the flags are known rather than guessed. glslangValidator -V for
#   attention, combine, row_product, half_batch, norm, rotate, place; -V
#   --target-env vulkan1.1 with -DSUBGROUPS -DWIDE, with -DSUBGROUPS
#   -DQUERY_TILE, with -DSUBGROUPS -DWIDE -DHALVED, and with those plus
#   -DGROUPED for the four other attentions; -V --target-env vulkan1.3 for
#   attention_matrix and for matrix_product, again with -DMORE_FORMATS,
#   with -DNARROW, and with both.
#
# load:  through the gate before each of the twenty-six rounds, alternated
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0 on the device, 22 layers, twelve tokens

# 2026-09-09, the 3.3 ms is not there, and the budget was calling a whole
# layer attention.
#
#   ATTENDING, BY ABLATION. attention.comp returns at the top of main after
#   writing zeros where its blend would go: every dispatch, every workgroup
#   and every other step of the layer stay where they were, and only the
#   attending goes. Three alternated rounds, five readings each, twelve
#   generated tokens on the device:
#
#     context   as it is   attention away   attending
#     about 18   0.2197 s      0.2183 s     0.12 ms a token
#     about 1431 0.2427        0.2200       1.89
#
#   A line through those gives 0.0013 MS A POSITION AND 0.1 MS A TOKEN THAT
#   DOES NOT DEPEND ON THE CONTEXT, against the 0.0073 and 3.3 the entry of
#   2026-08-30 published. Six times cheaper a position and thirty times
#   smaller a fixed cost -- 0.1 ms of an 18.3 ms token is half a per cent.
#
#   SO THE 3.3 MS IS NOT UNEXPLAINED. IT IS ABSENT. It was attributed to
#   sixty-seven submissions and withdrawn when the count fell; attributed to
#   dispatches this morning and withdrawn this afternoon; and it turns out
#   there was nothing left to attribute. Two entries were written about a
#   quantity that had stopped existing.
#
#   AND THE INSTRUMENT SAYS WHY. --budget on six prompt tokens and twelve
#   generated: PROJECTING 0.262 s, ATTENDING 0.215, everything else 0.034.
#   0.215 s attending in a run where the ablation says 0.0014. Since a
#   generated token's layer goes over as one sequence and comes back once,
#   the host has ONE CLOCK READING FOR THE WHOLE LAYER -- and llama.adb
#   charged it to Attending. The largest number in the budget was a layer
#   wearing one part's name, and the 3.6/13.9 figures the line was fitted
#   through are the most likely thing it came out of.
#
#   FIXED BY NAMING IT. Fusing is a phase: not a part of a layer but the
#   whole of one, on the only path where the host cannot divide it. The same
#   run now reads PROJECTING 0.262 (the prompt, which does not fuse),
#   ATTENDING 0.000, FUSING 0.215 (the twelve tokens, which do), READING_OUT
#   0.034.
#
#   CHECKED AND MUTATED. The test runs a batch unaccounted, turns the budget
#   on, generates one token on the device, and asks that Fusing holds
#   something and Attending nothing; charging the fused layer back to
#   Attending fails it. Written first against the default fixture it passed
#   for the wrong reason -- a seven-kilobyte binary32 model is not kept
#   resident by the device, so no layer fuses and Fusing is zero either way.
#   A trace of the six conditions said so: resident FALSE on 2,072 of 2,176
#   device layers in the suite. The check takes the quantized fixture.
#
#   WHAT IS LEFT. With attention removed entirely a generated token still
#   takes 18.2 ms, which is the products; those were measured at 55.5 GB/s
#   against a part that gives sixty. The device's remaining 1.07 against
#   llama.cpp is a streaming problem, not an arrangement one, and neither
#   attention nor dispatch count is where to look.
#
# load:  through the gate before each of the twelve readings, alternated
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0 on the device, twelve generated tokens

# 2026-09-09, seven groups restamped and none re-measured.
#
#   quality, engine, reference, device, drafting, outside and panels all
#   name model_runner-llama.adb, and it changed: the fused layer's one
#   clock reading is charged to Fusing rather than to Attending, and Phase
#   gained the value to say so.
#
#   NOT RE-MEASURED, AND THE REASON. Charge is what a budget uses and a
#   budget is off unless a caller asks; the change is which slot of the
#   Phase_Times array a Duration lands in, and the same Duration lands in
#   every one of them. No arithmetic, no order, no allocation and no
#   dispatch moved. The digest of twelve generated tokens is unchanged at
#   5abff916f9d83ca6 and the alternated rounds that produced the ablation
#   entry above were taken across this change, before and after, at 0.2197
#   and 0.2200 s -- which is the same reading.
#
# load:  not a timing
# model: not a timing

# 2026-09-09, a generated token taken apart, and the vocabulary product
# reading twenty-nine per cent faster than the layer products.
#
#   THE METHOD IS ABLATION, not the budget, which the entry above shows was
#   charging a whole fused layer to attending. Take one part out, leave
#   every dispatch and workgroup where it was, and the run loses what that
#   part cost. Twelve generated tokens, context about eighteen,
#   TinyLlama-1.1B Q8_0 on the device, three alternated rounds of five
#   readings each:
#
#     part                     as it is   without   costs      a token
#     attending                0.2197 s   0.2183    0.0014 s   0.12 ms
#     the cache read back      0.2190     0.2173    0.0017     0.14
#     the output projection    0.2177     0.2070    0.0107     0.89
#     the layer products, by difference                        17.05
#
#   NINETY-FOUR PER CENT OF A TOKEN IS THE LAYER PRODUCTS and what is left
#   is one and a half per cent of vocabulary, eight tenths of attending and
#   copying, and nothing else. No fixed cost is hiding in it. Three entries
#   went looking for 3.3 ms in submissions, in dispatches and in attention;
#   this is what a token is.
#
#   IN BYTES. The file is 1,170.8 MB. token_embd.weight is 69.6 of it and is
#   read a row at a time; the layer weights are 1,029.4 and output.weight is
#   69.6, and a generated token reads every byte of both.
#
#     the layer products     1,029.4 MB  17.05 ms  60.4 GB/s
#     the output projection     69.6      0.89     78.2
#     the token              1,099.0     18.20     60.4
#     llama.cpp's token      1,099.0     17.18     64.0
#
#   SO THE 1.07 IS SIX PER CENT OF STREAMING RATE AND NOTHING ELSE. Same
#   bytes, same part, four per cent of a byte a nanosecond apart. Every
#   section that looked for the gap in the shape of the work was looking in
#   the wrong place.
#
#   AND THE VOCABULARY PRODUCT DOES NOT FIT. 78.2 GB/s against the layer
#   products' 60.4 -- twenty-nine per cent, same part, same format, same
#   kernel. At the vocabulary's rate a token would be 13.2 ms against
#   llama.cpp's 17.2. Not the row length: the vocabulary's rows are 2048
#   long and so are the queries' and the gate's inputs. Not the workgroup
#   count: 5,632 rows and 32,000 rows both bury twelve compute units. What
#   differs is that the vocabulary product is ALONE IN ITS SUBMISSION and a
#   layer's products are fifteen steps of one sequence with a barrier
#   wherever a step reads what the step before wrote. Hypothesis, untested.
#   THE NUMBER TO BEAT IS 78.2 GB/S and the part evidently gives it.
#
#   CAVEAT ON THE LAST ROW. The layer products' 17.05 ms is a residual --
#   the token less the three ablated parts -- because there is no way to
#   take a layer's products away and still have a token. The other three are
#   direct and alternated; this one carries whatever they do not.
#
#   ALSO CHECKED AND NOT THE ANSWER: the weights are device-local, not
#   imported host memory. --device-memory swept at 1.3, 2, 4 and 8 GB reads
#   53.10 52.55 52.41, 52.53 52.72 52.99, 52.79 53.10 52.55 and 52.36 52.41
#   52.92 tokens a second -- flat, so nothing spills at the default. Asking
#   for the weights where they lie instead reads 5.81 5.77 5.75, which is
#   the nine times the table under "The device backend" already records.
#
# load:  through the gate before each of the thirty readings, alternated
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0 on the device, twelve generated tokens

# 2026-09-09, the hypothesis tested and refused, and one line under every
# shape a token asks for.
#
#   THE CLAIM UNDER TEST, from the entry above: the vocabulary product
#   reads at 78.2 GB/s and the layer products at 60.4, and the difference
#   is that the vocabulary product is alone in its submission where a
#   layer's products are fifteen steps of one sequence with barriers.
#
#   THE TEST: run the layer's own shapes alone, at one vector, which is the
#   shape a generated token asks for and the only one bound by the weights
#   rather than the arithmetic. tests device-bench, q8_0, two rounds:
#
#     keys        256 x 2048    0.56 MB     74.7 us    7.5 GB/s
#     query      2048 x 2048    4.46       136.7      32.6
#     gate       5632 x 2048   12.26       255.0      48.0
#     down       2048 x 5632   12.26       255.7      47.9
#     vocabulary 32000 x 2048  69.63      1162.7      59.9
#
#   THOSE FIVE ARE ONE LINE: 64.3 US A CALL AND 63.4 GB/S AFTER IT, every
#   one within 2.2 per cent of it. gate and down are the same bytes shaped
#   opposite ways -- 5,632 rows of 2,048 against 2,048 of 5,632 -- and read
#   255.0 and 255.7. A row product's time is its bytes and one call.
#
#   SO THERE IS NO SHAPE DIFFERENCE and nothing for the sequencing to
#   explain. REFUSED.
#
#   AND THE 78.2 GB/S WAS THE METHOD. Taking the output projection out of a
#   token loses 0.89 ms; the product alone costs 1.16, of which 1.10 is
#   streaming. Removing the last product of a token removes LESS than the
#   product costs, so ABLATION BY DIFFERENCE MEASURES A PART'S MARGINAL
#   COST, NOT ITS COST -- right for "what would removing this save", wrong
#   for "what does this cost". The three ablations in the entry above are
#   marginal costs and are to be read as such.
#
#   THE CORRECTED PICTURE. A token's 1,099 MB at this kernel's own 63.4
#   GB/s would take 17.33 ms. llama.cpp's whole token takes 17.18 and this
#   one takes 18.20. So llama.cpp's row product is about one per cent
#   faster than this one and THE REST OF THE 1.07 IS OVERHEAD AROUND THE
#   PRODUCTS: 0.12 ms attending, 0.14 copying the cache back, 0.06 the one
#   call a token waits on, and 0.55 NOT YET LOCATED. Smaller and better
#   posed than the three questions the entries above went after.
#
#   AND WHERE THE LINE STOPS. Past about seventy megabytes a matrix drifts
#   off it -- 139 MB by 2.9 per cent, 209 MB by 12.9, 418 MB by 46 -- and so
#   does the vocabulary's own bytes in rows four times as long, 8,000 by
#   8,192, by 8.9. The first looks like residency and the second like row
#   length; neither is established, and no model this program runs has a
#   matrix in either range.
#
#   The 64.3 us a call is the same quantity this file measured at 82.7 us
#   for a call before it computes anything, from the other direction and
#   two arrangements later.
#
# load:  device-bench times a product against its own clock, not the host's
# model: no model; matrices allocated for the shapes named

# 2026-09-09, the 0.55 ms found: it is the normalizations, and inside one
# it is the first read of the row.
#
#   The entry above leaves 0.55 ms of an 18.2 ms token unaccounted. Ablation
#   at the shader, twelve generated tokens, readings pooled over ten rounds:
#
#     as it is                              0.2187 s   18.22 ms a token
#     the two norms a layer taken away      0.2149     -0.317 ms a token
#     every element-wise step taken away    0.2136     -0.425
#
#   THE TWO NORMALIZATIONS A LAYER ARE 0.317 MS A TOKEN, 7.2 us each, forty-
#   four of them. Attending is 0.12 of the 0.425 and ROTATING AND COMBINING
#   ARE NOTHING -- each moves the reading by less than the spread. The
#   layer's non-product work is the normalizations and almost nothing else.
#
#   AND INSIDE ONE IT IS THE FIRST PASS. A norm reads the row to sum its
#   squares, folds across 256 lanes in eight barriered steps, and reads the
#   row again to scale and store.
#
#     the eight-step fold                    0.000 ms a token
#     the second pass, scaling and storing    0.058
#     the first pass, by difference           0.258
#
#   THE FOLD IS FREE AND THE FIRST READ IS THREE QUARTERS OF THE COST. Both
#   passes read the same 2,048 floats; the second finds them in cache and
#   the first does not, and it is ONE WORKGROUP of 256 lanes issuing eight
#   dependent loads a lane with nothing else on the part to hide them
#   behind. norm.comp argues its fold down from three earlier shapes and is
#   right to; what it does not say is that the fold was never the cost.
#
#   WHICH POINTS SOMEWHERE, BECAUSE A DISPATCH IS FREE. A call costs 64 us
#   and a dispatch inside a sequence already recorded costs under one. A
#   normalization split across twelve workgroups -- partial sums, the fold,
#   then a scaling pass with no ordering in it at all -- is one more
#   dispatch for twelve times the lanes on the part that costs. NOT BUILT.
#
#   THE TOKEN AS FAR AS IT HAS BEEN TAKEN APART:
#
#     the products, at the rate one reaches alone   17.33 ms   95.1 %
#     the two normalizations a layer                 0.32       1.7
#     attending                                      0.12       0.7
#     the cache copied back to the host              0.14       0.8
#     the one call a token waits on                  0.06       0.3
#     rotating, combining, joining                   0.00       --
#     still unlocated                                0.25       1.4
#
#   THE SAME CAUTION AS BEFORE: every line but the first is a marginal cost
#   and marginal costs need not sum to a total. With every element-wise step
#   removed the products still read 1,099 MB in 17.80 ms, 61.7 GB/s against
#   the 63.4 a single product reaches alone -- so 0.47 ms of the token is
#   the difference between a product in a fifteen-step sequence and a
#   product by itself, and the cache copy and the call wait are 0.20 of it.
#
# load:  through the gate before each of the thirty-three readings
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0 on the device, twelve generated tokens

# 2026-09-09, the normalization unrolled, built and refused, and two
# mechanisms refuted with it.
#
#   THE CLAIM UNDER TEST, from the entry above: a normalization's first pass
#   is three quarters of its cost because a lane's loads are a chain -- the
#   add cannot start until the load lands and the next load is not issued
#   until the add is written -- and one workgroup on twelve compute units
#   has nothing to hide that behind.
#
#   BUILT: the sum fetches four values, and then eight, before adding any of
#   them, with the adds left in the order they were in. Bit for bit the same
#   sum, and twelve tokens answer 5abff916f9d83ca6 either way.
#
#     a value at a time  0.219 0.222 0.217 0.218 0.219 0.219 0.218  0.21886
#     eight at a time    0.218 0.219 0.217 0.218 0.220 0.221 0.217  0.21857
#
#   0.024 MS A TOKEN over seven alternated rounds, a tenth of what the pass
#   costs and inside the spread. Four at a time, seven more rounds, reads
#   the same. REFUSED.
#
#   AND NOR IS THE FIRST READ COLD. The second pass reads the same row and
#   finds it in cache, so the obvious story is a DRAM trip after the barrier
#   that published the product before it. Tested by summing THE WEIGHT
#   instead -- same width, read by every layer of every token, as warm as a
#   buffer on this part gets:
#
#     summing the activation   0.219 0.217 0.216   0.2173
#     summing the weight       0.220 0.221 0.218   0.2197
#     no normalization at all  0.216 0.215 0.216   0.2157
#
#   A WARM ROW SUMS NO FASTER THAN A FRESH ONE.
#
#   WHERE THAT LEAVES IT. The normalizations cost 0.3 ms a token and that
#   figure stands: twenty readings, a 0.0038 s difference against a spread
#   of about 0.002. The internal split that suggested both mechanisms --
#   fold free, second pass 0.058, first pass 0.258 -- rests on differences
#   at the edge of what this instrument resolves and is to be read that way.
#   Both mechanisms it suggested are now refuted by direct test.
#
#   The only thing left that would touch the number splits the sum across
#   workgroups, which associates it differently and moves the answer. That
#   is a price this repository has not paid for one and a half per cent.
#
# load:  through the gate before each of the thirty-two readings
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0 on the device, twelve generated tokens

# 2026-09-09, seven groups restamped and none re-measured: a model may be in
# several files now.
#
#   quality, engine, reference, device, drafting, outside and panels name
#   model_runner-llama.adb and tests/src/speed_run.adb between them, and both
#   changed: Prepare refuses a shard of a model by name, and the speed tool
#   opens a model through Model_Runner.GGUF.Shards rather than through a file
#   source of its own.
#
#   NOT RE-MEASURED, AND THE REASON. The refusal is one comparison of two
#   numbers read out of metadata, before anything is allocated or timed. The
#   speed tool's change is in OPENING, which it times and reports separately
#   from everything else -- and a set of one file delegates every operation
#   to that file, mapping and base address included, so the weights are still
#   the file's own pages: `--verbose` says "the file's own pages, read as
#   they are touched" as it did before. Twelve generated tokens on the device
#   read 0.219, 0.219 and 0.221 s after the change, against 0.2187 as the
#   mean of ten readings before it.
#
# load:  through the gate before each of the three readings
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0 on the device

# 2026-09-09, a model in several files, built.
#
#   THE GAP. Above a few tens of gigabytes a published GGUF is not one file:
#   the converter writes <stem>-00001-of-00003.gguf with split.no,
#   split.count and split.tensors.count in each and the model's metadata in
#   the first only. Made with llama.cpp's own llama-gguf-split from
#   TinyLlama-1.1B Q8_0, three parts of 400, 397 and 374 MB:
#
#     this engine   MR-ARCH-0010: the model has no tensor named
#                   output_norm.weight
#     llama.cpp     41.10 tokens a second
#
#   `inspect` on the first shard said 50 tensors and 374 million parameters
#   where the model has 201 and 1,100 million. A shard is a WELL-FORMED
#   CONTAINER holding a third of a model, so nothing in the parser had cause
#   to object, and the refusal named a tensor that is in another file.
#
#   BUILT. Model_Runner.GGUF.Shards, a byte source over the files. The
#   parser is given the FIRST SHARD and then the rest -- not the set, because
#   a parse validates every offset against the size of what it was handed,
#   and handed the set the first shard's tensors end two files early with the
#   rest read as data following them, which is the first thing that went
#   wrong here. Each shard is parsed and validated alone, against its own
#   size; the merge adds its tensors with their offsets moved on by the bytes
#   of the shards before them.
#
#   THE SECOND THING THAT WENT WRONG is worth recording. The data section is
#   a SPAN and not a sum: it runs from where the first shard's tensors begin
#   to where the last shard's end, and the headers of the shards between lie
#   inside it. Summed instead, the arena is smaller than the offsets that
#   then index it -- reported as a tensor access outside its storage, against
#   the model rather than the files.
#
#   AND IT READS THE SAME MODEL:
#
#                              prompt   generating   output
#     one file,   processor    0.042 s    0.298 s    33f48397f89839f6
#     three files, processor   0.042      0.297      33f48397f89839f6
#     one file,   device       0.028      0.222      5abff916f9d83ca6
#     three files, device      0.026      0.221      5abff916f9d83ca6
#
#   A MODEL IN ONE FILE COSTS NOTHING. A set of one part delegates every
#   operation to that part, mapping and base address included: --verbose
#   still says the weights are the file's own pages, and twelve generated
#   tokens still read 0.219 s.
#
#   WHAT A SET OF SEVERAL GIVES UP IS THE MAPPING. Several files are several
#   address ranges and a model wants one, so a sharded model is read into an
#   arena as --no-mmap already does. Reserving one span and mapping each file
#   into it at a fixed address is possible on this host and is not done.
#
#   FIVE REFUSALS, each reached by a test rather than argued for: a shard
#   given without its siblings, one that says it is a different shard than
#   its name does, one that disagrees with the first about how many there
#   are, a set larger than this build will hold open, and a first shard whose
#   name does not follow the convention -- which is the only way there is to
#   find the others. The order is what every offset rests on, so a set
#   assembled in the wrong order would read tensors out of the wrong files
#   and say nothing about it.
#
#   AND A NOTE ON THE CATALOG, which cost an hour. A message may only use
#   placeholder names the catalog knows: {found} is not one, and a catalog
#   containing it does not load AT ALL -- every locale falls back to the
#   emergency form, and what the suite then reports is "the catalog did not
#   load" three tests away from the line that caused it. Renamed to {actual}
#   it loads. Worth knowing before adding a message.
#
# load:  through the gate before each of the readings
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0, whole and cut into three by llama.cpp

# 2026-09-10, past the context it was trained on: which stretch, measured.
#
#   THE GAP. The engine has stretched rotations since it first read a file
#   asking for one -- rope.scaling.type, the factor, Yarn's band, its
#   attenuation, the context it was trained on, all read and all run -- and
#   only a file could ask:
#
#     model_runner run tinyllama-...-q8_0.gguf --context-size 4096
#     MR-ARCH-0015: a context of 4096 tokens exceeds the model maximum of 2048
#
#   llama.cpp: `-c 4096 --rope-scaling linear --rope-scale 2` warns and runs.
#
#   BUILT: --rope-scaling {none,linear,yarn}, --rope-scale, --rope-freq-base
#   and four --yarn-*. Each applied only where named, so a factor alone keeps
#   the file's own band. A model stretched by request may be opened past its
#   trained context; one that was not may not.
#
#   AND WHICH STRETCH IS A MEASUREMENT. TinyLlama-1.1B declares 2,048.
#   Perplexity over this repository's corpus, second half of each chunk
#   scored, on the device:
#
#     chunk    as trained   linear      yarn
#       512      35.52       76.69      36.29
#     1,024      44.67      108.56      45.22
#     2,048      42.68      116.41      42.26
#     4,096     refused     112.80      35.98
#     8,192     refused     476.29      54.27
#
#   512 to 2,048 stretch by two; 4,096 by two and 8,192 by four, which is
#   the factor matching the context asked for.
#
#   YARN COSTS ALMOST NOTHING WHERE IT IS NOT NEEDED: 36.29 against 35.52 at
#   512, and 42.26 against 42.68 at 2,048, which is better than unstretched.
#   AT 4,096 IT READS 35.98 where the unstretched model cannot run at all --
#   the same as the model's best reading at any length. Twice the context
#   for nothing.
#
#   THE LINEAR STRETCH IS TWO TO THREE TIMES WORSE EVERYWHERE and 476 at
#   fourfold. It is what a bare --rope-scale means, because that is what the
#   key meant before there was more than one kind of stretch, and on this
#   model it is the wrong choice by a factor of three.
#
#   AND THE FACTOR MUST MATCH THE CONTEXT. Yarn at 8,192 with a factor of
#   two reads 2,542.99; with four it reads 54.27. Nothing warns and nothing
#   derives one from the other, because a caller may want a stretch larger
#   than the context being opened. What there is, is the reading.
#
#   COMPARABLE ACROSS A ROW AND NOT DOWN A COLUMN. Each row scores the
#   second half of its own chunks -- 1,020 positions at 512, 4,095 at 8,192
#   -- so the rows are different amounts of different text. The claim each
#   row makes is about the three ways of running the same positions.
#
#   AND WHAT THIS FOUND IN THE INSTRUMENT. tests perplexity refused any
#   chunk above 512, because it evaluated a chunk in ONE PASS and a pass
#   holds Max_Batch positions. The consequence was quiet: EVERY PERPLEXITY
#   FIGURE THIS REPOSITORY HAS PUBLISHED WAS MEASURED AT A CONTEXT OF AT
#   MOST 512 TOKENS. A chunk is now evaluated in as many passes as it takes,
#   into the one session, which is what a long context is. The q8_0 baseline
#   was re-measured across the change: 35.5256, which is the published
#   figure to the digit, so the change moved nothing at the chunk every
#   published figure used.
#
#   The instrument also kept the LAST note rather than the first, so a
#   session that would not open was reported as "nothing was scored" -- true,
#   and not the reason. It keeps the first now.
#
# load:  through the gate before each reading
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0 on the device, corpus of 15,440 tokens

# 2026-09-10, a mixture of experts and a bound on a count.
#
#   WHAT WAS BEING LOOKED FOR was llama.cpp's --cpu-moe: a way to say which
#   tensors keep the device when the model is larger than the device holds.
#   Qwen3-30B-A3B is 11.26 GB and this host offers 8.47:
#
#     model_runner run qwen3moe.gguf --backend device
#     MR-MEM-0001: backend_memory needs 11251795968 bytes, above the limit
#                  of 8472029184
#
#   Forced through with --device-memory it ran, and the statistics said
#   something better than the flag would have:
#
#     matrices on the device  4096
#     bytes on the device     2551635968
#     matrices given back     0
#
#   FOUR THOUSAND AND NINETY-SIX IS THE BOUND, EXACTLY, and three and a half
#   gigabytes of a six-gigabyte budget was unspent. Two bounds decide
#   residency and the tighter wins: the byte budget, and the size of the
#   table the kept matrices sit in. The comment on that constant said it was
#   "high enough that the byte budget is what actually decides".
#
#   WHY A MIXTURE. The engine takes each expert as its own matrix, because
#   that is what reading eight of a hundred and twenty-eight means: the file
#   stores them fused and the loader cuts them into per-expert views.
#   Forty-eight layers of a hundred and twenty-eight experts, three matrices
#   each, is 18,432 where a dense model of the same depth has a few hundred.
#
#   RAISED TO 32,768. Three alternated rounds, sixteen generated tokens, a
#   six-gigabyte budget, a 4,096-token context:
#
#     table    tokens a second      matrices   bytes
#      4,096   2.72 2.74 2.72        4,096     2.54 GB
#     32,768   4.64 4.66 4.66        8,443     5.33 GB
#
#   1.71 TIMES, and against the processor's 1.55 tokens a second the device
#   goes from 1.76 times to 3.0. The byte budget is now what binds.
#
#   AND IT COSTS THE DENSE CASE NOTHING, which was the thing to check: the
#   table is only as long as what is in it. Twelve generated tokens on
#   TinyLlama read 0.219, 0.218, 0.218 s against a mean of 0.2187 over ten
#   readings before, digest unchanged at 5abff916f9d83ca6.
#
#   THE EARLIER READINGS OF THIS MODEL WERE NOISE AND ARE NOT KEPT: 0.84 to
#   2.53 tokens a second at the same setting across sittings, which was the
#   page cache filling on an eleven-gigabyte file. Warm, the same setting
#   reads within a hundredth three times running.
#
#   THE REPORT NOW NAMES THE BOUND BESIDE THE COUNT -- "199 of 32768" -- so
#   a run the table stops says so. No static check can bound a table against
#   arbitrary files; what went wrong was not the number but that nothing
#   distinguished it from a measurement.
#
#   AND --CPU-MOE WAS NOT BUILT. It decides which matrices keep the device,
#   and the policy already does that correctly: what is read every token
#   stays and what is read one token in sixteen goes, which is what least
#   recently used means and is exactly right for a mixture. There was
#   nothing for the flag to improve once the table was large enough to hold
#   the answer.
#
# load:  the mixture rows are ungated CLI runs, warm, three alternated
#        rounds; the TinyLlama rows went through the gate
# model: Qwen3-30B-A3B Q4_K 11.26 GB, and TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0

# 2026-09-10, a mixture in two submissions a layer instead of twenty-four.
#
#   THE GAP. `A layer, in one submission` took a dense layer from three
#   submissions to one and left the mixture behind: the condition choosing
#   the fused layer reads Settings.Experts = 0. A mixture does more of what
#   the dense path stopped doing, because each expert is its own matrix --
#   which is what reading eight of a hundred and twenty-eight means. Three
#   products a expert, eight experts, forty-eight layers: 1,152 submissions
#   a token, each paying the 64.3 us call this file measured.
#
#   THEY ARE TWO GROUPS. The gate and up of every chosen expert read THE
#   SAME input, so all sixteen are one group -- machinery a layer's queries,
#   keys and values have used since they were three products. The down
#   projections each read their own expert's gated result, and LAID END TO
#   END IN ONE ACTIVATION they are a group too, if a product may say where
#   in that activation its vector begins. A step could already say it; only
#   a folded join had ever used it.
#
#   Two alternated rounds, 110-token prompt, eight generated tokens,
#   Qwen3-30B-A3B, six-gigabyte budget, 4,096-token context:
#
#                                prompt          generating
#     three products a expert    6.01 6.00 t/s   4.46 4.42 t/s
#     two groups a layer        10.59 10.63      6.26 6.34
#
#   1.77 TIMES THE PROMPT AND 1.42 THE GENERATION, with the same text out of
#   both -- the products are the same products in the same order, batched.
#   The processor is untouched at 3.80 and 2.92: what a group saves there is
#   a wake, and the pool is already awake. The device goes from 1.6 times
#   the processor to 2.8.
#
#   AND THE DENSE PATH IS UNTOUCHED: twelve generated tokens of TinyLlama on
#   the device read 0.220, 0.218, 0.216 s against a mean of 0.2187 over ten
#   readings before, digest 5abff916f9d83ca6.
#
#   ONE THING THE OFFSETS FOUND. The activation a sequence uploads is sized
#   by walking its steps, and a step reading at an offset was counted as
#   reading ROWS elements from there. For a join that is right and cannot be
#   wrong -- a join's rows and columns are the same number -- and only a
#   join had ever read at an offset. A down projection's are 2,048 and 768,
#   so the upload ran off the end of the array and the run reported an
#   invariant violation. It reads COLUMNS now, which is right for both.
#
#   WHAT IS LEFT. A prompt still costs about what generation costs a token,
#   10.6 against 6.3, where a dense prompt on this device is thirty-seven
#   times its generation. Each position of a batch routes to its own
#   experts, so the feed-forward is still done a position at a time.
#   llama.cpp's answer is mul_mat_id, which groups a batch's tokens by the
#   expert they chose. That one needs a kernel rather than an arrangement.
#
# load:  the mixture rows are ungated CLI runs, warm, two alternated rounds;
#        the TinyLlama rows went through the gate
# model: Qwen3-30B-A3B Q4_K, and TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0

# 2026-09-10, a batch's mixture gathered by expert, which is mul_mat_id.
#
#   WHAT WAS LEFT. A mixture's prompt cost about what its generation cost a
#   token -- 10.6 against 6.3 -- where a dense prompt on this device is
#   thirty-seven times its generation. A batch has no one matrix to multiply
#   the whole of it by, because each position routes to its own eight
#   experts of a hundred and twenty-eight, so the mixture ran A POSITION AT
#   A TIME however many were handed in.
#
#   WHAT THAT COSTS IS THE READS. An expert chosen by seven positions of a
#   hundred and ten had its three matrices read seven times, and on a model
#   larger than the device will hold, read means uploaded. A hundred and ten
#   positions choosing eight is eight hundred and eighty reads a layer where
#   there are a hundred and twenty-eight matrices.
#
#   GROUPED BY EXPERT: every position that chose one is gathered into a run
#   of vectors, the expert's matrices are read once and multiplied by all of
#   them, and the answers are scattered back. Nothing new was needed on the
#   device -- one matrix against several vectors is what the batched product
#   already is. Three alternated rounds, 110-token prompt, eight generated
#   tokens, Qwen3-30B-A3B:
#
#                              prompt                 generating
#     a position at a time     10.63 10.58 10.57 t/s  6.43 6.38 6.29 t/s
#     gathered by expert       16.70 17.32 16.96      6.17 6.30 6.43
#
#   1.60 TIMES THE PROMPT, and generation untouched because a generated
#   token is one position and there is nothing to gather. The processor
#   keeps the simpler loop and reads 3.80 and 2.89 either way.
#
#   ACROSS THE TWO MIXTURE ENTRIES the prompt has gone 6.01 to 17.0 tokens a
#   second, and against the processor's 3.80 the device is 4.5 times rather
#   than 1.6. The dense path is untouched: twelve generated tokens of
#   TinyLlama read 0.218, 0.220, 0.219 s, digest 5abff916f9d83ca6.
#
#   THE SUM IS IN THE ORDER IT WAS. A position adds its experts' answers
#   best first; grouping by expert would add them in expert order, which is
#   a different sum of the same numbers. Each answer is written to the place
#   its position and its rank name and the sums are done afterwards, which
#   is what the largest of these buffers is for and why the same prompt
#   gives the same text to the bit.
#
#   AND NOTHING HELD THAT. The fixture comparison runs a mixture on the
#   processor and against the reference, batched and a token at a time, and
#   it runs the device -- but never A MIXTURE BATCHED ON THE DEVICE, which
#   is the one arrangement the gathering changes. Dropping the share from
#   the sum was caught by nothing: no failing test, 41,780 conformance
#   sequences, 0 outside tolerance. The test added holds the two answers
#   equal TO THE BIT rather than within a tolerance, because a tolerance
#   would let the gathering associate the sum differently and say nothing.
#   Dropping the share fails it by 1.14; a part in ten million on one
#   element fails it by 2.4e-07.
#
# load:  the mixture rows are ungated CLI runs, warm, three alternated
#        rounds; the TinyLlama rows went through the gate
# model: Qwen3-30B-A3B Q4_K, and TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0

# 2026-09-10, two tests that only ran in one build profile.
#
#   NOT A TIMING. Recorded here because it is about what the gate sees.
#
#   Two tests wanted an infinity, to check the code under test refuses one,
#   and both made it by multiplying Real'Last by sixteen. The multiply
#   overflows and the assignment that follows is a range check against a
#   type whose last value is Real'Last, so both raised CONSTRAINT_ERROR
#   before they had tested anything:
#
#     release build (what the gate runs)   318 successful, 0 errors
#     development build                    317 successful, 1 error
#
#   At -O3 the check is elided, the infinity lands, and the tests do their
#   work. In the development build they die on the first line of it. AUnit
#   reports that as an ERROR rather than a failure and prints it above the
#   summary, and the gate builds release, so nothing that anybody ran said
#   so. A TEST THAT ONLY RUNS IN ONE PROFILE IS A TEST YOU DO NOT HAVE IN
#   THE OTHER.
#
#   The gate is not blind to it: reintroducing the fault and running the
#   development build through `tests check` gives failures 2, one of them
#   the suite. What hid it was that the two commands are usually run against
#   different builds.
#
#   Written as the bits IEEE gives it there is nothing to overflow, and the
#   store is done by one routine where the check it would fail is suppressed
#   once with a reason beside it. Both profiles now run 318 of 318.
#
# load:  not a timing
# model: no model

# 2026-09-10, a buffer given back is not given up.
#
#   WHAT IT IS BOUND BY. With a batch gathered by expert, a mixture's
#   GENERATED token became the slow side. A budget sweep, Qwen3-30B-A3B,
#   110-token prompt, eight generated tokens, 4,096-token context:
#
#     device memory   prompt      generating   resident
#      4 GB           17.17 t/s    4.93 t/s    4.00 GB
#      6 GB           17.43        6.37        6.00
#      8 GB           17.90       11.13        7.47
#
#   GENERATION SCALES ALMOST LINEARLY WITH RESIDENT BYTES AND THE PROMPT
#   BARELY MOVES. That asymmetry is the gathering working: a prompt reads
#   each expert once for the whole batch so a miss is shared over the
#   positions that chose it, and a generated token touches 1,152 expert
#   matrices alone and pays every miss by itself.
#
#   AND A MISS WAS NOT ONLY AN UPLOAD. Taking a matrix was vkCreateBuffer,
#   vkAllocateMemory and vkBindBufferMemory; making room was
#   vkDestroyBuffer and vkFreeMemory. Four hundred of each a token.
#
#   A mixture's expert matrices are all one size, so the buffer given back
#   is the right shape for the one being taken. Kept by byte count, at most
#   sixty-four, counted against the budget because the device has not had
#   them back. Three alternated rounds:
#
#                         freeing              keeping
#     generating, 4 GB    5.40 5.45 5.16       6.76 6.80 6.71   1.27x
#     generating, 6 GB    6.29 6.25 6.26       8.42 8.52 8.46   1.35x
#     the prompt,  6 GB  17.53 17.29 17.59    18.27 18.32 18.03 1.04x
#
#   SO THE ALLOCATOR WAS A THIRD OF A GENERATED TOKEN on a model that does
#   not fit: 157 ms a token becomes 118. The uploads it was hiding behind
#   are the other part. The prompt gains almost nothing because it was not
#   missing much.
#
#   A MODEL THAT FITS PAYS NOTHING EITHER WAY. The list is only fed by
#   evictions and a model whose matrices are all resident never evicts:
#   twelve generated tokens of TinyLlama read 0.218, 0.220, 0.219 s against
#   0.2187 before, digest 5abff916f9d83ca6.
#
#   AND THE ANSWER. A reused buffer is a buffer something else has written
#   to, so a matrix uploaded into one and read short would read the last
#   matrix's weights -- the failure the shape checks beside the residency
#   key were added for, by another route. The test runs the fixture at
#   whatever the device offers and again at a sixteen-kilobyte budget and
#   holds the two equal TO THE BIT; taking a reused buffer as though it
#   already held the weights fails it by 7.13.
#
#   WHAT IS LEFT IS RESIDENCY. Eight gigabytes generates at 11.1 and four at
#   6.8, and the device offers 8.47 against the model's 11.26. That is a
#   model too large for the part rather than an arrangement to improve, and
#   the lever is a smaller quantization.
#
# load:  the mixture rows are ungated CLI runs, warm, three alternated
#        rounds; the TinyLlama rows went through the gate
# model: Qwen3-30B-A3B Q4_K 11.26 GB, and TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0

# 2026-09-10, what a token reads decides whether it fits.
#
#   THE REFUSAL AND ITS REASON. A model larger than the device's share was
#   refused while loading, because such a model runs by uploading what does
#   not fit as it is wanted and that is slower than the processor. Still
#   true, and true of one kind of model. TinyLlama-1.1B Q8_0, 1.17 GB of
#   weights, sixteen generated tokens:
#
#     the processor                39.41 t/s
#     the device, 900 MB budget     5.22
#     the device, 600 MB            5.27
#     the device, 300 MB            5.66
#
#   SEVEN AND A HALF TIMES SLOWER. A dense model reads every weight for
#   every token, so a budget holding a fraction uploads the rest every
#   token and no arrangement changes it.
#
#   A MIXTURE READS EIGHT EXPERTS OF A HUNDRED AND TWENTY-EIGHT. With the
#   residency work of the entries above, Qwen3-30B-A3B -- 11.26 GB against
#   the 8.47 this part offers, refused outright until now -- reads 18.04
#   tokens a second on a 110-token prompt and 11.49 generating, against the
#   processor's 3.80 and 2.89. FOUR TIMES THE SPEED was being refused on
#   reasoning that belongs to the other kind of model.
#
#   The test is now every matrix, less the experts, plus the share of the
#   experts a token chooses. For a dense model that is the number it always
#   was. The check holds both halves against half of their own weights, so
#   neither passes by being smaller than the other; judging the mixture on
#   its whole weights fails it, and so does counting every expert as read.
#
#   AND THE DENSE FIGURE IS UNMOVED: twelve generated tokens of TinyLlama on
#   the device read 0.219, 0.220, 0.219 s, digest 5abff916f9d83ca6.
#
# load:  the TinyLlama rows went through the gate; the mixture rows are
#        ungated CLI runs, warm
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0, and Qwen3-30B-A3B Q3_K 11.26 GB

# 2026-09-10, mapped once instead of four hundred times a token.
#
#   The upload was vkMapMemory, a copy, vkUnmapMemory -- once for every
#   matrix, about four hundred times a generated token on a model that does
#   not fit, and since the spare buffers it was the same memory objects
#   mapped again and again. The cache has been mapped once and held since it
#   was written; the weights were the one thing still asking every time.
#
#   Mapped when the memory is made and kept as long as it lives. Three
#   alternated rounds, Qwen3-30B-A3B, 110-token prompt, eight generated:
#
#                          mapping each time     mapped once
#     generating, 4 GB     6.77 6.70 6.68 t/s    8.15 8.13 8.18   1.21x
#     generating, 6 GB     8.46 8.54 8.56        8.88 9.40 9.31   1.08x
#     the prompt,  4 GB   19.27 19.05 18.91     20.85 20.43 20.71 1.08x
#     the prompt,  6 GB   18.40 18.36 18.15     19.18 18.88 19.12 1.04x
#
#   AND NOTHING AT THE BUDGET THIS PART OFFERS: at 8.47 GB the same model
#   reads 17.92 and 10.84 against 18.04 and 11.49, which is the spread. The
#   tighter the budget the more matrices miss, and a matrix that does not
#   miss is never mapped again.
#
#   THE ESTIMATE THAT LED HERE WAS TOO HIGH. It put the mapping at about 26
#   ms of an 87 ms token, reasoning that a map and an unmap cost about what
#   the half-megabyte copy between them costs. They cost much less: at six
#   gigabytes the whole change is worth 0.7 tokens a second, about 7 ms. THE
#   COPY DOMINATES AND THE DRIVER CALL AROUND IT DID NOT. The same reasoning
#   was right about vkAllocateMemory, which measured a third of the token,
#   and the difference is that allocating really is expensive and mapping
#   really is not.
#
#   Kept anyway: a clear gain where the budget binds, nothing where it does
#   not, and a driver call out of a loop that runs four hundred times a
#   token. The dense path is untouched at 0.218, 0.220, 0.219 s, digest
#   5abff916f9d83ca6.
#
# load:  the mixture rows are ungated CLI runs, warm, three alternated
#        rounds; the TinyLlama rows went through the gate
# model: Qwen3-30B-A3B Q3_K 11.26 GB, and TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0

# 2026-09-10, a probe instead of a walk, and a chain instead of a scan.
#
#   TWO LOOPS over the residency table ran once for every matrix taken and
#   once for every matrix given back: the lookup asked every entry whether
#   it was the one wanted, the eviction asked every entry whether it was the
#   oldest. A mixture does about eighteen thousand of each over a table
#   twelve thousand long.
#
#   WHAT FOUND THEM was that the prompt got faster as the budget shrank --
#   23.57 t/s at two gigabytes against 18.25 at eight, with more of the
#   model resident. Nothing about memory does that; a list walked from one
#   end does.
#
#   Three alternated rounds, Qwen3-30B-A3B, 110-token prompt, eight
#   generated tokens:
#
#                        walking                 indexed
#     generating, 8 GB   11.04 10.93 11.02 t/s   13.33 13.15 13.27  1.21x
#     generating, 6 GB    9.39  9.25  9.24       11.60 11.48 11.48  1.24x
#     the prompt,  8 GB  17.89 18.17 18.44       18.71 18.62 18.38  1.02x
#     the prompt,  6 GB  18.68 18.73 18.69       18.98 19.31 19.43  1.03x
#
#   THE GENERATION GAINED AND THE PROMPT DID NOT, which is not what the
#   measurement that led here predicted. The budget curve was a prompt
#   curve, and the prompt is STILL faster at a smaller budget -- so whatever
#   that curve is, it was not these loops, and it is unexplained. Third time
#   today a number reasoned out ahead of the work came back different: the
#   dispatch price, the mapping, and this.
#
#   TWO FAULTS ON THE WAY, both worth writing down.
#
#   The index returns one entry for a key and the walk it replaced kept
#   looking. Two matrices can share an address -- a tied output holds one
#   under two names -- so stopping at the first key meant missing and then
#   keeping a second entry beside the first. The index compares the whole
#   identity, as the walk did.
#
#   And putting a FRESHLY TAKEN slot at the front of the chain by unlinking
#   it first reads its links as zero, concludes it is both the newest and
#   the oldest, and sets both ends of the chain to nothing. Every other
#   entry is orphaned and the eviction walks a chain of one:
#
#     right    2,550 held, 6,886 given back, 7.56 t/s
#     broken   3,376 held, 1,570 given back, 3.27 t/s
#
#   The residency counters are what caught it -- fewer given back and more
#   held, at a third of the speed. Linking and touching are two operations
#   now, and the one that unlinks says the entry must already be in a chain.
#
#   THE DENSE PATH IS UNTOUCHED: 0.221, 0.217, 0.220 s over twelve generated
#   tokens, digest 5abff916f9d83ca6. A model that fits is never evicted and
#   its table is two hundred entries long.
#
# load:  the mixture rows are ungated CLI runs, warm, three alternated
#        rounds; the TinyLlama rows went through the gate
# model: Qwen3-30B-A3B Q3_K 11.26 GB, and TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0

# 2026-09-10, suballocation, built and refused.
#
#   THE ONE PIECE OF STANDARD VULKAN PRACTICE THIS ENGINE DOES NOT DO. Every
#   matrix on the device has memory of its own: one vkAllocateMemory to take
#   it and one vkMapMemory to write it. llama.cpp does not do that; it takes
#   memory in large blocks and cuts buffers out of them at offsets, and the
#   two measurements before this one -- the spare buffers worth 1.35x, the
#   persistent mapping worth 1.21x -- were both working around what blocks
#   would have removed outright.
#
#   Built: runs of memory of ONE SLOT SIZE, one allocation and one mapping
#   each, cut into slots and handed out a matrix at a time, with a buffer per
#   matrix bound into its slot at an offset. It needed no shader change -- a
#   buffer bound at an offset begins where the matrix does -- and it needed
#   the eviction to become per size class, because a slot given back is a
#   slot of one size. It is correct: Qwen3-30B-A3B answers BYTE-IDENTICALLY
#   at eight gigabytes, and the suite passes at 320.
#
#   AND IT COSTS HALF THE GENERATION RATE. Three alternated rounds,
#   Qwen3-30B-A3B, 110-token prompt, eight generated, 64 MB runs:
#
#                       memory per matrix    memory in runs
#     generating, 2 GB   8.09 8.13 8.08      5.16 5.18 5.12    0.64x
#     generating, 8 GB  12.99 13.40 13.30    7.90 7.19 6.83    0.55x
#     the prompt,  2 GB 23.09 16.46 17.77   24.74 24.88 24.73   1.28x
#     the prompt,  8 GB 18.50 18.63 18.51   11.53  4.07  4.04   0.36x
#
#   THE PROMPT GAINS AND GENERATION LOSES, and the loss is the larger and
#   the steadier of the two. A run of any size does it. Sweeping the run
#   size at eight gigabytes, generation reads 6.02 at four megabytes, 6.41 at
#   sixteen, 10.76 and then 7.9/7.2/6.8 at sixty-four, 6.39/6.44 at two
#   hundred and fifty-six, and 4.06/2.71 at a gigabyte -- where the run no
#   longer fits the host-visible heap and the whole thing collapses. There is
#   no size at which it comes back.
#
#   IT IS NOT THE BOOKKEEPING. The same build with the runs turned off by
#   raising the fewest-slots-worth-making threshold reads 12.95 and 12.75
#   generating, which is the figure without any of this. The residency is
#   also the same either way -- 12211 matrices, none given back -- so nothing
#   is thrashing and nothing is being uploaded twice. What changed is where
#   the memory is.
#
#   AND A MODEL THAT FITS DOES NOT CARE: TinyLlama-1.1B Q8_0, which holds
#   1.1 GB against the 8.47 this part offers, reads 94.76 and 53.49 with runs
#   against 94.50 and 53.97 without. The loss appears only when the resident
#   set is most of the part.
#
#   WHICH IS THE READING: the driver places memory at the granularity it was
#   allocated at. Twelve thousand small allocations let it keep the ones a
#   token actually reads where they are fast; a hundred and twenty-eight
#   runs of sixty-four megabytes make that all-or-nothing per run, and a
#   generated token -- which reads a few matrices per layer out of a resident
#   set the size of the part -- pays for it. A prompt reads everything and
#   has nothing to place, which is why it gains instead.
#
#   Refused. Reverted to memory per matrix. llama.cpp suballocates because
#   its allocator serves a graph of tensors with known lifetimes; this one
#   serves an LRU cache of weights against a byte budget, and the two want
#   opposite granularities. THE SPARE BUFFERS AND THE PERSISTENT MAPPING ARE
#   NOT WORKAROUNDS FOR A MISSING SUBALLOCATOR -- they are what taking memory
#   per matrix costs, paid down, and paying it is worth what it buys.
#
# load:  the mixture rows are ungated CLI runs, warm, three alternated
#        rounds; the sweep and the no-runs probe are single ungated runs
# model: Qwen3-30B-A3B Q3_K 11.26 GB, and TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0

# 2026-09-10, a mixture layer taken whole on the device, and the two-bit
# decode factored.
#
#   WHAT WAS BUILT. A mixture's expert matrices reach the device as the
#   three stacks the file stores -- kept once, where the whole model fits
#   the budget (Model.Stacked) -- and a token's chosen experts are read out
#   of them in one dispatch a kind: the third dispatch dimension is the
#   member, and the slice it reads is in the push block or, routed on the
#   device, in what a routing step wrote. Three new steps in the products
#   engine: the gathered product, the routing (route.comp: softmax, the
#   largest Used, the shares over their sum) and the mix (mix.comp: the sum
#   by shares in rank order, precise, with the residual folded in), and
#   Groups on the normalization for a head at a time. Whole_Layer takes
#   query and key normalizations and a mixture in place of the gated
#   block, so a Qwen3-30B-A3B token is forty-eight chained submissions with
#   nothing on the host between the router and the experts, and a Qwen3
#   dense token takes the whole layer for the first time. A prompt reads
#   its experts as one sequence each out of the same stacks, routed on the
#   device through the same kernel, so a batch and a token choose alike;
#   a batch of one and a batch of six agree to the bit and the whole layer
#   is held within 1e-4 of them. On the processor the batched mixture is
#   gathered by expert as the device's was.
#
#   THE FIRST RUN ANSWERED GARBAGE AND THEN NAN: a gather routed on the
#   device reads the routing step's choice through no field the fence
#   logic knew, so nothing published it. This.Routed is in the barrier's
#   source set now, and the test that holds a token against a batch is
#   what would have caught it had the fixture been routed the same way.
#
#   MEASURED, alternated, warm, --context-size 4096, the device:
#
#     Qwen3-30B-A3B Q2_K, 1302-token prompt, 32 generated, 11.5 GB budget
#       a slice at a time (8.47 GB)     13.59 14.07 14.43 t/s generating
#       the whole layer                 18.24 17.90 18.07
#     Qwen3-8B Q4_K_M, 110-token prompt, 32 generated
#       HEAD                            10.42 10.49
#       the whole layer                 11.13 11.47 11.53
#     TinyLlama Q8_0, 110-token prompt  HEAD 51.02 51.84 51.44 / 366-369
#                                       now  51.38 51.54 51.71 / 362-370
#
#   llama.cpp reads 34.4 generating on the mixture, so the gap went 2.4 to
#   1.9. WHERE THE REST IS: the host is not it -- sixty samples of the
#   main thread during generation, forty-five in Await on the fence, none
#   recording. The experts are not gather-bound: the whole layer with one
#   expert instead of eight reads 27.7 against 18.1, so seven experts'
#   worth is 523 MB a token in 19 ms, 27 GB/s, which is what a plain Q2_K
#   product streams at inside a token on this part (TinyLlama Q2_K, 420 MB
#   in 14.8 ms) against Q4_K's 59. At one vector on its own the Q2_K kernel
#   streams at 57 GB/s once the 64 us call is taken off, so the two-bit
#   format loses in the token and not in the kernel: a third the bytes a
#   matrix, so each dispatch is shorter and its fixed part a larger share.
#   Two more barriers a step -- nineteen hundred a token -- cost 0.9 ms.
#
#   THE Q2_K DECODE, FACTORED. row_product.comp's two-bit branch now sums
#   factor * (sum of q * x) - lowest * (sum of x) a sub-block instead of
#   factor * q - lowest a weight, takes four quants out of a word with
#   unpack8, reads the two factors as one word unpacked as a half pair and
#   the lane's four scale bytes as two words instead of four byte reads --
#   five loads a block where there were ten. TinyLlama Q2_K on the device
#   67.74 -> 72.55 72.14 (the loads alone moved nothing: 69.29 72.87). The
#   mixture 18.1 -> 18.4, inside the spread. The association differs, so
#   the last bits differ; conformance is at zero outside tolerance.
#
#   THE ROW KERNEL'S PUSH BLOCK grew from thirty-two bytes to a hundred
#   and eight for the members, and every compilation carries it; the
#   TinyLlama rows above say what that costs, which is nothing.
#
#   SEVEN GROUPS RE-MEASURED, this sitting, TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat, medians of
#   three each, the load under 1.0 for the first eight rows and 2.3 to 3.9
#   for the rest, which is the one-minute average the runs themselves
#   raise:
#
#     engine 6-tok 12gen cpu     0.345 s -- 0.046 + 0.299, 1.85 s processor
#                                digest 33f48397f89839f6, unchanged
#     device 6-tok 12gen         0.249 s -- 0.025 + 0.223, 0.14 s processor
#                                digest 5abff916f9d83ca6, unchanged
#     reference 4tok cpu         0.042 + 0.099
#     reference 4tok reference   5.510 + 3.700
#     outside q8 110 cpu         0.333 prompt, 1.620 generating
#     outside q8 1419 cpu        4.434 prompt, 1.774 generating
#     outside q8 110 device      0.068 prompt, 1.209 generating
#     outside q8 1419 device     0.724 prompt, 1.343 generating
#     panels q4_0 110 rows       0.167 prompt, 0.257 for seventeen
#     panels q4km 110 rows       0.215 prompt, 1.173 for sixty-four
#     panels q5km 110 rows       0.228 prompt, 1.318
#     panels q2k 110 rows        0.250 prompt, 0.891
#     drafting q2 12tok          1.311 s; drafted 2.937 s, 24 proposed 7 taken
#
#   llama-bench at 95b8e33e1 in the same sitting, through tests outside:
#
#     q8_0 cpu     pp110 385.95  pp1419 309.84  tg64 40.34
#     q8_0 device  pp110 1651.69 pp1419 1885.22 tg64 57.17
#
#   Every row is inside the spread of the sitting before it, and both
#   digests are the digests. Restamped once more after the sitting for one
#   blank line taken out of model_runner-llama.ads, which the style check
#   had been refusing and which moves nothing.
#   The quality group names model_runner-llama.adb
#   and was NOT re-measured: its table is the processor on a dense model,
#   and the only processor path this changed is a mixture's batched
#   prompt, which no row of that table runs.
#
# load:  the gate's own instrument under 1.0 before the first eight rows;
#        the one-minute average ran 2.3 to 3.9 through the rest, raised by
#        the runs themselves
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0, Q4_0, Q2_K, Q4_K_M, Q5_K_M; Qwen3-8B
#        Q4_K_M; Qwen3-30B-A3B Q2_K

# 2026-09-11, a session bounded by half the host's memory, and seven
# groups restamped and none re-measured.
#
#   A model's declared context is a training fact and not a sizing one:
#   Qwen3-30B-A3B declares 40,960 tokens, which is 8.5 GB of cache, held
#   on the host and again on a device, on a machine with 30 GB -- and the
#   file's own pages and the weights on the device beside it. Twice on
#   2026-09-10 that was the desktop killed for want of memory before a
#   token was generated, both times by a run that named no --context-size.
#
#   Where the caller names no --memory-limit, a session is now bounded by
#   half of what the host has -- MemTotal out of /proc/meminfo on Linux,
#   hw.memsize on macOS, nothing where the host cannot be asked -- and the
#   device's own copy of the cache is counted with the host's, because on
#   an integrated part both come out of the same memory:
#
#     model_runner run qwen3moe.gguf --backend device
#     MR-MEM-0001: kv_cache needs 16510291801 bytes, above the limit of
#                  16407179264
#
#   The same model on the processor at the same context is 8.5 GB of 16
#   and runs, as it did. Every published figure names --context-size or a
#   model whose context is small, so none moves; the groups naming
#   model_runner-llama.adb are restamped for the check moved into a block.
#
# load:  no figure taken
# model: Qwen3-30B-A3B Q2_K, refused; nothing else run

# 2026-09-11, the heads of a layer made ready in one step, the dispatch
# thesis refuted, and the stacks held at load.
#
#   THE THESIS UNDER TEST, from the entry before: the two-bit format loses
#   in the token and not in the kernel because its dispatches are short and
#   the fixed part of a dispatch a larger share, so fewer, larger dispatches
#   a layer is the lever. BUILT: heads.comp, one dispatch over the query
#   heads that normalizes each over its own mean square where the
#   architecture states it and turns it, and one over the key heads that
#   does the same and places the keys and the values in the cache in both
#   precisions -- six dispatches a layer as two on Qwen3, four as two on a
#   model without head normalizations. The arithmetic is the arithmetic of
#   norm.comp, rotate.comp and place.comp in their order and precision, and
#   a test holds the fused step to the three it stands for to the bit; the
#   device digests are unchanged, 5abff916f9d83ca6 and 7ec6b755e53e16b4.
#
#   WORTH HALF A PER CENT. Two binaries alternated three times, Qwen3-30B-
#   A3B, 1302-token prompt, 48 generated:
#
#     the heads step   18.59 18.61 18.67 t/s
#     the six steps    18.54 18.58 18.50
#
#   A hundred and ninety-two dispatches fewer a token bought 0.3 ms, which
#   is about 1.5 microseconds a dispatch and not the 12.5 the entry before
#   priced them at. That figure was a normalization APPENDED to a layer --
#   its own reads and writes and a barrier behind a product -- and it was
#   read as the price of any dispatch. REFUTED: a small dispatch between
#   small dispatches costs almost nothing, and the twelve milliseconds the
#   entry before charged to nine hundred and sixty of them are elsewhere.
#   Kept anyway: the same bits, fewer steps to record, and a Qwen3 dense
#   token reads 11.48 11.51 where it read 11.13-11.53.
#
#   WHERE THE REST IS, corrected. The 18 t/s figures above are at a
#   1302-token context, and llama-bench's 34.4 is tg64 from an empty one.
#   Measured at a six-token prompt the mixture generates at 22.08 22.25
#   t/s, so the gap is 1.55 and not 1.9, and what the 1302 positions cost
#   is attention over them, forty-eight layers a token.
#
#   AND THE STACKS HELD AT LOAD. A mixture's experts were uploaded as
